This is another central idea within a much broader pattern concerning violations of kinship, the breaking of family bonds, and the forms that such violations can take within social and domestic relationships.

The innocent son—whether Hippolytus, Joseph, or Imruʾ al-Qays—refuses the love offered to him and thereby refuses to become his father’s rival for the same women. In Arab terminology, he refuses to become a dayzan. The word was also used of someone who pushes past or crowds others at a well.

Sumerian and later Babylonian literature, like the literature of many ancient cultures, also knew a type of story that might be called “falling in love with the guest.” The wife of a master, prince, or king falls in love with a guest in her household, and the result becomes a tragedy ending in the guest’s death or imprisonment.

Taken together, these myths and stories contain a remarkable variety of symbols and meanings. The Arabs clearly reshaped this material into myths of their own, and the Greek element within them is often unmistakable.

The history of these heroes, whatever names they may bear, is therefore mythical history. It is presented through forms transparent enough to make it look like genuine history, while at the same time offering almost unlimited possibilities for reading it through the language of myth.

Before bringing this introduction to a close, I want to stress one point of great importance, so that there can be no misunderstanding of the central idea I hope to establish in this book.

The presence of a Greek mythical element within ancient Arab myths and historical narratives does not mean that those Arab myths and narratives are Greek in origin. An impatient or inexperienced reader might easily draw that conclusion from some of the examples and arguments presented here, but that is not what I am saying.

The Greek element entered Arab mythical and historical discourse as a component that could easily be detached from one setting and merged into another. Something comparable happened in the opposite direction, when ancient Arab and Phoenician elements entered Greek myths, stories, and even religious beliefs, as Herodotus believed. See, for example, Bernal’s Black Athena.

These similarities may also fit the explanation proposed by James Frazer (1854–1941), who argued that human societies share what might be called certain psychological universals. Human impulses, desires, passions, ambitions, and dreams resemble one another across societies. That resemblance appears not only in similar customs, but also in recurring features of culture, patterns of thought, and imagination.

If some readers nevertheless wish to conclude, contrary to the author’s intention, that Arab myths and historical narratives must therefore have originated in Greece, they should also be prepared to accept the opposite conclusion once proposed by Herodotus:

His investigations led him to the conclusion that Greek myths and gods came from Egypt, and across the sea from the Arabian Gulf.

This book is certainly not intended to lead its readers toward such sweeping conclusions.

Its real purpose, as I understand it, is to help readers of Arab history develop a new and radical method: to separate the mythical from the historical within historical and religious texts, and then, where necessary, to examine each on its own terms.

Fadel al-Rubaie London, 26 October 2000

PreviousContentsNext1. The Legend of al-Nadira’s Marriage and the Siege of the City-State of Hatra

1. The Legend of al-Nadira’s Marriage and the Siege of the City-State of Hatra

Between Yaqut al-Hamawi, al-Tabari, and Nöldeke

Al-Tabari (224–310 AH) tells the story of al-Nadira, daughter of the king of Hatra, the small Arab state near Mosul in northern Iraq. According to the legend, she married Shapur son of Ardashir, king of Persia (241–272 CE), after he had kept Hatra under siege for four continuous years.

Al-Tabari places this story among his historical accounts of the Sasanian period in Persia. It appears in the context of a successful Persian attack on Roman garrisons in Syria, followed by an advance toward Hatra in Iraq. One result of this Persian campaign was the capture of the Roman emperor Valerian.

At the time, Hatra maintained close relations with Rome, Persia’s traditional political and religious rival. Its alliance with Rome naturally increased the threat Hatra seemed to pose within Persia’s own sphere of influence. The Persians therefore decided to put an end to this rebellious little state.

Like al-Tabari, Yaqut al-Hamawi, in his Dictionary of Countries, tells the marriage story as part of the historical account of the Persian campaign. That presentation gives the impression that the extraordinary marriage was a genuine part of the historical event.

But what evidence do we have that such a marriage ever took place? And what exactly is the meaning of a marriage occurring in the middle of a siege?

When we examine this remarkable story—a marriage taking place on the battlefield and bringing the war and its tragedy to an end—we find symbolic meanings that may be more significant than the supposed historical material surrounding it.

Al-Tabari and Yaqut tell us:

The king of Hatra was called al-Dayzan ibn Muʿawiya and belonged to the Arab tribe of Qudaʿa, which was of Yemeni origin. His city had been built and protected by magic, so that it could neither be conquered nor destroyed except through the blood of a grey ring-necked dove mixed with the menstrual blood of a blue-eyed woman. Al-Dayzan, father of al-Nadira, ruled from the city for some time and used it as a base for raids into Persian territory.

Besides al-Tabari and Yaqut, a considerable number of other writers preserve good versions of the legend, including al-Masʿudi in The Meadows of Gold, al-Abshihi in al-Mustatraf, and the author of Taj al-ʿArus.

Drawing on these accounts, the story can be summarized as follows:

Al-Dayzan ibn Muʿawiya, of Qudaʿa, was king of the region between the Tigris and Euphrates opposite Tikrit. He possessed a great palace known as al-Jawsaq, and his kingdom extended as far as Syria. He attacked the city of Shapur Dhu al-Aktaf, captured it, and took Shapur’s sister prisoner.

Shapur then besieged the walls of Hatra for four years without being able to break through.

Al-Nadira, daughter of al-Dayzan, began menstruating and was therefore sent outside the fortress, as was supposedly the custom with menstruating women. She was one of the most beautiful women of her age, while Shapur was likewise one of the handsomest men of his time.

They saw one another and fell in love.

Al-Nadira sent him a message:

“What will you give me if I show you how to destroy this city and kill my father?”

He replied:

“I will let you name your reward.”

In other words, according to the explanation given in the tradition, he would make her queen. Al-Tabari adds that he promised to place her above his other wives.

She told him:

“Take a grey ring-necked dove and write upon it with the menstrual blood of a young woman. Then release it. When it lands on the city wall, the whole city will collapse.”

This was the magic spell protecting Hatra.

Shapur did as she instructed. The walls collapsed. He stormed the city by force, killed al-Dayzan, destroyed Hatra, took al-Nadira away with him, and married her in the Iraqi town of Ayn al-Tamr.

Yaqut, drawing on al-Tabari and others, continues the story:

But the treacherous girl could not sleep that night.

She said to Shapur:

“I have never slept on a bed rougher than yours.”

Shapur replied:

“Woe to you! Do kings sleep on anything softer than this?”

He examined the bed and discovered a single myrtle leaf. It had become stuck to her body between two folds of flesh.

Shapur asked:

“What did your father feed you?”

She answered:

“Honey from virgin bees.”

The Sasanian king became angry and said:

“If you could betray your father after he treated you with such care, how could I ever trust you as my wife?”

He then ordered her killed and her body cut into pieces.

The hero must not know the secret. He must not solve the riddle or reveal the mystery of the enchanted city. When the hero supplies the solution, his destruction is already contained within that act.

As Lévi-Strauss observed, the hero must not solve the riddle because, by its nature, the mythical riddle has no solution. To violate this principle is to invite destruction. A mythical riddle is a question without an answer—or at least a question whose answer must never be spoken.

This is what happens to Oedipus in Greek mythology.

According to Lévi-Strauss’s reading, solving the riddle is itself a violation of the natural order. The riddle belongs to a class of questions for which no answer should be given. Oedipus violates that rule by answering it, and punishment follows.

Lévi-Strauss also notes that the natural order does not permit a mother to marry her son. Such a union breaks the boundaries of human kinship. Yet Oedipus violates this order as well: first by solving the riddle, and then by marrying his mother after killing his father.

Al-Nadira plays a similar role in the Arab legend. She violates the natural order when she offers Shapur the secret of the city in exchange for love. That violation ultimately ends in suffering and death.

Notice that the solution to the riddle depends on one fundamental act: the shedding of blood.

Al-Nadira herself eventually pays that price when Shapur realizes the enormity of her betrayal of her father.

At the symbolic level, the menstruating woman is the woman who offers her blood as the price of marriage. She sheds it so that the marriage can be completed, just as blood traditionally carries symbolic meaning on the wedding night.

But what historical meaning can we draw from a story that al-Tabari presents as an organic part of his account of Sasanian rule in Persia and Iraq?

Was there really a king named al-Dayzan whose daughter was called al-Nadira? Did the handsome Persian king Shapur fall in love with her after a four-year siege? Did she then betray her father?

Almost all early Arab and Muslim chroniclers assumed that the rulers of the Arab state of Hatra belonged to the tribe of Qudaʿa. Ibn Khaldun alone gives a different account, writing in his history that “the kingship of Hatra belonged to the descendants of ʿUbayd ibn al-Abras,” although he gives no explanation for this claim.

The great German Orientalist Theodor Nöldeke (1836–1930), who personally worked on al-Tabari’s history and produced a rare and remarkable translation of its section on the Sasanians, rejected al-Tabari’s account almost completely.

On the basis of a series of historical arguments, Nöldeke denied that Shapur Dhu al-Aktaf ever besieged Hatra or that the city fell to him. In his view, Hatra had already fallen to Shapur’s father, Ardashir, in 241 CE.

This conclusion gives considerable support to those Arab accounts that attributed the siege to Shapur al-Junud, or “Shahpur of the Army,” rather than to Shapur Dhu al-Aktaf. According to the explanation preserved in these traditions, Shapur al-Junud is an Arabic rendering of the Persian expression Asbahand, meaning “the army.”

This Shapur was most likely a Persian military commander who led the expedition sent to punish Hatra and its king for daring to challenge Persian influence and maintain relations with Rome.

The poet al-Aʿsha, known as the “Minstrel of the Arabs,” refers to the siege in a famous poem, attributing it to Shapur al-Junud. In other words, he places the campaign in the reign of Ardashir rather than that of his son Shapur Dhu al-Aktaf:

Have you not seen Hatra, when its people lived in ease? Yet what blessing can last forever? Shahpur of the Army stayed against it for two full years, while the feet of war beat against it.

Al-Aʿsha’s poem suggests that the siege lasted two years, rather than the four years claimed by al-Tabari. This may help reduce some of the confusion found in the early Arab historical traditions, where history and myth were repeatedly merged.

Nöldeke’s reconstruction of the siege, together with al-Aʿsha’s poem, clearly undermines the mythical foundation on which the extraordinary marriage story was built.

It does not, however, rule out the possibility that some form of marriage alliance took place at some point in the region of Ayn al-Tamr, perhaps involving a Persian military commander—possibly a man called Shahpur who commanded a Persian unit.

The mythical basis of the marriage that supposedly brought down the walls of the city must be sought elsewhere.

In 1930 and 1931, a German archaeological expedition carried out important excavations in the regions of Hatra and Nineveh, or Assur. These excavations revealed not only the city’s ancient Aramaic—or Arab—character and its many inscriptions, but, more importantly for our argument, its Arab-Greek character.

This strengthens the case that Hatra’s relationship with Rome went well beyond ordinary political contact. The mixture of Arab, Greek, and Roman elements created a new cultural foundation, and some of its echoes may survive in the legend of al-Nadira’s marriage.

Three Greek myths appear to have supplied the Arab storyteller with the basic material.

  1. Theseus, Ariadne, and the Minotaur. Ariadne was the daughter of King Minos, but she fell in love with Theseus, who was both her enemy and her father’s enemy. After a series of adventures, Ariadne gave her lover the thread that would allow him to find his way out of the labyrinth. Yet Theseus soon abandoned her and treated her with contempt.

  2. Nisus, king of Megara. The king’s daughter gave her lover—again, an enemy of both herself and her father—the magical lock of hair that protected Nisus from death. Scylla, daughter of Nisus, cut off the lock and gave it to her lover as proof of her love. This enabled him to kill her father. Yet he, too, soon rejected and abandoned her.8

  3. Comaetho, daughter of Pterelaus. Comaetho, daughter of the king of the island of Taphos, fell in love with Amphitryon when he attacked the island. Her father could not die so long as a golden hair remained on his head. Comaetho therefore cut off the hair and gave it to her lover, allowing him to defeat her father. After his victory, however, Amphitryon showed her no gratitude. He killed her.

These Greek stories form, in my view, the mythical foundation from which the Arab narrators constructed the legend of al-Nadira.

I would even suggest that the name al-Nadira itself was formed from the Greek name Nisus, the name of the murdered king of Megara.

One final point should be made about the function of this myth.

By telling the story, the Arab tribes were also expressing their own attitude toward marriages between Persian occupiers and Bedouin Arab women. Persia repeatedly sought such marriages as a political means of securing tribal loyalty and used every possible method to encourage them.

One of the greatest kings of al-Hira, al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir, ultimately paid with his life for refusing such demands. The Persians imprisoned and killed him in a famous episode.

The Arab tribes were determined to deny Persia the chance to drink from the desert spring. Marriages of this kind could tie the hands of tribes that hoped to establish kingdoms and sheikhdoms independent of Persian control.

Within this political setting, the daughter’s betrayal of her royal father becomes especially meaningful. It expresses more than the breaking of family bonds. Even more clearly, it expresses the fate awaiting any woman who falls in love with the enemy.

PreviousIntroductionNext2. The Legend of al-Khawarnaq Palace and Sinnimar’s Reward

2. The Legend of al-Khawarnaq Palace and Sinnimar’s Reward

Echoes of Greek Myth in the Accounts of al-Tabari and Ibn al-Athir

Why must the keeper of a secret be killed?

Why must he be thrown from the top of a mountain or a palace?

Several Arab myths revolve around a simple idea: if a person wishes to escape death, torture, or exile, he must never become the sole keeper of a dangerous secret.

The extraordinary story of al-Khawarnaq, the legendary palace at al-Hira around 399 CE, belongs to this pattern.

The palace was celebrated again and again by pre-Islamic poets, while storytellers and desert wanderers passed down the tale of a building whose foundations were, in effect, consecrated with blood.

At the heart of the story lies the price paid for possessing a secret.

The Roman—or Greek—architect Sinimmar, a figure who has no place in verifiable history and whom we may confidently regard as fictional, loses his life because he knows the secret of al-Khawarnaq, the palace he is said to have built for al-Nuʿman ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Mundhir.

The magical and wholly unbelievable secret is this: if one particular stone were pushed, shifted, or moved even slightly with a finger, the entire building would collapse.

Accounts preserved by al-Tabari in his History of the Kings, Ibn al-Athir in The Complete History, Yaqut in his Dictionary of Countries, Ibn Habib in al-Muhabbar, al-Isfahani in al-Aghani, and others tell the story in roughly the following form:

Al-Khawarnaq was a palace outside al-Hira. The sources disagreed about who had built it.

Al-Haytham ibn ʿAdi said that the man who ordered the construction of al-Khawarnaq was al-Nuʿman ibn Imruʾ al-Qays. He ruled for eighty years, while the building of al-Khawarnaq took sixty.

A Roman man called Sinimmar built it for him. He would work for two or three years, then disappear for five, and continued in this way for sixty years until the palace was complete.

Al-Nuʿman climbed to the top and looked out. Before him he saw the water, and behind him the land. He saw fish, lizards, gazelles, and palm trees.

He said:

“I have never seen a building like this.”

Sinimmar replied:

“I know the position of a single brick. If it were removed, the entire palace would fall.”

Al-Nuʿman asked:

“Does anyone else know which one it is?”

Sinimmar said:

“No.”

Al-Nuʿman replied:

“Then I will make sure no one else ever learns.”

He ordered Sinimmar to be thrown from the top of the palace to the ground below. His body was torn apart, and the Arabs thereafter made his fate into a proverb.

A poet said:

He rewarded me—may God repay him with the worst reward— with the reward of Sinimmar, though Sinimmar had done no wrong.

Al-Nuʿman had repeatedly campaigned in Syria. One day, while sitting in his palace at al-Khawarnaq, he looked toward Najaf and the gardens, palm groves, orchards, and rivers stretching westward, and toward the Euphrates in the east. A branch of the river curved around al-Khawarnaq like a moat.

Looking at the greenery, the light, and the rivers, he said to his minister:

“Have you ever seen a view as beautiful as this?”

The minister replied:

“By God, my king, I have never seen anything like it—if only it could last.”

Al-Nuʿman asked:

“What does last?”

The minister replied:

“What is with God in the life to come.”

The king asked:

“And how does a man attain that?”

The minister answered:

“By giving up this world, worshipping God, and seeking what is with Him.”

After this, the story says, al-Nuʿman ibn Imruʾ al-Qays abandoned his kingdom of al-Hira,9 wandered away, and was never seen again.

Some chroniclers explain his title al-Saʾih, usually understood as “the Wanderer,” by saying that he acquired it because “he put on coarse woollen clothing, left in disguise, and fled without anyone knowing, and no one has discovered what became of him to this day.”

This is a superficial interpretation repeated by the chroniclers. As we shall see, the title al-Saʾih, which al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir bore before his disappearance around 420 CE, is deeply connected with his conversion to Christianity.

The version attributed to Ibn al-Kalbi, on which the later Muslim chroniclers largely depended, places both the construction of al-Khawarnaq and the disappearance of al-Nuʿman III in a setting that is less mythical and more historical.

According to this account:

Yazdegerd, son of Shapur Dhu al-Aktaf and ambitious king of Persia from 399 to 420 CE, adopted a new policy aimed at securing the loyalty and goodwill of the Arab tribes, perhaps also hoping to strengthen himself against internal challenges facing the Sasanian state.

He therefore decided to send his eldest son, Prince Bahram Gur, to al-Hira in Iraq, where the boy would be raised, learn horsemanship and Arabic, and receive medical treatment.

Bahram Gur arrived at al-Hira as a young boy suffering from dropsy.

King Yazdegerd asked for “a healthy place free from sickness and disease, so that Bahram could be sent there out of fear for his illness.”

His physicians advised him to send the boy away from Persia and into the land of the Arabs, where he would drink camel urine and camel milk.

Yazdegerd therefore sent him to al-Nuʿman and ordered the latter to build him a palace in the form of al-Khawarnaq. Al-Nuʿman built it, housed the prince there, and treated him until he recovered.

Bahram then asked his father for permission to remain with al-Nuʿman. Permission was granted, and he continued to live at al-Khawarnaq until he reached adulthood and his father Yazdegerd died.

Other versions also stress that Yazdegerd “could not keep a son alive,” and that Bahram Gur suffered in childhood from an illness resembling dropsy.

What can be established historically, according to these accounts, is that al-Nuʿman III played the decisive role in placing Bahram Gur on the Persian throne by force.

Internal struggles had intensified in Persia, and a faction at court wanted to prevent the family of Yazdegerd—known as “the Sinner”—from continuing to rule. Al-Nuʿman mobilized the Arab tribes behind him, and his intervention proved decisive in bringing the young prince to the throne.

Bahram Gur could hardly have forgotten such a service.

We are therefore faced with several layers of material.

There is a myth about the building of an extraordinary palace, whose architect is thrown from its roof and killed because he possesses the palace’s secret.

There is a historical account of a sick Persian prince living in a building said to be al-Khawarnaq.

And between the myth and the historical report lies another story, in which the king abandons his throne, rejects the world, and disappears into a life devoted to the worship of God.

This is precisely what makes these narratives so difficult.

Where does myth stop carrying traces of history?

And where does history cease to be historical and become mythical or imaginary?

The disappearance of al-Nuʿman III, known both as al-Aʿwar, “the One-Eyed,” and al-Saʾih, together with his conversion from paganism to Christianity, eventually produced a series of contradictions in the historical narratives that are very difficult to reconcile.

What is especially striking is that none of the early chroniclers appears to have seriously investigated the report that al-Nuʿman ibn Imruʾ al-Qays adopted Nestorian Christianity.

The myth, however, supplied its own picture of the conversion through the dialogue between al-Nuʿman and his minister.

Their conversation turns on the emptiness of greed for a passing world, the call to renounce worldly life, and the need to devote oneself to God.

The “minister” of the myth corresponds to the bishop of al-Hira in the historical account.

Taking apart the legend of the Punishment of Sinimmar, or the Building of al-Khawarnaq, may therefore help us understand the historical report of al-Nuʿman’s conversion to Christianity and his later withdrawal from public life.

It may also give us a clearer picture of the development of Persian-Arab relations, as well as the Persian-Roman struggle over al-Hira, the most important Arab kingdom of its age, during the fourth and fifth centuries CE.

Neither al-Tabari nor Ibn al-Athir tells us why a Roman—or Greek—architect should have been present in al-Hira to build a miraculous palace for a sick Persian prince hurriedly sent into the Arab desert to recover and be educated. Yaqut, naturally enough, offers no explanation either.

Even if, purely for the sake of argument, we accept that a Roman architect called Sinimmar really was present at al-Hira, it is difficult to imagine his murder taking place during the reign of Yazdegerd. His period was marked by peace with Rome and by an end to the persecution of Christians within Persia.

It is equally difficult to imagine such a killing being ordered by a man whom the legend itself presents as being on the verge of conversion to Christianity—the religion of the murdered architect.

A sounder way to approach the historical material is to separate it completely from the legend of al-Khawarnaq’s construction. In other words, the entire story of the Punishment of Sinimmar must be detached from the historical account and treated as a myth of Greek origin that was later inserted into a narrative about the king of al-Hira.

There was, in fact, no Roman architect in al-Hira during the reign of Yazdegerd, nor did Yazdegerd ask al-Nuʿman to construct a palace for his sick son Bahram Gur.

The building, in my view, had originally been a rest house prepared to receive the young and sick Persian prince.

There are many indications that al-Khawarnaq was originally a private retreat belonging to al-Nuʿman III.

The Persian word khurankah means a drinking hall or drinking place, and the Arabs derived from it the word Khawarnaq, which came to be used for this retreat.

Al-Asmaʿi relates:

I asked al-Khalil ibn Ahmad al-Farahidi about the word Khawarnaq. He said: “It must be derived from khirnaq, meaning a young rabbit.”

Al-Khalil’s answer did not satisfy al-Asmaʿi, who added:

Al-Khalil has achieved nothing here. The word comes from khurankah.

The pronunciation described by al-Asmaʿi was itself slightly confused: the final Persian consonant is pronounced as a k, not the Arabic q he imagined.

Yet this drinking hall appears to have undergone a striking change of function when al-Nuʿman underwent his religious transformation.

A verse attributed to Khidash ibn Zuhayr al-Amiri says:

I reminded him, by God, of what lay between us, and of the bond between us, if only he would remember; by the White Marwa, then by Tubala, and by al-Nuʿman’s majlisa, where he became a Christian.

The word majlisa, used here by the poet, commonly appears in descriptions of al-Khawarnaq.

What is remarkable is that it can refer, in one context, to a place devoted to eating, drinking, and pleasure, while in another it refers to a place where a profound change occurred in the king’s life—a sacred place by which people could swear an oath, like the “White Marwa,” identified here with al-Lat.

This brings us back to a crucial question.

Was al-Khawarnaq, in its earliest form, actually a religious place? And did the word majlisa refer to a hermitage or cell, rather than simply to a hall for drinking and eating, as the Arab chroniclers and philologists assumed?

That possibility leads us once again to the significance of the Roman architect in the legend—the man who is thrown from the top of the palace and killed.

There can be little doubt, in my view, that the Greek story known as the myth of Trophonius is the real source from which the Arab legend developed.

My investigation of the mythical material surrounding the construction of al-Khawarnaq points to an ancient Greek story of the following form:

Trophonius was a famous architect, the son of the god Apollo by his wife Epicaste.

Together with Agamedes, Trophonius undertook the construction of the temple of Apollo at Delphi.

When the temple was complete, however, the latter killed the architect Trophonius by throwing him from the top of the temple.

The presence of a Roman architect in al-Hira, within al-Nuʿman’s private majlisa—perhaps a place of solitude and retreat that later came to be described simply as a hall for food and drink—can therefore be understood through the transparent screen provided by the murder story.

Both the original Greek legend and the Arab version contain the same image: a Greek architect is thrown from the top of a palace or temple because he possesses a secret.

The religious dimension becomes even clearer when we notice that, in both stories, the event is connected with worship and religious ritual.

At Delphi, Trophonius dies after completing the temple.

At al-Hira, the Roman Sinimmar dies after completing the majlisa, the place where, as we have seen, al-Nuʿman is said to have become a Christian.

Al-Nuʿman III maintained close and friendly relations with the bishop of al-Hira, a man named Shimʿun. He was an Arab from the tribe of Banu al-Harith ibn Kaʿb, descended from Aws ibn Qallam, one of whose ancestors had once helped establish the kingdom of al-Hira.

Their relationship was close enough to make it reasonable to suppose that Shimʿun guided al-Nuʿman toward Christianity, specifically its Nestorian form. Persia tolerated Nestorian Christianity, though reluctantly, while Rome viewed it with a degree of hostility.

Some Syriac sources and church records also suggest that the sick Persian prince Bahram Gur did more than receive medical treatment in al-Hira: they claim that he also received instruction in Christianity there.

This may be an exaggeration. We have no firm evidence that Bahram Gur actually converted.

Had an Arab kingdom converted a Persian prince to Christianity and then later imposed him upon Persia as king by military force, the consequences for Roman-Persian relations could have been enormous. No such historical transformation occurred.

A more restrained explanation is therefore possible.

Bahram Gur, like his Arab royal host, may have received instruction in Christianity from the bishop of al-Hira, but unlike al-Nuʿman he never publicly declared himself a Christian.

Al-Nuʿman, by contrast, appears to have announced his conversion after successfully helping Bahram Gur secure the Persian throne. He then withdrew into his majlisa, after which, according to the tradition, no one saw him again.

Like the earlier Muslim chroniclers, the author of Bulugh al-Arab records the following story about al-Nuʿman’s disappearance:

After this king had ruled for thirty years, he was one day sitting in his palace and began to reflect on his kingdom, his wealth, and the treasures he possessed.

He said:

“There is no good in something that belongs to me today and will belong to someone else tomorrow.”

He then renounced his kingship and ordered the guards at his door to withdraw.

When night fell, he wrapped himself in a cloak and went out wandering across the earth.

No one ever saw him again.

Muslim chroniclers repeated this moving story many times, drawing on older Arab traditions concerning the disappearance of al-Nuʿman III after his conversion to Christianity.

At some point, however, the storytellers seem to have merged several different bodies of material.

They combined the historical report with the legend of the construction of al-Khawarnaq, and then blended both with stories concerning the disappearance of one of al-Nuʿman’s descendants: al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir, known as Abu Qabus, around 602 CE.

After a political and religious dispute, the Persians lured this later al-Nuʿman into their territory, imprisoned him, and killed him. His body was never returned, and the precise circumstances of his death or disappearance in Persia were never revealed.

Like his ancestor al-Nuʿman III, this al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir had adopted Nestorian Christianity, the form of Christianity widespread in al-Hira.

The transmitters of the story of the Punishment of Sinimmar therefore produced an extraordinary fusion of images.

In place of al-Nuʿman III, they introduced another Christian king who was also called al-Nuʿman. The disappearance of the later king then became an organic part of the legend, even though it originally belonged outside it.

Al-Nuʿman III, however, did not disappear.

He withdrew from kingship and public life and devoted himself to worship in his hermitage—his majlisa—which the historical tradition places inside al-Khawarnaq.

The word saʾih, which became his title and was later misunderstood to mean that he had vanished or wandered aimlessly across the earth, actually means one who fasts.

The Qur’an uses related forms in expressions such as “women who are saʾihat, previously married and virgins”10 and “those who praise, those who are saʾihun.”11 The word here has the sense of those who fast.1213

Al-Nuʿman al-Saʾih, then, should be understood in much the same way as the Babylonian king Nabonidus, or Nabunaid, who ruled until 539 BCE.

Al-Nuʿman left his kingship after becoming a Christian and became saʾih: he withdrew from ordinary life and practiced Christian fasting and asceticism. The word does not mean that he was lost or simply vanished, as the chroniclers imagined.

This also recalls another old Arab legend, the story of Amr ibn ʿAdi, who disappeared after fleeing from his maternal uncle Jadhima al-Abrash and was later found by two drinking companions who had gone out to entertain the king.

In my view, al-Saʾih was a title adopted by al-Nuʿman after his conversion to Christianity, meaning something like the Faster or the Ascetic. Understanding the word in this way gives it an entirely different significance within the mythical narrative.

In both history and myth, kings often leave their cities and kingdoms and go into the desert to devote themselves to worship.

The last king of Babylon, Nabonidus, for example, lived at Tayma for about ten years and made it the capital of his empire.

Where, then, would a king of the desert go if he wished to abandon worldly life and devote himself to worship?

There could hardly be a more natural place than a majlisa—a hermitage in the desert.

This is how an old Arab proverb, “the reward of Sinimmar,” came to be merged with a Greek myth that may have circulated throughout Arabia during the Persian-Greek wars around 300 BCE.

Later, an Islamic narrator added historical reports concerning al-Nuʿman III and al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir, Abu Qabus, combined the two kings into a single person, and transformed the mythical Sinimmar into a historical figure.

Previous1. The Legend of al-Nadira’s Marriage and the Siege of the City-State of HatraNext3. al-Zabbaʾ Is Not Zenobia

3. al-Zabbaʾ Is Not Zenobia

The Legend of Jadhima al-Abrash in the Kingdom of al-Hira

In his account of the kingdom of al-Hira and its legendary king, Jadhima al-Abrash, al-Tabari writes (History, 1:439):

Jadhima al-Abrash belonged to the earliest of the original Arabs. His lineage went back to the Banu Wabar ibn Umaym. He was among the wisest of the Arab kings, the boldest in his campaigns, the harshest against his enemies, and the firmest in judgment. He was the first to bring the kingship of Iraq under his sole authority and unite the Arabs there under his rule. He suffered from a skin disease.

Continuing with the same care for detail and objective description, al-Tabari adds:

The Arabs were reluctant to call him “the leper” or identify him by that condition, out of respect for him. They therefore called him Jadhima al-Waddah and Jadhima al-Abrash. His territory lay between al-Hira, al-Anbar, Baqqa, and Hit. [...] Jadhima claimed prophetic powers and practised divination. He also kept two idols known as al-Dayzanan, whose site in al-Hira was well known. He appealed to them for rain and sought their help against his enemies.

Al-Tabari thus gives us a compact description of the hero at the centre of his stories about al-Hira, a city lying about three miles south of modern Kufa in Iraq. The city's name appears in the Babylonian Talmud in the form Hirta di-Tayyiba. He also defines with some precision the geographical area in which this kingdom existed.

Unfortunately, all that precision begins to break down as soon as myth enters the story as one of its sources. At that point, the narrator moves from historical report into legend.

The reader of al-Tabari has the impression that the author suddenly abandons the care and objectivity with which he had worked to establish the historical and geographical setting. Myth is then allowed to construct the story according to its own rules.

This happens when al-Tabari begins to tell of a proposed marriage that ends in deception and of Jadhima al-Abrash falling into the hands of al-Zabbaʾ at Palmyra. According to his account, the king of al-Hira was lured to Palmyra despite repeated warnings from his shrewd minister, Qusayr ibn Saʿd, and was murdered there.

History knows nothing of such an event. Nor does it know the supposedly brilliant minister Qusayr ibn Saʿd, whose name became associated with the famous sayings, “Qusayr did not cut off his nose for nothing,” and “Qusayr's advice is never heeded.” We likewise know of no war between al-Hira and Palmyra that ended with the king of al-Hira being captured and killed.

One striking feature of al-Tabari's account is its repeated suggestion that the plot began when al-Zabbaʾ offered to marry Jadhima.

That makes little sense if, as is usually assumed, al-Zabbaʾ was Zenobia of Palmyra. Zenobia was already the wife of the powerful king of Palmyra, the Arab emperor Odaenathus.

The story must therefore concern another queen called al-Zabbaʾ, one who either captured Jadhima or invited him to marry her as part of a scheme to take revenge on him.

We can now give a condensed version of the legend of Jadhima al-Abrash's death as related by al-Tabari in his History of the Kings and by al-Masʿudi in The Meadows of Gold.

The legend runs as follows:

Al-Zabbaʾ's soldiers were remnants of the Amalekites and of the original Arabs. She had a sister named Zubayba, for whom she built a palace on the Arab bank of the Euphrates. Al-Zabbaʾ “would spend the winter with her sister in the heart of al-Najjar, and then return to Palmyra. Once her affairs were settled and her kingdom secure, she resolved to make war on Jadhima al-Abrash and avenge her father.”

Zubayba opposed the idea of open war and urged her sister instead to devise a plot that would lure Jadhima to Palmyra and kill him. This was what eventually happened.

Al-Zabbaʾ sent word to Jadhima offering him marriage, “because she found that the rule of a woman brought nothing but an ugly reputation and weakness in authority.”

Jadhima received the message while on an excursion at Baqqa near Hit. Yet his appetite for conquest proved stronger than the peaceful proposal. He decided to march against her and seize her kingdom.

After heated discussions with his advisers and with his shrewd minister Qusayr ibn Saʿd, Jadhima left the throne in the hands of his beloved nephew, ʿAmr ibn ʿAdi, and personally set out against al-Zabbaʾ.

She, however, had prepared carefully for their meeting. She arranged a peaceful reception and showered her enemy with gifts and kindness. Once Jadhima became convinced that the proposal was genuine and that he was safe, the trap closed around him.

Al-Zabbaʾ then had Jadhima killed. “She seated him on a leather mat and ordered the veins in his arms to be cut. [...] She was told that if any of his blood fell anywhere outside the basin, vengeance would be demanded for it, since kings were not normally killed by having their heads struck off except in battle. Jadhima died, and al-Zabbaʾ inhaled his blood and placed it in a tuft of cotton.”

After this, the minister who had escaped returned to the dead king's nephew and told him to prepare to avenge his uncle. Qusayr said that he had a plan.

The cunning minister then cut off his own nose and fled from al-Hira to Palmyra, where he sought al-Zabbaʾ's protection. He told her that ʿAmr ibn ʿAdi, Jadhima's nephew, had mutilated him and declared his blood forfeit because he blamed Qusayr for allowing Jadhima to fall into the trap and be killed.

Al-Zabbaʾ believed the fugitive minister's story.

Having won her confidence, Qusayr suggested that he organize trading caravans between al-Hira and Palmyra. Al-Zabbaʾ agreed. Three successful journeys followed, bringing the city considerable wealth and merchandise.

The minister-spy then prepared his fatal trick.

Instead of merchandise, he filled the great sacks carried by the camels with armed horsemen, placing two men inside each pair of panniers14 and fastening them to the animals.

Once the soldiers were hidden inside the sacks and panniers, the camel caravan left al-Hira for Palmyra. The young king ʿAmr ibn ʿAdi himself was among the warriors concealed inside. He had decided to take part personally in avenging his murdered uncle.

“When they came close to her city, Qusayr rode ahead to al-Zabbaʾ and gave her the good news. He told her of the great quantity of cloth and rare goods he had brought and asked her to come out and see the camels and their loads.

“Al-Zabbaʾ came out. She saw that the camels' legs were almost sinking into the earth beneath the weight of what they carried. [...]

“The camels entered the city one after another. As the last camel passed the gatekeeper, a Nabataean holding a goad, he jabbed the sacks beside him. The point struck the side of the man hidden inside, making him break wind. [...]

“The gatekeeper said in Nabataean: Bishta bisqa”—that is, ‘There is evil in the sacks.’15

“Once the camels had reached the centre of the city, they were made to kneel. The men climbed out of the panniers, raised the alarm against the people of the city, and attacked them with their weapons. [...]

“When al-Zabbaʾ saw ʿAmr ibn ʿAdi, she sucked on her ring, which contained poison.”

For the purpose of analysis, and using a cautious method that makes as few assumptions as possible, we can divide al-Tabari's text—and by extension al-Masʿudi's—into two parts: the historical report and the myth.

We will first establish a historical framework in which to examine the “report” in al-Tabari. We will then take apart the second element, the legend itself.

The main purpose of this method is to make textual criticism easier.

The king of Palmyra and husband of Zenobia was Odaenathus. His great-grandfather was Nasr, or Nasru, and his grandfather was Wahballat. His father was called Hairan.

An inscription from 235 CE gives Odaenathus's name as:

Odaenathus son of Hairan son of Wahballat son of Nasr

At the time he was a member of the Roman Senate, when Palmyra was still subordinate to Rome. According to Roman documents, his father was known as Septimius Hairan, an aristocratic title rarely granted to an Arab king allied with Rome.

The king of al-Hira, meanwhile, was Jadhima ibn Malik ibn Fahm, known as Jadhima al-Abrash. His name appears in inscriptions as:

Jadhimat, King of Tanukh

A famous inscription known as the Umm al-Jimal inscription, found about seven kilometres south of Bosra in Syria, bears the following words on a grave marker:

This is the tomb of Fihr ibn Shali [Sali], tutor of Jadhimat, King of Tanukh.

Archaeologists have dated the inscription to about 270 CE, placing it in the same general period in which Odaenathus ruled at Palmyra.

The period in which the historical events and the legend are set, roughly 226–261 CE, was one of upheaval throughout the East. The Sasanian dynasty was rising in Persia under Ardashir son of Babak.

During the fierce wars between Persia and Rome in 253–260 CE, the Persian king Shapur managed to capture the Roman emperor Valerian. Odaenathus, the powerful king of Palmyra, then organized a major offensive against Shapur. He defeated him and forced him to flee the battlefield.

This success encouraged the Romans to grant the Arab ruler the title Emperor of the East.

These, in very brief outline, are the two historical “heroes” behind the report and the legend as presented by al-Tabari, al-Masʿudi, and other Arab chroniclers.

We should add one further point.

Odaenathus was killed in mysterious circumstances at Palmyra around 266–267 CE, together with his son Herudat. Jadhima likewise died in circumstances that remain obscure, at al-Hira around 271 CE.

Al-Tabari disastrously confused Jadhima al-Abrash, king of al-Hira in Iraq, with Odaenathus, king of Palmyra in Syria. He wrongly concluded that al-Zabbaʾ had killed Jadhima at Palmyra after deceiving him.

The strangest part is that the deception supposedly began with an offer of marriage, even though al-Zabbaʾ, if she is identified with Zenobia, was the wife of the powerful king Odaenathus.

It would be unfair simply to blame al-Tabari for the confusion, although the confusion itself certainly deserves criticism. The name Jadhimat has puzzled even archaeologists, and disagreements continue over the Umm al-Jimal inscription and why it should have been found in this part of Syria when Jadhima was king of al-Hira in Iraq.

Did his kingdom extend as far as the region south of Bosra? Or had he merely travelled there?

Whatever the answer, al-Tabari merged Jadhima with Odaenathus. A similar process transformed al-Zabbaʾ into Zenobia.

The result is a particularly striking and serious historical confusion.

Like other chroniclers, al-Tabari effectively created a character called al-Zabbaʾ who did not exist in this period, or indeed in history in the form presented to us.

We will now try to understand how this happened.

The woman called al-Zabbaʾ, whom modern Arab historians have mistakenly identified with Zenobia, was in fact, I would argue, the Arab queen who resisted Tiglath-Pileser III (727–744 BCE) and who appears in Assyrian records under the name Za-bi-bi.

Stories of this queen and of her capture continued to circulate among the tribes. Over time they must have become mixed with legends about other queens, just as the name Za-bi-bi, or Zubayba, became confused with that of Zenobia.

Tiglath-Pileser III is known to have captured this queen during one of his military campaigns. Alois Musil proposed that Za-bi-bi had ruled at Dumat al-Jandal, in the region of today's al-Jawf in Saudi Arabia, where the famous fortress known as al-Ablaq stands.

There is further support for reading the name as Zubayba, Za-bi-bi, rather than Zenobia.

Al-Tabari himself gives the name Zubayba to al-Zabbaʾ's sister and makes her a queen west of the Euphrates rather than at Dumat al-Jandal.

This detail seems to preserve, unconsciously, traces of recurring oral tribal traditions about this queen's wars against the Assyrians, rulers of ancient Iraq. Those traditions became blended automatically with the wars of Zenobia, queen of Palmyra, just as the figures of Jadhima and Odaenathus were also merged.

In my view, this survival reveals something of the confused historical truth preserved within al-Tabari's accounts.

The legend does not really take place in Palmyra during the age of its great queen Zenobia. Its setting is elsewhere, west of the Euphrates, precisely where al-Tabari tells us a queen called Zubayba ruled.

It seems that one of the old kings of al-Hira16 fought wars against her and eventually died in one of them.

Because of al-Tabari's account, later Muslim chroniclers became accustomed to treating the names Zenobia and al-Zabbaʾ as equivalents. Modern historians then followed the same tradition with almost no caution or scrutiny, repeatedly equating the two names.

In doing so, they reproduced the same uncreative fusion found in al-Tabari's stories about al-Hira and Palmyra.

Arab writers consequently became accustomed to using the name al-Zabbaʾ whenever they wrote about Palmyra, believing that Zenobia and al-Zabbaʾ were one and the same person.

They were not.

Al-Tabari's legend misleads its audience in two ways:

  1. It asks us to accept the mysterious assassination of Odaenathus at Palmyra in 266–267 CE as though it were the same event as al-Zabbaʾ's killing of Jadhima al-Abrash, king of al-Hira, also at Palmyra. In other words, we are asked to believe that Jadhima was slaughtered after falling into a trap prepared by al-Zabbaʾ, even though there is no firm evidence for this. By contrast, we can at least entertain the possibility that Odaenathus himself was killed by his wife Zenobia after being lured into a trap.

  2. It asks us to imagine that al-Zabbaʾ was Zenobia, queen of Palmyra. This does not appear plausible.

Al-Tabari's report and the legend embedded within it therefore provide an unusually useful example of the way echoes of history can take shape inside old tribal traditions.

The following section will try to explain that process more clearly.

Al-Zabbaʾ's small chiefdom—or that of Za-bi-bi—lay west of the Euphrates, as al-Tabari himself confirms. During the age of Tiglath-Pileser III, it lived in a state of continuing political tension and unrest with its neighbouring kingdom of al-Hira, or Hirta.

The history of military and political tension between these two tribal chiefdoms becomes easier to understand when placed within the traditional rivalries between Arab tribal kingdoms in the deserts of Iraq and Syria.

One had been founded by the Lakhmids, while the other belonged to remnants of the Amalekites.

This explains why al-Tabari and al-Masʿudi repeatedly insist that al-Zabbaʾ's soldiers were remnants of the Amalekites. Had they really meant the army of Palmyra, there would have been no reason to describe them in this way.

Another detail helps reveal the confusion.

Virtually all the early Islamic accounts—including those of al-Bakri in The Dictionary of Unfamiliar Place-Names, Yaqut al-Hamawi in The Dictionary of Countries, as well as al-Masʿudi, al-Tabari, Ibn al-Athir and others—say that al-Zabbaʾ was:

“the daughter of ʿAmr ibn al-Zarb the Amalekite.”

This genealogy is invented. It does not occur in Arab genealogical trees and, more importantly, has nothing to do with the ancestry of Zenobia of Palmyra.

The ancient writers themselves disagreed over Zenobia's Arab origins. Al-Masʿudi even questioned her lineage, claiming in The Meadows of Gold that she was “a Roman woman who spoke Arabic.”

There is more.

The ancient and Muslim chroniclers also repeatedly claim that:

“Palmyra was named after Tadmur, daughter of Hassan ibn Odaenathus the Amalekite.”

Such claims cannot be verified historically, but they can be studied as myth.

The uncertainty, contradiction, and confusion surrounding attempts to give both the queen and the city an acceptable genealogy arise, in reality, because the woman in question was the tribal queen al-Zabbaʾ rather than Zenobia.

The word Tadmur itself seems to have been an old source of confusion among the Arabs. It is the Aramaic pronunciation of the Arabic word tamr, “dates”: tatmur, Tadmur.

I believe the original form was tathmur, from thamar, “fruit,” with the th later softened to t, just as Thamir can become Tamir. The name would therefore be connected with the presence of a dense palm oasis.

There is a reasonable basis for this assumption if we remember that the Romans called Palmyra Palmyra, a name derived from palma, “palm tree.” This suggests that the Romans understood the meaning of the older name Tadmur and reproduced it using a Latin equivalent.

Yet this same name could also belong to a woman, just as the early chroniclers claimed.

In Genesis (11/38–26/37), the name Tamar, daughter-in-law of Judah, appears as a Hebrew form of the name Tamr.

It seems that the ancient Arabs knew this name in the forms Tamar, Tamr, and Tatmur, and that some of their early traditions linked the building of the city with the prophet-king Solomon.

Under the influence of this same confusion, al-Nabigha al-Dhubyani retold the legend of Palmyra's construction in a famous poem:

Except for Solomon, when God said to him: Rise in the wilderness and keep it free from folly. Subdue the jinn, for I have given them leave To build Palmyra with stone slabs and pillars.

All this suggests that the claim about a woman named Tadmur, daughter of Hassan the Amalekite, may belong to the city's distant childhood—to traditions from before Palmyra emerged clearly onto the historical stage.

The story therefore points us west of the Euphrates, rather than to Roman Palmyra.

When we turn to the sources from which the legendary narrative was constructed, we can identify three main elements used by whoever created the story that al-Tabari later transmitted, sometimes as history and sometimes as myth.

1. The rumours surrounding the death of Odaenathus

Rumours circulated in Palmyra in 266–167 CE that Zenobia had arranged the assassination of her husband and his son by another wife, Herudat.

The circumstances of the killing remained mysterious, but suspicion continued to follow Zenobia. Several lines of argument suggested that she had much to gain from Odaenathus's death, since it would allow her own son to inherit his father's throne.

These rumours appear to have travelled widely and circulated among the Arab tribes.

Only about three years later, in 271 CE, a similar event occurred: Jadhima al-Abrash died in mysterious circumstances, after which the young ʿAmr ibn ʿAdi, Jadhima's nephew, came to the throne at al-Hira.

A clever but highly suspicious identification was then made. The two deaths were fused together and presented as a single assassination whose great stage was Palmyra.

ʿAmr ibn ʿAdi—called Bar ʿAdi in the Talmud—was effectively like a son to Jadhima, who was said to have been a childless priest. The Persians installed ʿAmr as king of al-Hira.

Brockelmann, in his History of the Peoples, believed that Shapur I (241–272 CE) was the ruler who imposed ʿAmr ibn ʿAdi as king over the Arabs of al-Hira.

Jadhima al-Abrash's death was mysterious, and al-Tabari's legend encourages the suspicion that some conspiracy lay behind it. This made the story especially easy to combine with the assassination of Odaenathus and the installation of the underage Wahballat, with Zenobia serving as regent.

The rumours circulating in Palmyra at the time all moved in the same direction: the beautiful and educated Zenobia had killed her husband Odaenathus.

This helps explain why the story has al-Zabbaʾ propose marriage to Jadhima. Symbolically, al-Zabbaʾ is made to slaughter her own husband.

Some rumours went further and claimed that the Romans themselves had arranged Odaenathus's assassination in order to rid themselves of a powerful and ambitious king during an unstable period.

That is at least a plausible hypothesis if we consider Roman fears about the ambitions of the “Emperor of the East,” who had become intoxicated by his victories over Persia.

If that hypothesis is correct, it would mean that Persia also hastened to replace its own man at al-Hira, arranging Jadhima's mysterious death in parallel with what Rome had done.

2. The Trojan Horse

The story of Troy and its horses was apparently well known among the tribes, as al-Tabari's text itself seems to show. This would have been entirely possible given the spread of Greek culture.

The legend of Jadhima therefore appears to imitate the story of the Trojan Horse.

Instead of a wooden horse, however, we have a caravan of camels.

In the Greek legend, the Greek heroes hide with their weapons inside a huge wooden horse prepared by King Agamemnon on the advice of the seer Calchas.

When the horse is brought into Troy, Laocoön strikes its belly with the spear in his hand to make sure that it is empty.

The Nabataean gatekeeper at Palmyra does precisely the same thing when he strikes the sacks full of hidden horsemen with the goad he is carrying.

And just as the spy Sinon plays a decisive part in persuading the ruler of Troy to bring the wooden horse inside the city, the minister Qusayr ibn Saʿd performs the same role in the Arab legend.

3. Cleopatra’s death

The third source used in constructing the Arab legend is the scene of Cleopatra's death, here borrowed in the form of al-Zabbaʾ sucking poison from her ring.

Several considerations encouraged this borrowing, all related to the Arab chroniclers' claim that al-Zabbaʾ was of Amalekite origin while also assuming that she was Zenobia.

First, Zenobia claimed Egyptian ancestry. This was widely reported in historical traditions.

Second, the Amalekites had once, in the distant past, ruled Egypt around 1750 BCE. In that sense they too could be described as Egyptian.

Another identification was therefore created:

If al-Zabbaʾ was of Amalekite origin, then she was Egyptian. And if Zenobia claimed Egyptian ancestry, then Zenobia must have been al-Zabbaʾ.

The scene of Cleopatra's death by poison after her defeat by the Roman emperor was accordingly borrowed from Egypt and transformed into a solemn, magnificent ending worthy of al-Zabbaʾ.

In al-Tabari's legend she dies by sucking poison from a ring, in a scene modelled on Cleopatra's death.

Yet history tells us that Zenobia died in Rome after being taken there as a humiliated captive.

Al-Tabari's story of Jadhima al-Abrash's death at Palmyra contains far more than confusion over names and places.

It preserves an echo of a remote and legendary past, still reverberating through our modern world in the form of tangled stories that we are expected to accept as historical fact.

Serious criticism of historical texts therefore requires serious engagement with material that may at first look like unbelievable myth.

Palmyra's fame, which overshadowed the many smaller and older Arab kingdoms, may have encouraged ancient storytellers to imagine every flourishing kingdom as another Palmyra.

In much the same way, the fame of Za-bi-bi, or Zubayba—the courageous Arab queen who resisted the Assyrian Empire—may have overshadowed the memory of other tribal queens, until every “great queen” became al-Zabbaʾ.

What matters in the end is our ability to draw a boundary between historical information about such figures and the myths passed down by the tribes from one generation to the next.

This is how history has repeatedly fused one image with another.

Previous2. The Legend of al-Khawarnaq Palace and Sinnimar’s RewardNext4. The Wars of Alexander the Great and the Legend of Zarqaʾ al-Yamama

4. The Wars of Alexander the Great and the Legend of Zarqaʾ al-Yamama

Ancient Arab mythology, like Greek mythology, often requires the prophecy of a priestess or seer before tragedy can unfold.

The central pattern in ancient Arab and Greek tragedy is remarkably similar.

A seer stands before the people and announces that disaster is coming. Everyone is told to prepare. Yet the tragedy can only be fulfilled once the prophecy is collectively rejected. The people refuse to believe it and may even denounce the person who delivered the warning.

Then the disaster comes.

The legend of Zarqa al-Yamama, one of the best-known stories in both ancient and modern Arab popular tradition, follows precisely this pattern.

Arab and Islamic sources retold it again and again as an integral part of what was presented as a historical account of a war between Tasm and Jadis, two tribes traditionally counted among the extinct Arabs.

Al-Tabari, al-Masʿudi, and Yaqut al-Hamawi all relate the legend within historical accounts of tribal warfare and of the role played by ancient Yemen in installing and supporting tribal rulers.

Readers therefore rarely stop to consider whether the story of Zarqa al-Yamama really belongs to an actual war between two Arab tribes, or whether Yemen truly played the decisive role in determining their historical fate.

According to the historical narrative found in al-Tabari, Ibn al-Athir, and al-Masʿudi, the tribe of Jadis was ruled by Tasm.

One of Tasm's kings was named ʿAmliq and was allied with Saba.

According to the legend, his cruelty and injustice eventually became so extreme that he imposed a brutal rule on Jadis: every bride had to be brought to him before her wedding, and “no virgin woman of Jadis was allowed to marry until she had first been taken to him, so that he might violate her before her husband.”

The legend continues by saying that a rebel from Jadis organized a successful assassination of King ʿAmliq, his court, and his followers.

This allowed Jadis to seize control from its rival tribe, Tasm.

One man from the defeated tribe escaped to Yemen, where he appealed for help to its king, Asʿad Tubban ibn Malikikarib.

The king sent a military expedition to punish Jadis for its rebellion and restore Tasm to power. The campaign was led by his son, Tubbaʿ Hassan, who immediately set out to carry out the mission.

Tubbaʿ marched with his army until he came near Jaww, which is al-Yamama.

When he was about a night's journey away, near a mountain there, Riyah—the name of the fugitive—said:

“Stop here, O king. I have a sister married among Jadis called Yamama. She has the keenest eyesight of anyone God created. She can see a person from a distance of a day and a night's journey. I fear she will see us and warn the people.”

Tubbaʿ stopped at the mountain and ordered one of his men to climb it and report what he saw.

When the man reached the top, a thorn entered his foot. He bent down to remove it.

Yamama saw him. She had blue eyes.

She said to her people:

“My people, I can see a man on the mountain, and I think he must be a spy. Be on your guard.”

They asked:

“What is he doing?”

She replied:

“Either he is mending a sandal or chewing on a shoulder.”

They refused to believe her.

Riyah, the man from defeated Tasm, then said to the king:

“Order your men to cut branches from the trees and hide behind them so that Yamama will be deceived. Have them march like that during the night.”

Tubbaʿ asked:

“Can she see at night as well as she can by day?”

He replied:

“Yes, O king. Her sight is even sharper at night.”

Tubbaʿ gave the order. His men cut branches from the trees, each soldier carrying one in his hand.

As they approached Jaww at night, Yamama looked out and cried:

“People of Jadis! Either the trees themselves are marching towards you, or the horsemen of Himyar are coming!”

Again they refused to believe her.

At dawn the Himyarite army fell upon them. Hassan destroyed Jadis, killed its people, and took its children captive.

He then summoned Yamama, daughter of Murr, and ordered her eyes to be torn out.

Inside them they found black veins.

He asked her what they were.

She replied:

“They come from a black stone called ithmid. I used it as kohl around my eyes.”

She was said to have been the first person to use it this way, after which other people adopted it as kohl.

The king then ordered Yamama to be crucified at the gates of Jaww.

The people said:

“Call Jaww by the name al-Yamama.”

Following an old cultural tradition whose Greek model can be seen in the Iliad, pre-Islamic poets turned historical tragedies into poetry.

They did this not simply because stories, fables, myths, and inherited traditions fascinated them, but because poetry itself had originally grown out of narrative prose before becoming an independent literary form.

The legend of Zarqa al-Yamama seems to have captivated poets for a long time.

Al-Aʿsha himself retold it in verse, in a Homeric manner:

When she looked, hers was no idle glance, As the shimmering haze raised up the dog's head.

She said: I see a man holding a shoulder in his hand, Or perhaps mending his sandal—what a strange sight.

They called her a liar, and at dawn The lord of Hassan's house came upon them, driving spears and weapons before him.

They forced the people of Jaww from their homes And brought their towering buildings down.

Among the possible transmitters of the legend, one man is especially important: Jubayr ibn al-Hassan.

According to Yaqut al-Hamawi and the chain of transmission he cites, Jubayr was himself from al-Yamama.

As we shall see, this matters greatly because he came from the city or region universally regarded by the narrators as the historical home of the legend.

The scholars who evaluated transmitters disagreed about Jubayr ibn al-Hassan.

Abu Hatim al-Sijistani said that there was nothing objectionable in his reports, while al-Nasaʾi considered him weak.

As we shall also see, this disagreement is extremely important when we come to separate the historical report from the myth.

In some forms of ancient Arab mythic storytelling, the narrator works hard to explain why a particular people, community, place, phenomenon, or even a secondary name came into existence.

Decoding and interpreting names lies at the heart of the function of myth.

Thus the place called Jaww becomes al-Yamama because a woman from Jadis named Yamama was crucified there after warning her people of the approaching disaster and being ignored.

There is little point in asking why, if the Yemeni king tortured the woman, killed her, and hung her body at the city gates, he would then allow the city to be named after her.

The ambiguity in the narrator's behaviour is deliberate. It directs us towards the symbolism of crucifixion, guilt, and remorse.

But another question remains.

Why does the myth suddenly turn to kohl? What connection could kohl possibly have with the black veins supposedly found inside the woman's eyes?

The mythical narrator, whose story al-Tabari presents—strangely enough—as historical fact, connects the Arabs' discovery of ithmid, the mineral used as kohl, with the existence of a blue-eyed woman.

This connection becomes more understandable if ithmid is placed within a “sacred family” of substances possessed by the Arabs in the ancient world: incense, myrrh, and frankincense, commodities that made them one of the wealthiest peoples of antiquity.

These substances were associated with temples. Rome itself complained about the burden placed on its finances by the enormous cost of incense imported for its temples from the south-western Arabian Peninsula.

Each substance therefore carried a sacred quality and required a miraculous origin.

That miraculous origin is born from tragedy.

Another connection also seems to structure the story: the relationship between exceptional eyesight and ithmid.

If we go further, another link appears between the girl's name, Zarqa, and the Arabic root z-r-q, which the author associates here with sharpness of sight.

Several observations can be made.

1. Kohl and extraordinary sight

This part of the myth rests on an old popular belief that survives in Arab folk culture to this day: kohl makes the eyesight sharper, or even greatly strengthens it.

The scene in which the girl's eyes are torn out and black veins of ithmid are discovered inside them therefore expresses an ancient mythical idea about the substance that would become kohl.

Within this belief, it takes the form of a plant or substance appearing as black veins, and these veins are the secret of her unusually sharp—and, through their blueness, beautiful—eyes.

2. The meaning of the name Zarqa

The narrator chose the word Zarqa with particular care.

The root z-r-q, in this interpretation, can suggest keen or powerful sight.

Arab and Muslim philologists debated the real meaning of the word.

Thaʿlab, for example, explained the Qur'anic phrase:

“On that day We shall gather the criminals zurqan”17

by saying that zurqan meant “thirsty.”

Ibn Sidah rejected this as a forced interpretation. He believed the word referred to the eyes becoming blue from extreme thirst.

Sibawayh held a similar view, as did al-Sirafi, who understood zurq to mean sharp-sighted.

In my view, Thaʿlab was not entirely wrong in his interpretation of the verse, because zurqan conveys intensity in the description of the criminals' punishment. The same idea of intensity appears in the expression for powerful or penetrating vision.

Zarqa al-Yamama, on this reading, means the woman of powerful sight.

3. Zarqa bint Zuhayr, the priestess of Hajar

The ancient Arab origin of the legend is connected with the existence of a priestess in the land of Hajar called Zarqa bint Zuhayr.

Specific traditions circulated about her, some preserved by al-Bakri in The Dictionary of Unfamiliar Place-Names.

The narrators also put poor rhyming prose and verses into her mouth, presenting them as prophecies of future tragedies and disasters.

This belongs to an old Arab cultural tradition.

We find it in a long chain of legendary stories about male and female seers who announce prophecies to the tribes and clans that follow them, much as in the prophecies of Jewish prophets and priests.

One piece of rhymed prose attributed to Zarqa bint Zuhayr may contain a forgotten origin for the idea of cutting down branches and using them to conceal the movements of the Yemeni army.

It is said that the tribes once asked her about a particular matter, and she replied:

“Palm fronds, ahan, tigers, and milk are better than humiliation.”

The saying has no clear meaning. Its value lies elsewhere: it preserves the idea that a woman called Zarqa, the great priestess of Hajar, gave advice in which palm fronds were somehow connected with events that were about to occur.

There is certainly a real geographical connection between Hajar and al-Yamama, which according to ancient measures lay about ten days away.

The lands of Tasm and Jadis extended across this broad region and towards Bahrain, which included Hajar, while Hassan, the Yemeni prince, was based in Najran.

The narrator of the Arab legend took the fact that Zarqa bint Zuhayr was a priestess of Hajar and al-Yamama and developed from it the idea of prophecy, because prophecy is what a seer does.

He then developed a second idea that paralleled and completed the first.

To prophesy is to see things, events, and dangers before they happen.

Prophecy can therefore be imagined as a form of vision—an unusually sharp and powerful kind of sight.

The meanings attached to the legendary and historical name were thus brought together.

The name Zarqa points to a female seer, but it also points to extraordinary eyesight.

From these elements the narrator created the striking image of a legendary woman able to see the enemy before they had even reached us.

And as listeners to the legend, we strongly desire precisely this kind of figure: someone capable of seeing our enemies while they are still on their way.

If these are, broadly speaking, the main elements of the old Arab legend of Zarqa al-Yamama, the priestess of Hajar, then the historical material requires a much more cautious approach.

We need to criticize the sources used by al-Tabari, who transmitted the legend, and separate the historical material from the mythical material in his account.

The same applies to al-Masʿudi and Yaqut al-Hamawi. Like al-Tabari, both remained faithful to an old cultural convention: telling historical reports in a mythic form.

So who was Hassan, the Himyarite prince who marched to al-Yamama on a military campaign?

Was he the same figure whom some Islamic sources call Tubbaʿ ibn Hassan ibn Tubbaʿ ibn Malikikarib al-Aqran?

We know nothing precise about any king or prince bearing this exact name.

We know almost nothing about the supposed king of Tasm called ʿAmliq, the period in which he lived, or the revolt of Jadis against him.

Yet Yemeni inscriptions do speak of a Yemeni prince called Hassan Yuhaʾmin ibn Asʿad Karib, who lived around 430 CE.

This Hassan ruled Najran alongside his father, ruler of Saba, Dhu Raydan, and Hadramawt.18

Tubbaʿ Hassan undertook a military expedition into Arabia to punish tribes that had rebelled against Yemeni authority. This is reported by Ibn Khaldun, Ibn Qutayba in al-Maʿarif, al-Tabari in his History of the Kings, and Wahb ibn Munabbih in The Crowns of the Kings of Himyar.

Such punitive campaigns were a traditional practice of Yemeni kings against northern tribes seeking independence.

The literary accounts, however, expand Hassan's campaign far beyond plausible geographical limits.

They claim that he marched through al-Anbar and al-Hira and continued as far as Tibet and China, according to al-Tabari and Wahb ibn Munabbih.

That, as we can see, is the mythical enlargement of a historical war.

The Yemeni inscriptions restrict the campaign to a much smaller geographical area: al-Yamama and Hajar.

Hartmann doubted whether a historical hero called Hassan had existed at all because of confusion in the reading of his name in Yemeni inscriptions.

Sometimes the inscriptions appear to call someone the son of Hassan, while elsewhere the same figure appears as Hassan himself. In one place he seems to be the father, in another the son.

This recalls the same confusion found in the Arabic chronicles, where the name is sometimes recorded as:

Tubbaʿ ibn Hassan ibn Asʿad ibn Malikikarib.

Hartmann proposed that by “Hassan” the Arab chroniclers may actually have meant the Yemeni king Sharhabil Yaʿfur, who ruled around 450 CE.

Yet Ryckmans's list places this Hassan before Sharhabil Yaʿmur.

Jawad Ali, in his Detailed History, quotes inscription Philby 288, which reads:

Ab Karib Asʿad and his son Hassan Yuhaʾmin, kings of Saba, Dhu Raydan, Hadramawt, Yamanat, and their Arabs of the highlands and lowlands. The sons of Hassan Malik Karib Yuhaʾmin, king of Saba, Dhu Raydan, Hadramawt, and Yamanat.

Like Philby, Glaser, Ryckmans, and others, Jawad Ali understood this to mean that Ab Karib Asʿad, father of Hassan Yuhaʾmin, was himself the son of a Hassan Malik Karib Yuhaʾmin who had also been king.

He therefore asked whether we are dealing with a king whose name was Hassan Malik Karib Yuhaʾmin, or whether this was actually the same Malik Karib Yuhaʾmin who appears in earlier inscriptions, with Hassan functioning as a title rather than a personal name.

The confusion in reading the inscription—father in one place, son in another—is strikingly similar to the confusion found among the chroniclers.

What explains it?

Dr Jawad Ali may indeed have been right to suggest that Hassan was a title rather than a personal name, borne by a Yemeni king of the family of Asʿad, the ruler known as Asʿad al-Kamil.19

Whatever the precise solution, there is enough evidence to support the reality of the military campaign and therefore the basic accuracy of the historical report preserved by the chroniclers.

What damages that accuracy is the excessive blending of much older memories into the historical event.

Hassan's campaign in Hajar was originally directed against the land of Maʿadd. It was not a campaign against Tasm and Jadis.

Those two tribes could no longer have existed by around 450 CE, since tradition itself places them among the extinct Arabs.

Al-Tabari's insertion of their names, relying in part on al-Aʿsha's poetic account, belongs to a process we may call successive fusion: names and places from different periods are repeatedly merged within the structure of early Arab historical storytelling.

The result is that the campaign of the Yemeni king Hassan Yuhaʾmin against northern tribes in the land of Maʿadd comes to look like an echo of much older expeditions by the kings of Yemen against two ancient tribes of the original, now vanished Arabs: Tasm and Jadis.

The memory of the Yemeni campaign into the land of Ma'add around AD 410 seems to have awakened older memories buried deep in the past, memories whose historical setting was Hajar and al-Yamama. The storyteller then combined places, heroes, and victims into a new legendary narrative. Ma'add gave way to Tasm and Jadis, who were placed in al-Yamama; and as this merging continued, the leader of the campaign was recorded at one point as Tubba' Hassan and at another as Tubba' ibn Hassan. It is almost as though the uncertainty surrounding the original inscription produced a corresponding uncertainty in the Arab and Islamic sources. Dr Jawad Ali's suggestion that Hassan should be treated as a title rather than a personal name deserves serious consideration. It may allow us to reread the inscription and the historical narrative together, giving each equal weight.

Within this repeated process of combining and reshaping traditions, the Greek mythical element remains strongly present in the structure of Arab legendary narrative.20 Yet through the force of storytelling and continual adaptation, it eventually became thoroughly Arab. The legend of Zarqa al-Yamama is, in fact, an echo of Greek stories and myths, some of them connected with the Trojan War. The Arab tribes appear to have heard these stories again and again. Al-A'sha may even have imitated the Iliad, retelling Greek legendary material in verse as the story of the prophetess Zarqa. This is what I shall try to show here.

One Greek story, forming part of the wider cycle of legends surrounding the Trojan War, tells of Cassandra, daughter of Priam, king of Troy, and his wife Hecuba. Cassandra became the victim of Apollo's anger. The god fell passionately in love with her and, because of that love, gave her the gift of prophecy. At first Cassandra appears to have promised to return Apollo's love. But she soon became afraid that she might lose her prophetic gift, broke her promise, and withdrew from him. Apollo immediately took revenge by ensuring that no one would ever believe her prophecies.

When the Greeks attacked Troy, Cassandra warned the inhabitants that the enemy was approaching. No one was prepared to believe her. The Greek heroes were therefore able to enter Troy while Cassandra's prophetic power had, for all practical purposes, been rendered useless.

Here, then, is the Greek counterpart of Zarqa al-Yamama: Cassandra. Her original image was blended with similar figures, or figures derived from it, including Zarqa bint Zuhayr, the great prophetess of Hajar. Within the historical narratives, tribal wars between Tasm and Jadis in al-Yamama were then imagined anew. In the crude machinery of the new legend, the conflict came to appear as though it had really taken place in the time of Hassan Yuha'min, and as though he had crucified a young woman called Zarqa al-Yamama.

The storyteller in this case was Jubayr ibn al-Hasan. Al-Nasa'i regarded him as a weak transmitter, while al-Sijistani also questioned him because of the weakness of his transmission of hadith. Yet he did more than repeat the old legend. He introduced new elements into it. Remembering the prophetess of Hajar—and al-Yamama, his own homeland, as we have already seen—he merged her image once again with that of Cassandra, who warned the Trojans but was not believed.

This suggests that Ibn al-Hasan, or perhaps one of the other transmitters of the story, may himself have heard the Greek legend. And why not? Hajar, whose great historic capital was Gerrha, was according to the Greek geographer Strabo, who visited it, one of the wealthiest and most important cities of its age. Greek merchants were constantly present in its markets.

Earlier I suggested that some importance should be attached to the fact that this story was transmitted by a man who came from al-Yamama itself, the supposed scene of the events. Why should that matter?

A modern reader need not find the following possibility surprising: Jubayr ibn al-Hasan may have heard a Greek version of the Trojan War in al-Yamama or in its flourishing markets. A large Greek community once lived in Arabia along the Red Sea coast and around the port of Aden. In time, this community was absorbed into the Arab tribes. Yet its culture, memories, and perhaps even its myths need not have disappeared with it. They may instead have survived by being repeatedly merged with new stories transmitted by later settlers.

We have a particularly good example of this kind of settlement. The Yemeni island of Socotra21 was home not only to Arab and Indian communities, but also to a Greek one. Greeks had reached the south-west of the Arabian Peninsula by sea during the military campaigns of Alexander the Great, around 336 BC.
