Al-Hamdani, the Yemeni historian, estimated their number at ten thousand fighting men and said that they later converted to Christianity.

The author of the Periplus of the Erythraean Sea, however, believed that the presence of this Greek community was partly the result of a Ptolemaic campaign against the port of Aden, after which a number of Greek families were settled there. At a later period, around 50 BC, the port fell to the Romans following another military expedition.

Yaqut al-Hamawi, for his part, claimed that Aristotle himself had advised Alexander the Great to seize Socotra and Aden and to leave a Greek colony on the island. According to this tradition, that community continued to live there while preserving the memory of its origins and ancestry.

Could the legend of Cassandra, then, have taken root in Arabia more than a thousand years before Hassan's campaign against the tribes of Ma'add?

One final point remains. The intensity and effectiveness of this continual blending of images within classical Arab legendary narrative can be seen especially clearly in the presence of elements belonging to Sumerian mythology. In the Epic of Gilgamesh, for example, we can find what appears to be the original model for the Arab king Imlīq, who is, of course, a non-historical figure. The epic tells us:22

He brought shame and corruption upon the city, burdening the unfortunate city with abuses and forced labour. They sounded the drum for the king of Uruk, city of the great marketplaces, so that at its call he might choose whatever bride he desired. For Gilgamesh, king of Uruk, city of the great marketplaces, the drum was sounded so that he might choose brides before their husbands and be the first bridegroom before the man they were to marry.

The echo of these successive images can surely be heard, in some form, in the legendary world of The Thousand and One Nights, where the killing of brides each night becomes an even more explicit symbolic representation of defloration. Shahriyar kills his bride every night. The Arab Imlīq and the Sumerian Gilgamesh perform a symbolically similar act by taking the virginity of brides before their weddings.

Several layers of material—ancient Arab, Sumerian, and later Greek—therefore helped shape the legendary narrative surrounding Zarqa al-Yamama. Ultimately, these layers point back to genuine historical reports that have been transformed into myth: reports of a relatively late Yemeni military campaign against the northern tribes of Ma'add.

During the telling of that campaign, memories of earlier Yemeni expeditions against ancient Arab tribes were revived. Those older campaigns belonged to a period when Yemen was a powerful centralized state.

Cassandra, meanwhile, was absorbed into the Arab tradition as Zarqa, a name interpreted here as “the one who sees,” an idea close to that of the prophetess or seer. Her presence is strongly felt in the stories surrounding this later military campaign. The tribal tragedy was imagined as another Troy: a disaster centred on a young woman whose predictions no one could be persuaded to believe.

There is something especially striking here. The Greeks imagined their prophetess in much the same way as the Arabs later imagined theirs. In Greek mythology she appears in the form of the Pythia, the prophetess of the temple at Delphi. The Pythia speaks in broken, obscure utterances, very much like the speech attributed to Zarqa al-Yamama, and a priest then interprets what she has said.

The prophetess sits above a fissure from which vapours rise. She then falls into a violent trance and utters words that seem meaningless. What Arab tradition calls the obscure saj' al-kuhhan—the rhymed speech of soothsayers, irregular in form and lacking an obvious logical sequence—is likewise one of the characteristic features of the work of a priestess or Pythia. Her prophecies therefore often require interpretation.

This is the Greek Zarqa al-Yamama: the prophetess who warned the Trojans.

I should also mention another Greek legend, the story of Perdix and Daedalus. Perdix, an architect and inventor, was the nephew of Daedalus. Jealous of his nephew's talent, Daedalus threw him from the top of the temple of Athena on the Acropolis. When a court assembled to judge the crime, Daedalus was sentenced to exile.

The exile of Daedalus parallels the disappearance of al-Nu'man after he threw Sinimmar from the top of the palace of al-Khawarnaq.

Previous3. al-Zabbaʾ Is Not ZenobiaNext5. The Fabrication of Yemen’s History and the Greco-Persian Wars

5. The Fabrication of Yemen’s History and the Greco-Persian Wars

Kings Who Never Existed

The genealogies of the kings of ancient Yemen, as published by Arab genealogists and recorded in the writings of two of Yemen's most important historians—al-Hamdani, writing around 350 AH, and Nashwan ibn Sa'id al-Himyari, around 573 AH—reveal one of the most familiar problems in the Arab treatment of the past. These genealogies are crowded with mythical kings, leaders who never existed, and “heroes” who were never born.

Al-Hamdani's early concern in al-Iklil seems to have been directed not simply towards establishing these genealogies, some of which are mythical from beginning to end, but also towards arguing that one lineage or another should be moved from one branch of a genealogical tree to another.

The clearest example is a dispute that produced heated debate among Yemeni scholars from around 400 AH onwards. Nearly two centuries after al-Hamdani's death, his enormous work al-Iklil had begun to establish older beliefs more firmly, embedding them within accounts of the history and genealogy of Yemen's kings and spreading them among the tribes.

Nashwan ibn Sa'id al-Himyari then launched a severe attack on al-Iklil. He mocked its author and called for the rejection of one of the book's central claims: that the lineage of the legendary king al-Harith al-Ra'ish should not be placed among the descendants of Lesser Himyar but somewhere else.

What is particularly interesting about the disputes of this period is that they did not concern only the mythical genealogies of the Qahtani tribes and peoples. They also concerned the supposed historical existence of kings of ancient Yemen for whom there is no reliable evidence at all—either that they were kings or, in some cases, that they existed in the first place.

The Yemeni scholar Muhammad Abd al-Qadir Bafaqih observed in Yemeni Studies 2 (1985) that al-Hamdani's discussion centred on the ancestry of al-Harith al-Ra'ish and his insistence that the king belonged to the line of al-Sawwar.

This famous debate, preserved by the chroniclers as a series of conflicting accounts concerning genealogies and rulers supposedly once sovereign over Yemen, confirms how old and deeply inherited the confusion surrounding Arab genealogy really is. It eventually developed into an entire culture of dispute rooted in mythical conceptions of history.

That, very briefly but adequately, is the broader historical setting of the problem I want to address.

The name of the legendary king al-Harith al-Ra'ish—whose ancestry was disputed by the genealogists of Yemen and the Arabs, and whose name has never appeared in any Yemeni inscription discovered by scholars—raises an important question.

Could there be a connection between his title, al-Ra'ish, and the legendary wars he was said to have fought?

Ancient writers imagined the title to come from the Arabic verb rasha, with meanings such as acquiring wealth, taking booty, bringing riches, or giving. According to al-Andalusi in Nashwat al-Tarab fi Akhbar Jahiliyyat al-Arab, Wahb ibn Munabbih in al-Tijan fi Muluk Himyar, Ubayd ibn Shariya al-Jurhumi in Akhbar al-Yaman, and al-Hamdani himself in al-Iklil, al-Harith was called al-Ra'ish because he “enriched all the people.”

Ibn Qutayba says in al-Ma'arif:

Kingship then passed to al-Harith al-Ra'ish. He was the first of them to go out on military campaigns, seize spoils, and bring them back to Yemen. He was therefore called al-Ra'ish because through those riches he made the people prosperous.

Beyond the qualities attributed to this king, the word rasha, from which the ancient writers unanimously derived his title, gives us reason to pause. It can be compared with the English word rich, meaning wealthy, and with Dutch rijk. The same form appears clearly in Dutch-French dictionaries as riche, with the same meaning, and in our own colloquial Arabic expression mrayyash, meaning a wealthy man.

Dr Ali Fahmi Khishaym, in Is There Anything Foreign in the Qur'an?, observed that this root, rich, may be connected with the Latin rix, meaning “king.” Khishaym did not pursue the possible connection between this word and the supposed historical figure, limiting himself to that brief observation.

I would suggest, however, that Ra'ish should not be understood as the name of a Yemeni king, as researchers have generally assumed, but as a royal title. This distinction is extremely important, because it offers a possible solution to the puzzle and may help us identify the approximate period in which the ruler behind the title lived.

If al-Ra'ish was the title of an obscure local ruler whose image was transformed into myth after some historical event, we can reconstruct a possible setting for that event.

Ancient Arab writers imagined the existence of a Yemeni king famous for campaigns far beyond Yemen and Arabia. They eventually had him conquering India, Persia, and Babylon before returning home victorious.

This picture seems to have arisen through the merging of two major but separate events belonging to very different periods. One was an event of world-historical importance; the other was local and limited, though politically significant.

Arab chroniclers and Yemeni genealogists also confused two different figures: the Yemeni king Yasir Yuhan'im, known to the chroniclers as Nashir al-Ni'am, and a local, legendary ruler called al-Harith al-Ra'ish.

The first event was international in scale. It belonged to the age of the Greco-Persian wars and the conquests of Alexander the Great in Asia Minor, Syria, Egypt, and Persia, extending as far as India around 330 BC. Yemen at this time lay within the Persian sphere of influence. The news of Alexander's victories and conquests in Persia and the surrounding regions must certainly have echoed throughout Yemen.

The second event concerned the success of some unknown and obscure Yemeni king or tribal leader in unifying the ancient kingdom of Saba by forcibly incorporating Hadramawt after the two had remained separate under his predecessors.

Something like this happened several times in the history of ancient Yemen.

According to an account in al-Isfahani's History, which is reasonably precise, al-Ra'ish was the first ruler to be called Tubba' because he succeeded in bringing the kingdoms of Saba and Hadramawt together under a centralized state. Al-Isfahani claims that fifteen generations separated al-Ra'ish from Himyar.

Two events were therefore fused into one. The local unification of Hadramawt and Saba, achieved by force, was imagined as though it were equivalent to Alexander's defeat and subjugation of Persia.

We shall later see how deeply feelings of hostility towards Persia ran among the Yemenis. They celebrated Alexander's victories as if they were victories of their own, just as other peoples oppressed by Persian influence also welcomed them.

This merging of the global event with the local one can be explained by the fact that, during the age of the Greco-Persian wars, Yemen itself longed to escape Persian domination.

At some point, the Yemenis may therefore have Arabized the Latin word rix so that it coincided with an Arabic root carrying a similar idea: ric meaning king, followed by the associations “to enrich,” rasha, and finally Ra'ish. Alternatively, the Arabic, Greek, English, and French words may all descend from some other common root. That question lies outside the scope of this study.

What matters here is that the title al-Ra'ish, attached to a legendary king, arose within a group of romantic ideas cherished by Yemenis who longed for release from Persian political domination, a domination whose roots reached back to the age of Cyrus.

Those hopes were repeatedly revived by the events of the Greco-Persian wars. Every Greek victory over Persia could then be imagined as a local, even national, Yemeni victory. Alexander himself could accordingly be transformed into a “Yemeni hero.”

We shall see the same process when we examine the legends surrounding the supposed Yemeni king Ifriqis and Dhu al-Qarnayn.

The early Yemeni chroniclers Ubayd ibn Shariya al-Jurhumi and Wahb ibn Munabbih, followed by al-Andalusi, identify al-Ra'ish as Dhu Mara'id, rather than the son of Dhu Mara'id as other writers claim. They also call him “the Younger Ra'ish,” which by implication requires the existence of an Elder Ra'ish.

Ibn Qutayba, in al-Ma'arif, places him within the genealogy of Himyar:

Kingship remained among the descendants of Himyar after him. Their rule did not extend beyond Yemen, and none of them went out on military campaigns for many generations, until kingship passed to al-Harith al-Ra'ish. He was the first among them to campaign abroad, seize spoils, and improve their condition. He was called al-Ra'ish because he enriched the people with those spoils.

Ibn Qutayba's account repeats a familiar pattern in early Arab historical narrative. Al-Ra'ish is presented as a ruler who strengthened Yemen and brought prosperity to its successive kingdoms, though the nature of that achievement and the period in which it occurred are never clearly identified.

The text nevertheless hints at foreign campaigns that supposedly placed him among the great conquerors of ancient Yemen.

These “conquests,” I would argue, are simply the conquests of Alexander the Great. Their dazzling image became merged with memories of an internal political and military success: the reunification of Hadramawt with Saba, something that happened more than once.

The accounts of al-Isfahani and later al-Andalusi, in which Hadramawt was restored to the kingdom of Saba after a long period outside central control during the age of the petty kings, return the title al-Ra'ish to its proper setting. The title derives from a word associated with the prosperity and wealth of the kingdom during a period of renewed unification, which coincided with the defeat of Persia or at least a weakening of Persian control over Yemen.

This, I believe, is the historical material lying behind the legend that al-Harith al-Ra'ish conquered India and Babylon and returned victorious.

It also supplied the raw material for the romantic idea that fired the imagination of the Yemeni chroniclers: the rebirth of an ancient, centralized, prosperous Yemen. A local victory was transformed into a world victory, and the conquest of Hadramawt was cleverly identified with the Greek conquest of Persia.

In other words, a global victory was steadily and unusually absorbed into Yemeni memory as a local victory as well.

We shall see this process very clearly when we examine how the title of Alexander the Great, Dhu al-Qarnayn, was borrowed and applied to local kings in Yemen and al-Hira in Iraq.

Within this framework, al-Andalusi gives al-Ra'ish's ancestry in Nashwat al-Tarab as follows:

He was al-Harith ibn Qays ibn Sayfi ibn Saba the Younger.

Ibn al-Kalbi, as quoted by Ibn al-Athir in al-Kamil, gives a slightly different genealogy:

Al-Harith ibn Qays ibn Sayfi ibn Ya'rub ibn Qahtan.

Both writers place him in the time of the Persian king Manuchihr and claim that he invaded India, took prisoners, killed his enemies, seized enormous wealth, and then returned to Yemen, where he received the title al-Ra'ish. He later set out once again, this time to invade Babylon.

These legendary wars, which expand from the local—the annexation of Hadramawt—to the global—the conquest of India and Babylon—are simply another blending of images drawn from different periods of Yemeni history.

They were magnified in order to express the same romantic idea: the revival of a country repeatedly weakened by devastating natural disasters, including volcanic eruptions and torrents of lava, the collapse of the Marib Dam, and sudden breakdowns in the political, economic, and social networks that held the country together.

This fragmentation then produced what the chroniclers call the “petty kings,” each of whom hurried to detach a part of Yemen and establish an independent territory beyond the reach of the central government.

The Yemeni historical imagination rearranged these different images and combined them into a new narrative. Ultimately, they are echoes of the campaigns of Alexander the Great, who defeated Persia and invaded India and Babylon, just as the Arab accounts describe.

The passages already quoted from Ibn Qutayba, al-Andalusi, al-Isfahani, and Ibn al-Athir contain more than enough material to construct the image of a legendary king. Naturally, that image resembles the equally repetitive portraits of other supposed rulers.

The important conclusion to draw from al-Harith al-Ra'ish is that behind the legend may lie a shrewd local ruler who at some point succeeded in imposing political unity on Yemen by annexing Hadramawt and thereby securing a period of prosperity.

We do not know his real name. Nor is there any archaeological evidence supporting the claims made by Arab chroniclers and genealogists about his existence.

This king effectively “disappeared” behind a title invented by the chroniclers and became hidden beneath legends echoing the victories of Alexander the Great. Yet Yemeni genealogists still argued over his ancestry. Did he belong to the line of Lesser Himyar?

Anyone who studies the history of ancient Yemen sooner or later finds himself face to face with legendary, mysterious, almost fantastical figures performing heroic deeds that are difficult even to imagine: conquering Babylon, invading India, subduing Persia.

Their transformation into myth is intensified by the remarkable level of detail with which Arab chroniclers describe the “history” of these characters. They are repeatedly presented as members of royal dynasties that ruled ancient Yemen and are carefully assigned places within the genealogies of Saba and Himyar.

Modern Arab historians have generally responded to this almost formulaic picture of Yemen's ancient kings in an equally formulaic way. They reject the non-historical portraits and accuse the early chroniclers of invention and fabrication.

It has by no means been unusual to hear a distinguished historian such as the late Dr Jawad Ali call for this mythical material to be treated simply as the inventions of chroniclers. Others followed the same pattern, dismissing the narratives of Ibn al-Athir, Ibn al-Kalbi, Hammad al-Rawiya, al-Tha'alibi, Wahb ibn Munabbih, and Ubayd ibn Shariya al-Jurhumi. Important historical works have themselves been reduced to the status of worthless collections of stories.

This habit of dismissal has long been widespread and continues to enjoy strong support among historians of the traditional school, as well as among occasional readers and amateurs interested in history and mythology.

As a result, repeated opportunities have been lost to examine critically these non-historical, mythical, and fantastic portraits of Yemen's ancient kings. A more careful approach would reclassify the narrative material and separate, within the texts themselves, the historical from the mythical.

The history of ancient Yemen can be reopened and reconsidered calmly as part of a larger attempt to understand the remote historical childhood of the Arabs. It offers researchers an ideal field in which to undertake such work, provided that they possess a new, cautious, and genuinely critical method.

The obscurity surrounding some of these royal figures became so great that the name of the legendary king Shammar Yuhar'ish—or Bara'ish—the First, for example, came to refer to three or four different individuals, each supposedly bearing the same name. This can be seen in Dr Jawad Ali's al-Mufassal, despite the absence of any adequate or understandable reason for doing so.

The door was then opened for other scholars to perform an equally arbitrary operation. They simply added “the Second,” “the Third,” or “the Fourth” to the name Shammar Yuhar'ish.

We suddenly possessed three or four new kings who could be inserted into the lists of ancient Yemeni rulers.

Modern Arab scholars had therefore increased the mythical character of the very Yemeni history they had originally criticized, while accusing the ancient chroniclers of fabrication.

Until the mid-1980s this remained the prevailing atmosphere in Yemeni historical studies. Then a new factor appeared, producing another, much more productive kind of confusion—one that would later help to resolve the uncertainty surrounding this Shammar Yuhar'ish.

The Yemeni scholar and historian Mutahhar Ali al-Iryani obtained the Bayt Di'yan inscription, found near Sanaa, and produced a detailed reading of it. In my view, his work settled the matter decisively and allowed at least one of these supposed kings to be removed from history altogether.

The inscription records important events involving a rebel against the rule of the Yemeni king Ilsharah Yahdub. The rebel, called Shammar Bihamad, succeeded with the support of tribes loyal to him in seizing strategically important positions and proclaimed himself king of Saba and Raydan.

The legitimate king, Ilsharah Yahdub, resisted the rebellion and fought a fierce war against Shammar Bihamad and his tribal allies. He also launched a broad campaign of humiliation and defamation against his opponent, apparently hoping to erase his name from memory and remove him mercilessly from history.

He seems to have succeeded brilliantly.

In his inscriptions, Ilsharah Yahdub referred to his enemy simply as:

Shammar of Raydan.

Shammar himself, meanwhile, proclaimed that he was king of Saba and Raydan.

The form in which Ilsharah Yahdub recorded his opponent's name—Shammar of Raydan—clearly expresses contempt and an attempt to diminish both the man and his military achievements. At best, the legitimate king was acknowledging him merely as a rebel who had seized Raydan.

Shammar Bihamad, for his part, remained silent in the face of this humiliation and made no attempt to answer it. He simply continued to publish his name as “Shammar of Raydan,” without any personal or political title.

In this way, a genuine historical figure became known to us through inscriptions written by his enemy, King Ilsharah Yahdub.

Yet as soon as modern historians encountered this “Shammar of Raydan,” they rushed to identify him as Shammar Yuhar'ish II and inserted him into their lists of Yemen's kings.

The example shows that a number of supposed kings of ancient Yemen never existed at all. Some of them were inventions not merely of the ancient chroniclers, but of modern Arab historians.

There is an irony here. Modern historians directed fierce criticism, often coloured by contempt and even dislike, at the mythical imagination of the ancient chroniclers. Yet in the course of doing so they ended up founding new historical myths of their own, inventing figures who had no real basis.

One example is the famous story that Tariq ibn Ziyad burned his ships. This, I would argue, was originally an ancient Yemeni legend that modern historians reshaped and then introduced into history as a factual report.

From this perspective, mere astonishment or dislike at the imagination of the chroniclers and their ability to generate non-historical images is of little value. It may even obstruct a freer and more productive reading of history.

Wahb ibn Munabbih in al-Tijan and al-Andalusi in Nashwat al-Tarab fi Akhbar Jahiliyyat al-Arab both provide a broadly plausible historical setting for the appearance of these kings and for their involvement in violent local struggles: the age of the Greco-Persian wars, around 300 BC.

Together, they also present a picture of the age of the petty kings in Yemen, a genuine historical period that coincided with Alexander the Great's victories over Persia.

Al-Andalusi writes that the appearance of the petty kings in Yemen coincided with Alexander's success in killing Darius, king of the Persians. He also connects it with the great flood—the collapse of the Marib Dam—and the beginning of the migration of the Lakhmids from Yemen.

As a starting point, this is a reasonable picture, because the fragmentation of ancient Yemen did not in practice result simply from the collapse of the Marib Dam.

Taken by itself, that explanation belongs to the realm of myth. A deeper and more convincing cause was the weakening of Persia's grip on Yemen, followed by the collapse of Persian influence after Persia's defeat by Alexander.

The fall of Persian power encouraged the aqyal of Yemen—its local lords—and tribal leaders to rebel against the central authority and establish themselves independently in districts, petty states, and small kingdoms.

At a later period, during the reign of Ilsharah Yahdub, the rebel Shammar Bihamad appeared within this same general pattern. Modern writers mistakenly identified him as Shammar Yuhar'ish, or Bara'ish, II.

This supposed Shammar Yuhar'ish II can therefore be removed from history without difficulty.

The same principle applies to another legendary king around whom all the ancient sources accumulated stories and myths: the Yemeni ruler Ifriqis ibn Nashir al-Ni'am, Nashir al-Ni'am being Yasir Yuhan'im in the inscriptions.

Al-Isfahani claims in his History that Ifriqis was the son of Abraha and that he became king after Nashir al-Ni'am.

The Yemenis, according to both al-Andalusi and al-Isfahani, claimed that this same Nashir al-Ni'am was Dhu al-Qarnayn, “the Two-Horned One,” and that he received this title because he had two locks of hair hanging down his back.

All the early chroniclers also claimed that Ifriqis gave Africa its name after marching there and conquering it, having already subdued India, Persia, and Babylon.

In reality, no Himyarite king of Yemen with this name ever existed. Nor did any Yemeni king at any point in the ancient history of Yemen or the Arabs accomplish such conquests.

After extensive investigation, I have concluded that this Ifriqis is simply the title borne by the great Greek commander Aemilianus Scipio during the Greco-Persian wars.

Within this framework, I was able to establish clearly that Aemilianus—or Ifriqis—was a contemporary of the Persian king Artaxerxes II, who ruled from 404 to 359 BC.

Among the Greeks this Persian king was known by the title Artaxerxes. During the periods when Yemen came under direct Persian rule or influence, the Arab tribes eventually Arabized such royal terminology into Kisra, which then became the familiar title applied to the kings of Persia.

The brilliance and suddenness of Greek victories seems to have fascinated Yemenis who had grown weary of Persian domination. Stories of these wars penetrated deeply into Yemeni and wider Arab memory, where Greek victories were gradually absorbed and reimagined as Yemeni victories.

Later, during the fragmentation of Yemen and the weakening of Persian power after its defeats by Alexander, the petty kings began fighting among themselves. Each tried, according to his strength and ambition, to seize part of Yemen and establish an independent state outside the control of the central authority.

The Greek Ifriqis then became an attractive model. One of the Yemeni aqyal may even have imitated him and adopted Ifriqis as a borrowed title. Along with the title came the legends of military campaigns that carried the Greeks through Egypt, Persia, and Babylon as far as India.

Alongside Ifriqis stands an even more thoroughly legendary Yemeni king: al-Sa'b Dhu al-Qarnayn.

In the genealogies of Yemen's kings, al-Sa'b appears as the son of al-Harith al-Ra'ish, whom we have already shown to have been either a minor tribal leader or one of the petty kings. Naturally, al-Sa'b too is credited with a long sequence of legends involving the conquest of India, Persia, and Babylon.

The author of al-Tijan, followed by the author of Nashwat al-Tarab, relates the following:

Ali ibn Abi Talib, may God be pleased with him, said: “Tell stories about Himyar, for its stories contain wonders, and among the most wondrous of them is the story of Dhu al-Qarnayn.”

A bitter dispute arose among the Arabs before Islam and continued after the revelation of the Qur'an concerning the identity of Dhu al-Qarnayn.

Some Muslim chroniclers believed that he was “one of the Arabs.” Others said he was a foreigner, and they then disagreed over which foreign people he belonged to.

When Ibn Abbas, may God be pleased with him, was asked about the matter, he understood with his characteristic learning and good judgment the nature of the difficulty that divided Muslims: history and myth had become intertwined.

He answered:

“He was from Himyar. He was al-Sa'b ibn Dhu Mara'id, whom God established firmly in the earth and to whom He gave the means to accomplish all things, until he reached the two horns of the sun.”

In this answer, the two main elements of the historical tradition—history and myth—are plainly interwoven.

The historical element lies in Ibn Abbas's identification of Dhu al-Qarnayn as al-Sa'b ibn Dhu Mara'id. Such a man may indeed have been one of Yemen's local lords or a minor tribal king from the age of the petty kings.

The mythical element lies in the extent of the campaigns attributed to him.

The phrase “whom God established firmly in the earth” can properly apply only to the imperial Alexander, Dhu al-Qarnayn, rather than to his Yemeni local counterpart, who admired Alexander and modelled himself on his military achievements.

Ibn Abbas effectively acknowledges this distinction when another curious questioner asks him:

“What, then, of Alexander?”

In other words: what about Alexander the Roman, the man whom God really did establish across the earth, east and west?

Ibn Abbas replies:

“He was a righteous Roman man. He built a lighthouse by the sea, subdued the West, and built cities and fortifications there. The Tubba' kings boasted that Dhu al-Qarnayn was their ancestor and mentioned this in their poetry.”

Ibn Abbas thus drew a reasonable boundary between the two figures, the Greek and the Yemeni, even though each bore the same title.

The most interesting part of his answer is the valuable observation that the Tubba' kings of Yemen regarded Alexander the Great as their ancestor.

This recalls the claims of the Abyssinians and the Jews of Ethiopia to descend from Solomon, king of Yemen. Such claims of descent from a distant legendary ancestor clearly belong to a broader search for sacred genealogy.

The Arab chroniclers say that Dhu al-Qarnayn once dreamed that he would sweep across the whole earth:

“When he awoke, he saw that all the animals had been gathered before him. He then marched before the nations.”

The account appears in both Nashwat al-Tarab and al-Tijan.

The dream takes us back to the mythical image of Solomon, the king and prophet who possessed the power to speak with animals, creatures that submitted to his authority.

After the dream, Dhu al-Qarnayn sets out with his armies:

“Killing and dealing harshly, he passed by black peoples with blue eyes, and by others whose ears were like the ears of camels. He then crossed into al-Andalus, overcame its regions, and sought to sail upon the encircling ocean...”

There can be little doubt that one of Yemen's petty kings adopted the title associated with Alexander the Great, inspired by Alexander's victories. This borrowed title became the source of confusion between the two men, whose images were eventually fused into a single marvellous legendary hero.

The extravagant and magical Yemeni imagination, to which Ali ibn Abi Talib alluded when he said, “Tell stories of the wonders of Himyar,” reflects something the Arabs and Muslims themselves understood very well.

Yemeni chroniclers were famous for an exceptionally fertile imagination and for an astonishing ability to generate an apparently endless stream of images and stories. Much of this arose from a creative power of combination in which historical reality could melt into imagination and invention.

The images of the petty kings were therefore merged with the heroes of the Greco-Persian wars.

Alexander's victories fascinated the Arab tribes in general, but they seem to have fascinated the Yemenis in particular. Here was the image of a heroic liberator coming from beyond the seas to drive their oppressors from the stage of history.

The Arabs, and especially the tribes of Yemen, carried deep feelings of hostility towards what they saw as Persian cruelty and domination. Alexander's spectacular victories in the East must therefore have seemed like a historic opportunity to escape the pressure of the Persian Empire.

Within this historical setting, later local victories in Hadramawt, Saba, Raydan, and Aden—belonging to a much later age when Yemen's petty kings fought among themselves—were repeatedly and attractively reimagined.

A polished and compelling equivalence was created between local victories and the great victories of world history. From this process emerged entire dynasties of legendary kings who had never existed, yet were said to have ruled Yemen and whose genealogies were traced back to Saba and the elder Himyar.

A powerful literary convention seems to have entered Arab historical storytelling at this point and later left a deep mark on historical writing under Islam. Its basic method was to retell the stories of neighbouring peoples, rival tribes, or foreign nations as though they belonged to the community doing the telling.

Under this convention, the Persian defeat at the hands of the Greeks became a Persian defeat at the hands of the kings of Yemen. Wars between Greeks and Persians became Yemeni wars against Persia.

Al-Andalusi, the intelligent and careful author of Nashwat al-Tarab, stands almost alone among the chroniclers in offering a highly plausible answer to the puzzle of the supposed Yemeni Dhu al-Qarnayn. He writes:

“Alexander the Macedonian astonished the world with his rapid victories over the Persians, and because his fame rose so high, the nations struggled over him, each claiming that he belonged to them.”

That precise and eloquent sentence may be enough on its own to describe the meaning and impact of the Greco-Persian wars in the fabrication of ancient Yemeni history.

It shows how far the echoes of those wars travelled and how deeply they remained embedded in Yemeni historical memory. Long afterwards, Yemen retold those wars, no longer in the voices of the peoples who had actually fought them, but in the voices of those who had adopted their roles and made their victories their own.

Previous4. The Wars of Alexander the Great and the Legend of Zarqaʾ al-YamamaNext6. Did Arab Tribes Find Yemeni Inscriptions Beneath the Foundations of the Kaaba?

6. Did Arab Tribes Find Yemeni Inscriptions Beneath the Foundations of the Kaaba?

A New Reading of Ibn Hisham’s Life of the Prophet

Of all the early reports concerning the history of Mecca, those claiming that the northern Arab tribes discovered inscriptions beneath the Kaaba during the pre-Islamic period are of exceptional importance. If subjected to serious historical study, they may prove more valuable than almost any comparable body of material concerning Mecca’s ancient past.

At the very least, the legendary accounts of Quraysh and Kinana finding inscriptions beneath the Kaaba—most of them apparently Yemeni in character—raise an intriguing possibility. The tribes may have known the legendary history of Mecca not only through oral traditions passed down from generation to generation, but also through written inscriptions that supported, reinforced, and gave authority to those traditions.

Several accounts also suggest that the Arab tribes were somehow able to read these inscriptions, whether they were written in ancient Arab dialects or in other languages.

Yet these reports, like so much else preserved by the great early Arab chroniclers—al-Tabari, al-Yaʿqubi, and later Ibn al-Athir—have attracted remarkably little attention from scholars. Arab researchers seem largely to have passed over them with indifference, perhaps even contempt, because they regarded them as worthless legends.

Historians of the ancient Arab world often assume that history is full of myths. My own view is that myths are also full of history.

That is the starting point of this study. We need to move away from the traditional approach to ancient Arab history and begin paying serious attention to its myths, because hidden inside them may lie a lost and unwritten history.

For that reason, I intend to examine these reports in all their legendary richness, beginning with the form in which they appear in Ibn Hisham’s Prophetic Biography, and then comparing them with parallel accounts preserved by early chroniclers such as al-Fakihi and al-Azraqi, the latter writing around 223 AH. These two are among our finest early historians of Mecca.

My purpose is to test how far these remarkable reports might help us reconstruct a history of the Arabs—and of their sacred religious centre, the Kaaba—that is more coherent and more consistent with historical reality.

Put another way, I want to push the process of extracting history from myth as far as it can reasonably go. What belongs to history should be restored to history; what belongs to myth should remain within mythology. The symbolic structure of those myths, and the meanings carried by their imagery, can be examined separately on another occasion.

Modern Arab scholars of Mecca and the Kaaba have tended to look down on anything in the historical record that appears to them to be nothing more than legend or folklore. They have rarely tried to learn the alphabet of myth—the language Erich Fromm called the “forgotten language”—as a way of gaining a deeper understanding of the past.

The early Arab writers, by contrast, seem to have possessed a strong instinct that history could lie buried inside myth, folklore, and even the most unbelievable reports. That is why they continued to record almost everything that came before them.

This alone helps explain why sensible Muslim historians devoted so much attention to stories that, at first glance, look like little more than popular legends or fables. They were working from sources that have never reached us, and that fact alone should make us appreciate the value of much of what they preserved.

We can begin with several reports about “inscriptions” supposedly discovered by Arab tribes beneath the Kaaba.

1.

Ibn Hisham claims in the Sira that the Arabs found inscriptions written in Syriac beneath the Kaaba. He quotes Ibn Ishaq:

Quraysh found writing in Syriac at the Yemeni Corner. They did not know what it said until a Jewish man read it for them. It said: “I am God, Lord of Bakka. I created it on the day I created the heavens and the earth, formed the sun and the moon, and surrounded it with seven upright angels. It shall not disappear until its two mountains disappear. Blessed are its people in water and milk.”

2.

Ibn Ishaq also says:

I was told that they found writing at the Maqam which said: “Mecca is the Sacred House of God. Its provision comes to it by three roads. The first of its people shall not violate its sanctity.”

3.

Ibn Hisham, again quoting Ibn Ishaq, says that the Arabs found a stone bearing a moral saying:

Ibn Ishaq said: “Layth ibn Abi Sulaym claimed that, forty years before the Prophet’s mission—if what he reported is true—they found a stone at the Kaaba on which was written: ‘Whoever sows good will harvest happiness, and whoever sows evil will harvest regret. Do you commit evil deeds and expect to be rewarded with good? Certainly not, just as grapes are not harvested from thorns.’”

The brackets in the original edition were added because the transmitted text is confused.

4.

Ibn Hisham also claims that the Arabs once discovered an ancient inscription beneath the Kaaba which read:

“Who ruled Dhamar? The noble Himyarites. Who ruled Dhamar? The wicked Abyssinians. Who ruled Dhamar? The free Persians. Who ruled Dhamar? Quraysh the merchants.”

5.

Al-Azraqi, author of Reports of Mecca, preserves another account attributed through its chain of transmission to Ali ibn Abi Talib:

“When the Kaaba burned during the pre-Islamic period, Quraysh demolished it in order to rebuild it. When they uncovered the foundations beneath one of its corners, they found a stone on which was written: ‘I am Yaʿfur ibn Abd Qara. I send greetings to my father from the beginning of three thousand years.’”

These accounts have been arranged here for purposes of study and analysis. They require careful examination so that later additions can be separated from earlier material.

We must also distinguish the report of an inscription being discovered from the supposed contents of the inscription itself.

Accepting, for the sake of investigation, that an inscription may have been found does not require us to accept everything later narrators claimed it contained. As we shall see, some of the chroniclers may have attached later Islamic material to older reports.

The appearance of the name Quraysh, for example, immediately gives us reason for caution. Quraysh was not known by that name until the period of Qusayy, the Prophet’s ancestor. There is therefore no evidence that Quraysh ever ruled Dhamar, whether Dhamar refers to a city or to a tribe.

Even so, the bare report that Arab tribes discovered inscriptions connected with the history of Mecca—and particularly the Kaaba—is extremely valuable, regardless of the legendary content later attached to it.

Here we have what may be historical reports wrapped in mythical material. The two must be separated.

If the reports themselves contain a historical core, they undermine the common claim that the ancient history of the Arabs, and by extension that of Mecca and the Kaaba, was based entirely on oral tradition, with Muslims later inventing the earliest portions of that history through legendary storytelling.

For all these reasons, the idea that ancient Arab tribes discovered inscriptions—or, as the old sources call them, “stones”—is particularly compelling. It suggests that written records may once have existed concerning this very early period, a period that is still generally understood almost entirely through religious and legendary tradition.

Yet Ibn Hisham’s reports about these stones immediately raise another question.

Where did the tribes keep the inscriptions they discovered?

Did they return them to the places where they had found them? Did they place them once again beneath the ancient foundations of the Kaaba?

And did the ancient Arabs deliberately preserve inscriptions of this kind beneath the foundations of the Sacred House in the first place?

What connection might these stones have had with the idea of the “cornerstone” in ancient Arab religious belief?

The reports above say that the tribes found such stones in the corners of the Sacred House, at the Yemeni Corner, and at the Maqam. This suggests that the old religious architecture rested on a building practice that has survived, in one form or another, to the present day: placing an inscribed cornerstone recording something about the building itself or about the event associated with its construction.

Many archaeological discoveries have confirmed the antiquity of this architectural custom. Even today, the expression “cornerstone” remains part of our everyday language.

The more important question, however, is this:

Could the supposed Dhamar inscription mentioned by Ibn Hisham be the same inscription later discovered by Glaser?

Or might it somehow be connected with the Bayt Dabaʿan inscription?

Ibn Hisham’s report of the “Dhamar inscription,” which he and others claim was found beneath the foundations of the Kaaba and contained the name Quraysh, may be the most important of all these accounts in relation to our argument that written records existed concerning the oldest period of Arab history.

From this point onward I shall call it the “reported Dhamar inscription”, to distinguish it from the Glaser inscription and the Bayt Dabaʿan inscription. The other reports can be examined separately on another occasion.

The name D-M-R / Dhamar appears in several ancient Yemeni inscriptions, including the well-known Glaser inscription, GL 1228, and the Bayt Dabaʿan inscription discovered by the Yemeni historian Mutahhar Ali al-Iryani.

What is striking in all these texts, whether epigraphic or literary, is that the name D-M-R / Dhamar remains ambiguous.

It may refer to the city of Dhamar, which today lies roughly an hour by car from Sanaʿa.

It may refer to a legendary king named Damar or Dhamuran, about whom Yaqut tells a remarkable story.

Or it may be the proper name of a famous Yemeni tribe.

Yaqut al-Hamawi defines Dhamar as follows:

Dhamar: a name for Sanaʿa. Sanaʿa is an Abyssinian word meaning fortified and secure. The Abyssinians gave it this name when they saw Sanaʿa after entering Yemen with Abraha. Ibn Duraid gave the word with an open vowel and said: when Quraysh demolished the Kaaba during the pre-Islamic period, they found in its foundations a stone written in the Musnad script: “Who ruled Dhamar? The free Persians. Who ruled Dhamar? Quraysh the merchants.”

In substance, Yaqut’s account differs little from Ibn Hisham and the other Arab chroniclers. It develops two basic ideas.

First, Arab tribes—here specifically Quraysh—found inscriptions containing the names of kings, tribes, or states, including the ambiguous name Dhamar.

Second, there appears to be some obscure relationship between the Kaaba and Dhamar, whether the name refers to a city or a tribe.

Nor do we know precisely what kind of relationship linked Persia and Abyssinia with Dhamar, and finally with Quraysh.

This means that the reported inscription must be read differently, without overlooking the literary form in which its message has been preserved.

It also means that the tribes were somehow able to read these inscriptions and understand what they said.

As for Yaqut’s claim that “Sanaʿa is an Abyssinian word” and that Dhamar was another name for Sanaʿa, several points need to be made.

1.

It is possible that ancient Sanaʿa was at one time known as Damar or Dhamar, long before the Abyssinian occupation of Yemen in AD 525. The modern city of Dhamar still lies close to Sanaʿa.

The name itself may originally have belonged to the well-known tribe of Dhamar.

The problem can therefore be approached in two ways: Dhamar may have been the name of both a city and a tribe.

2.

The name nevertheless raises another possibility because of its apparent connection with the legendary king Dhamuran.

Yaqut al-Hamawi reports, from unnamed sources, that Dhamuran and his brother Dalan were the first men to bring beautiful foreign women into the country and spread sexual immorality.

This is clearly the surviving fragment of a myth that settled into the depths of oral tradition. Little remains of it beyond a confused and obscure report apparently intended to explain why two villages near the modern city of Dhamar were called Dhamuran and Dalan.

3.

If Dhamar and Dhamuran are connected in any way, the relationship probably belongs to a cultural convention known both to the Arabs and, for example, to the Israelites of the Bible.

According to al-Hamdani in al-Iklil, ancient Sanaʿa once bore the extremely old and legendary name Uzal.

The same name appears in the Bible in two forms: once as the name of an ancestral father, and once as the name of a city.

Ezekiel 27:13, 29 says:

“Wedan and Javan traded your goods from Uzal for wrought iron, cassia, and sweet cane.”

In Genesis, meanwhile, Uzal appears in the genealogical lists as one of the sons of Joktan son of Eber.

There are also Arab and biblical traditions that confuse the name of an Assyrian king with the name of his capital. In religious accounts of the conflict between the Prophet Abraham and Nimrod, Islamic traditions say that Abraham fought Nimrod. In the Bible, however, Nimrod appears in one place as the name of a king and elsewhere as the name of a city.

We know Nimrud as the great capital of the Assyrian Empire.

The ambiguity surrounding the name Dhamar should therefore be understood within a much older cultural tradition in Yemen, where the same name could refer simultaneously to a city, a king, and a tribe.

4.

If we assume that Dhamar refers to a city, we immediately encounter a problem in interpreting the refrain of the reported inscription:

“Who ruled Dhamar?”

Does the following line—

“Who ruled Dhamar? Quraysh the merchants”

—mean that Quraysh conquered the city of Dhamar?

That does not seem logical or historically acceptable.

We therefore need another reading, one in which Dhamar refers to the well-known tribe of Damar or Dhamar.

5.

Sanaʿa is not an Abyssinian word, despite Yaqut’s claim.

If we accept Ibn Duraid’s explanation, we would also have to accept—without adequate evidence—that the name Sanaʿa appeared only in AD 525, when the Abyssinians invaded Yemen, occupied ancient Dhamar, and supposedly renamed it Sanaʿa.

Early Arab linguists routinely tried to derive every word from an Arabic root, even when the word was foreign. When they failed, they often declared it to be foreign.

In the case of Sanaʿa, an attempt to derive the name from the three-letter Arabic root ṣ-n-ʿ may have proved unconvincing, leading Ibn Duraid to suppose that the word was Abyssinian.

Yet the name can instead be related to the ancient Himyaritic word maṣnaʿa, referring to the water reservoirs for which the ancient Yemenis were famous.

This is also the significance, in my view, of the statement in Ezekiel 27:13, 29:

“They traded your goods from Uzal for wrought iron.”

The word maṣnaʿa appears explicitly in the Bayt Dabaʿan inscription. In essence, the inscription says:

“This is the inscription of Sharjaʿathat, son of al-Dharanahi, of Banu Dharanah, lords of the palace Ahram, and the leaders of the tribe of Dhamar, the Murabiʿin of the Qashm confederation. He declares that he built and founded, restored, renewed, completed, and adorned their maṣnaʿa called Taʿrman, with all its houses, temples, wall, and two cisterns.”

The occurrence of maṣnaʿa, together with the reference to its cisterns, undermines Ibn Duraid’s explanation.

More importantly, the Bayt Dabaʿan inscription may settle the problem of Dhamar in favour of an entirely different interpretation.

The ancient city built and inhabited by tribes was a product of the culture of its inhabitants. They did more than give it their name. At every stage of their collective life, they absorbed the city into their inherited culture and legendary history.

The city could therefore become the tribe, while the tribe could appear in several forms as the owner—and even the master—of the city and its history.

The Bayt Dabaʿan inscription’s reference to the leaders of the tribe of Dhamar strongly supports the view that Dhamar was indeed a tribal name.

Yet the problem does not end there, because the Musnad script does not always record certain consonants appearing within a word. It remains possible that Dhamar was an ancient name covering both the city and the neighbouring population around it (Yemeni Studies, 18, 1984).

Once this is placed within the framework of old cultural traditions, writings, and myths, the fusion of tribe and city into a single name becomes understandable.

The city signifies the tribe, and the tribe signifies the city.

We find the same pattern in the Bible: Nimrod the king and Nimrod the city; Uzal the ancestral father of the Qahtanites—if we follow the claim that Qahtan corresponds to the biblical Joktan—and Uzal the ancient city later known as Sanaʿa.

What, then, does the text on the “stone” supposedly found by Quraysh beneath the Kaaba actually tell us?

And what precisely do the names mean when arranged, with their accompanying descriptions, in the following order?

“Rule of Dhamar; the noble Himyarites; the wicked Abyssinians; the free Persians; Quraysh the merchants.”

1.

As we have already seen from Mutahhar Ali al-Iryani’s reading of the Dabaʿan inscription (Yemeni Studies, 18, 1984), and as we shall also see from Glaser inscription GL 1228, the name Damar/Dhamar genuinely seems to carry a double meaning.

It can refer to the tribe and to the city, and it may also have been the name of a legendary king.

The best way to understand this range of meanings, in my view, is to relate it to an old cultural convention in which the king’s name could easily be used to refer to the tribe, and the tribe’s name could likewise stand for its ruler.

The same interchange appears frequently in later Arab and Muslim writing.

When early chroniclers such as al-Azraqi in Reports of Mecca and al-Fakihi in Reports of Mecca in Ancient and Recent Times say that Dubba or Mudar “ruled” Mecca and the Hijaz, this does not mean that the individual ruler was actually named Dubba or Mudar. It means that the tribal branch known by that name had imposed its authority over Mecca and the Hijaz.

We have already seen how this old cultural convention, with its clearly Yemeni roots, also appears strongly in the biblical text.

Uzal can refer to a legendary ancestral father as well as to the city which al-Hamdani identifies as ancient Sanaʿa.

It is significant that the Bible mentions Uzal alongside Hadramawt. Hadramawt itself appears sometimes as the name of a city, at other times as the name of a son descended from Joktan son of Eber, and elsewhere as the name of a small state.

The same principle applies to the Assyrian name Nimrod, which Genesis uses once for a mighty king and elsewhere for a great city.

Damar, in this sense, represents the tribe-city known among the ancient communities of south-western Arabia, comparable in some respects to the city-states of Mesopotamia: a tribe builds its own city and comes to be known by its name.

2.

According to the order of names and descriptions in the reported Dhamar inscription, authority over this tribe-city then passed to the “noble” Himyarites.

These were, in my view, none other than the central Himyarite state, which subdued the various Yemeni communities, tribes, and cities by force and brought them together within a centralised state around 1200 BC.

The reference reveals the narrator’s careful interest in historical sequence. The history of the tribe-city is presented as an ancient inheritance whose roots extend into a remote and sacred past.

3.

Immediately after the Himyarites, the inscription says that Damar fell under the rule of the “wicked Abyssinians.”

Historically, this sequence is correct and logical: the tribe-city fell into the hands of the Abyssinian occupiers around AD 525.

Although the writer leaps over enormous stretches of history and many major events, he compresses the sequence into three major turning points.

4.

As we shall see, these were the fall of the tribe-city to the Abyssinians, its subsequent liberation and subjection to Persian rule, and finally its alleged submission to Quraysh.

The memory of Yemen’s liberation from Abyssinian rule remained alive in Yemeni consciousness for a very long time, surviving partly through the group later known as the Abnaʾ, the “sons” or descendants of the Persians.

The inscription describes Persia as “the free Persians.”

In reality, the Persians played a direct and decisive part in liberating the Yemenis from the Christian Abyssinian occupation in AD 575, after roughly half a century in which the Yemenis had endured severe hardship.

Arab chroniclers describe this “war of liberation” in accounts preserved, among other places, in al-Masʿudi’s Meadows of Gold.

According to these reports, the Yemenis made repeated attempts to persuade Rome to abandon its Abyssinian allies in Yemen and support the local groups that were willing to protect Roman interests.

Several traditions suggest that these Yemeni groups hoped for a form of government resembling, at least outwardly, the position of the Ghassanid principalities in Syria: they would act as political and military agents of Roman interests in Arabia.

Al-Masʿudi says that Sayf ibn Dhi Yazan, the powerful tribal leader, personally travelled to Rome:

“Sayf ibn Dhi Yazan crossed the sea and went to Caesar seeking his help. He remained at his gate for seven years, but Caesar refused to assist him, saying: ‘You are Jews and the Abyssinians are Christians. Religion does not permit us to support one who differs from us against one who agrees with us.’”

All the early chroniclers tell some version of this story.

It points to the difficulties faced by Sayf ibn Dhi Yazan’s revolt against the Abyssinian occupation and to the complicated conditions under which the resistance operated.

After returning disappointed from Rome, Sayf quickly sought help from the Persians, apparently making contact with them during his return journey through Syria.

5.

A Yemeni-Persian alliance was theoretically possible at the time, although the Persian court was dealing with serious religious unrest arising from its struggle against the Mazdakite movement.

Even so, the powerful Persian king Anushirvan, who ruled from AD 531 to 579, listened to Sayf ibn Dhi Yazan’s request for military assistance and for a campaign to expel the Abyssinians from Yemen.

A close reading of pre-Islamic and Islamic Arabic poetry concerning this “war of liberation”—including al-Buhturi’s celebrated Siniyya and poems by Jarir—would, I believe, reveal the hidden religious dimension of this alliance.

The Yemenis repeatedly imagined that they and the Persians shared a common ancestral father, Abraham, and a sacred mother, Sarah.

This belief may sound strange unless placed in what I consider its proper historical setting.

Around 539 BC, Persia had seen Judaism spread at court, while several biblical traditions revolved around the marriage of the Persian king to the Jewish heroine Esther.

Within this framework, Jewish Yemen could remember a religious connection with Persia while seeking Persian military support against the Christian Abyssinians.

Jarir expresses this idea in a verse quoted by Ibn Sallam in Classes of the Poets:

A father unites us with the noble sons of Sarah; after him, we care little who denies our kinship.

Jarir composed these lines in praise of the Persians while also praising the Yemeni Qays.

Ibn Sallam relates that when Jarir entered the mosque of Damascus—the Umayyad Mosque—the Yemeni tribes hurried to greet him and cheered while repeating his poem in praise of the Persians.

This marked a striking revival of collective Yemeni memory long after the war of liberation itself.

The belief that Yemenis and Persians were linked through Judaism seems to have been widespread in the early chronicles.

Al-Tabari even makes the Persian Manuchihr a direct descendant of “Afrīqis son of Isaac son of Abraham, to whom the kingdom passed after Afridun.”

Muslim chroniclers, following al-Tabari, cited Jarir’s verses as evidence that the Persians were descendants of Sarah.

Al-Buhturi, meanwhile, expressed sorrow in his famous Siniyya over the fall of the Persian state while acknowledging the great role Persia had played in Yemen’s liberation:

They upheld our kingdom and strengthened its power, fierce warriors beneath coats of mail; they helped us against the battalions of Arbat, striking and thrusting at throats.

6.

Muslim writers disagreed widely over why Persia came to be called “the free.”

As usual, linguists offered several explanations.

Al-Isfahani says in Kitab al-Aghani that the Persians were called “the free” because they were free from the brown complexion of the Arabs, the fair or reddish colouring of the Romans, and the black complexion of the Abyssinians. Anything unmixed or pure, he says, is “free.”

Al-Suhayli offers a different explanation in al-Rawd al-Unuf:

“They called Persia ‘the free’ because kingship among them had been hereditary from the beginning of the world, from the age of Gayumarth, whom the Persians identify with Adam.”

The correct explanation, in my view, is that the Yemenis began calling the Persians “the free” after the liberation of AD 575.

7.

There can be little doubt that Persia came to Yemen’s aid and committed part of its military strength to the struggle against Christian Rome’s influence in the region.

Its motives were political and cultural and overlapped in several ways. Yet Persia also proceeded cautiously because of the sensitivity of its internal situation.

Anushirvan therefore devised a remarkable plan.

He ordered the prisons opened and selected hardened criminals, forming them into a mercenary army that was trained in haste. He then placed a small fleet at their disposal and sent it down the Tigris towards the Persian Gulf.

Al-Masʿudi’s version suggests that these mercenaries came from prisoners serving sentences in Persian-controlled Iraq, in the region of what is now Khanaqin.

Anushirvan appointed Wahriz, governor of Daylam, commander of the army and fleet, telling him:

“If they conquer, we shall say so; and if they perish, that too is what I wanted. Either outcome is a victory.”

According to al-Masʿudi, the fleet travelled down the Tigris until it reached Basra:

“It was then an opening onto the sea, and at that time there was neither Basra nor Kufa. [...] They boarded the sea-going ships and sailed until they reached the coast of Hadramawt, at a place called Mathubash. They disembarked, though some had already died at sea. Wahriz then ordered them to burn the ships so that they would know death was before them and there was nowhere to which they could retreat, forcing them to exert themselves to the utmost.”

This, in my view, is the distant origin of the legend that the Muslim commander Tariq ibn Ziyad burned his ships.

The later Islamic account must have arisen when Muslim Yemeni narrators, still remembering the Persians as the force that had once liberated Yemen, blended images from that earlier war of liberation into their collective memory of Tariq ibn Ziyad’s campaign.

The result was that Tariq’s expedition came to resemble a heroic continuation of an earlier struggle: liberators sacrificing everything for a noble purpose.

The campaign of Tariq ibn Ziyad therefore appears, in part, as a later version of a “war of liberation” that had once taken place in Yemen and now occurred again elsewhere.

In both cases, there was a Yemeni fighter dreaming of liberation.

If so, we would have to suppose that these narrators were drawing on Yemeni soldiers who had participated in Tariq ibn Ziyad’s expedition.

Through Islam and its wars to liberate pagan peoples, the Yemeni fighter recovered the memory of an older war in which ships had been burned off the coast of Hadramawt.

Now, speaking to enthusiastic Muslims, he told the story of a new victory in North Africa, and the motif of the burning ships entered a new narrative about a new kind of liberation.

8.

The defeat of the Abyssinians opened the way for Persia to establish direct control over Yemen.

Earlier Persian influence had been mainly political and cultural. What mattered most in this later period, however, was how quickly the “liberators” became integrated into the local population.

Intermarriage accelerated, producing a new generation of Yemeni rulers who came to be known as the Abnaʾ.

Islamic traditions preserve several stories about Himyarite women marrying Persians and Persian women marrying Yemenis.

One famous account even explains the beauty of the legendary Yemeni poet Waddah al-Yaman by claiming that he inherited it from his Persian mother.

9.

After “the free Persians,” the writer of the inscription places “Quraysh the merchants.”

This supposedly marks the submission of Damar, the tribe-city, to northern Adnanite influence.

Yet although I have doubts about the text itself, those doubts are separate from the question of whether its broad historical sequence has meaning.

We cannot reasonably accept that Dhamar, the tribe-city, literally came under the rule of Quraysh.

The reported inscription may instead reveal the circumstances in which its surviving form was written.

In my view, that form belongs to the early Islamic period.

In other words, the original inscription is unknown to us. What we possess is a reported text, written or reshaped by enthusiastic Muslims who inserted Quraysh into an account of the ancient history of Damar or Dhamar.

Even if we assume that the surviving version was fabricated from an older original, however, studying it remains useful because it may reveal some of the mechanisms through which ancient Arab history became mythologised.

If the insertion of Quraysh into the reported Dhamar inscription is a late addition, it still tells us something important.

Its purpose was to elevate Quraysh in the age of prophecy, in keeping with Islamic traditions that saw the triumph of Islam, in at least some respects, as an Adnanite triumph.

The poetic and prose contests between Yemeni tribes and the northern Nizari or Adnanite tribes at the Umayyad and later Abbasid courts reveal the same tendency.

10.

There is another important possibility.

The reference to “Quraysh the merchants” may itself preserve valuable historical information, if we assume that the phrase is genuine rather than invented.

Its importance lies in the way it describes Quraysh specifically as merchants within a broader historical setting dominated by political power and warfare.

Quraysh’s authority in Mecca before Islam, and its administration of the Sacred House, continued an earlier period of rule by Mudar. Both belonged to the wider northern Adnanite tradition.

Quraysh’s position came after Khuzaʿa had been displaced from guardianship of the Sacred House.

Within this framework, inserting the name Quraysh may express a desire to establish an ancient, almost timeless existence for the tribe, projecting it far back into periods long before it actually emerged as a recognisable historical force.

In fact, Quraysh was not known by this unusual and much-debated name until the age of Hashim ibn Qusayy, the Prophet’s ancestor.

Hashim followed the path begun by his father Qusayy and developed an impressive diplomatic programme aimed at securing commercial caravan routes between Mecca and Syria and between Mecca and Yemen.

These security agreements with the Ghassanid and Himyarite rulers became known as the ilaf, and date, in the author’s view, to around AD 450.

A whole series of linguistic and social explanations has been offered for the name Quraysh.

What matters here is that they point towards one important fact.

In Qusayy’s time, the group later called Quraysh was known as the Banu al-Nadr ibn Kinana.

Qusayy defeated Khuzaʿa, drove them out of Mecca and the sanctuary, and gathered together various branches of Kinana.

According to the tradition, these groups acquired the name Quraysh after a severe famine struck Mecca and Qusayy fed the starving tribes who had gathered in the valley.

History and myth are clearly mixed in this account.

Even so, its basic message is plain: the tribe called Quraysh did not emerge under that name until around AD 400. Before then, it was known by the larger tribal branch from which it came, Banu Kinana.

The reported Dhamar inscription therefore contains history, but it speaks in a halting voice because its language is the language of myth rather than the language of history.

Previous5. The Fabrication of Yemen’s History and the Greco-Persian WarsNext7. The Armour of al-Nuʿman or the Armour of Imruʾ al-Qays?

7. The Armour of al-Nuʿman or the Armour of Imruʾ al-Qays?

The Dispute over a Dead Hero’s Weapons

Who was al-Samawʾal?

Did the famous story known in early Arabic literary tradition as “The Faithfulness of al-Samawʾal” really happen?

And is the old Arab proverb “More faithful than al-Samawʾal” actually connected with this story?

What, moreover, does any of this have to do with the legend of the fortress of al-Ablaq at Tayma, where al-Samawʾal’s son was supposedly killed beneath its walls?

And who was it that demanded the return of the weapons entrusted to al-Samawʾal?

Was it al-Harith ibn Abi Shamir al-Ghassani, one of the Ghassanid rulers of Syria?

Or was it al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ al-Lakhmi, ruler of al-Hira in Iraq?

A long chain of historical reports preserves this legend. Taken together, they are deeply confused accounts in which names and places have become badly mixed. At their centre is the claim that Imruʾ al-Qays made a journey to the Byzantine capital to seek military help from the emperor and recover his stolen throne—a journey for which there is no historical basis. On the way, we are told, he passed the fortress of the Jewish poet al-Samawʾal.23

The traditional account runs as follows:

When Imruʾ al-Qays died, al-Harith ibn Abi Shamir al-Ghassani went to al-Samawʾal ibn ʿAdiya and demanded the armour of Imruʾ al-Qays, which numbered one hundred pieces, together with the property he had left in al-Samawʾal’s care. Al-Samawʾal refused to hand it over. Al-Harith then seized one of his sons and said: “Either you surrender the armour, or I will kill your son.” Al-Samawʾal still refused to surrender anything, so al-Harith killed him.

That, in its simplest form, is the traditional account of the legendary poet al-Samawʾal, who was said to have lived in the fortress of al-Ablaq at Tayma and whose loyalty became proverbial among the Arabs. After the death of his son, the following lines were attributed to him:

I kept faith over the Kindite’s armour; when others are blamed for betrayal, I keep my word.

And ʿAdiya once charged me: “Samawʾal, do not tear down what you have built.”

To understand this account properly, we need to separate it into two distinct parts: the historical report and the myth. In other words, we must break the old narrative into two independent units, each of which deals, according to the conditions in which it was formed, with the same cluster of material: the war at Tayma, the journey of Imruʾ al-Qays, his death, and finally the killing of al-Samawʾal’s son.

What we have here is a familiar type of traditional report, one we have encountered before, in which myth has been narrated as part of history. A serious analysis therefore requires two steps.

First, we must extract the mythical element from the historical narrative, define it clearly as the mythic component of the story, and examine it independently as myth.

Second, we must separate what is historical from what is mythical so that the historical material can be studied on its own. Put another way, we need to reconstruct the historical account behind the story of the armour of Imruʾ al-Qays.

The earliest precise reference to the supposed events of this story connects the death of al-Samawʾal’s son with the poetry of al-Aʿsha. According to the tradition, al-Aʿsha—the celebrated Sannajat al-ʿArab, or great singer of the Arabs—was the original transmitter of the story, which he supposedly turned into a poem of twenty-one lines in his Diwan.

This reminds us again that there may be something significant behind the Arabs’ description of al-Aʿsha as their great singer: he was made into the poet who recorded their heroic tales in verse, in a manner inspired by Homer.

In one poem attributed to him—though it may itself have been composed by later Muslim transmitters—al-Aʿsha says:

Be like al-Samawʾal when the mighty warrior closed around him with an army rolling forward, black as night.

The poem continues:

When he forced upon him two humiliating choices, he answered: “Say what you will. I am listening.”

Another transmitted version gives:

The protection of Ibn Hayya, for whoever comes under his pledge, is more faithful and more secure than the protection of Ibn ʿAmmar.

A number of Arabic and Islamic sources also say that al-Aʿsha once visited a man named Shurayh ibn Hisn ibn ʿImran ibn al-Samawʾal and praised him in a poem rhyming in rāʾ. In it, he mentions two sons of al-Samawʾal, Hawt and Mundhir.

Yet neither the Arab chroniclers nor al-Aʿsha himself ever gives the name of the son who was supposedly killed. Some reports claim that the killer was al-Harith ibn Zalim.

There is also a story that al-Aʿsha was once captured by a man from the tribe of Kalb whom he had viciously satirized. The Kalbite brought him back to his tent, where Shurayh, the grandson of al-Samawʾal, happened to be staying as a guest. Proud of his captives, the man paraded them before Shurayh.

When al-Aʿsha passed in front of him, he recited his poem. Shurayh then asked the Kalbite to give him the prisoner as a gift. The man agreed, and Shurayh set al-Aʿsha free, telling him:

“Stay with me until I can honour you properly.”

Al-Aʿsha instead asked for a camel, left Tayma, and afterwards spread his poem about al-Samawʾal.

Alongside al-Aʿsha, who supposedly preserved the legend in verse, there is another transmitter: Darim ibn ʿAqal ibn Muzaqiya. Abu al-Faraj al-Isfahani rejected the genealogy attributed to him, writing:

“This is impossible, because al-Aʿsha lived to meet Shurayh ibn al-Samawʾal and also lived into the Islamic period, whereas ʿAmr Muzaqiya belonged to a much earlier age.”

Ibn Durayd, meanwhile, makes al-Samawʾal a descendant of Ghassan, as does Ibn Habib, author of al-Muhabbar. Thaʿlab, in his commentary on the Diwan of al-Aʿsha, gives Shurayh’s ancestry as:

Shurayh ibn Hisn ibn ʿImran ibn al-Samawʾal ibn Hayya ibn ʿAdiya.

Early Arab scholars were plainly unsure how even to record al-Samawʾal’s name and genealogy. Among the forms they give are:

al-Samawʾal ibn Gharid ibn ʿAdiya, in al-Aghani and Ibn Sallam’s Tabaqat.

al-Samawʾal ibn Hayyan ibn ʿAdiya, in al-Maydani’s Book of Proverbs.

al-Samawʾal ibn Hayya ibn ʿAdiya ibn Rifaʿa ibn al-Haritha ibn Thaʿlaba ibn Kaʿb, in Ibn Durayd’s al-Ishtiqaq.

Other sources call him al-Samawʾal ibn Awfa ibn ʿAdiya, or even derive him from Harun ibn ʿArran.

Taken together, the old accounts, the poem of al-Aʿsha, and the report of Darim suggest that al-Aʿsha was the first person to tell the story of al-Samawʾal’s loyalty and the armour. He is also said to have been the only poet who regularly visited al-Samawʾal’s fortress of al-Ablaq.

For the purposes of analysis, then, we may reasonably suppose that the source of the legend now known as “The Faithfulness of al-Samawʾal” lies in the poetry attributed to al-Aʿsha, which preserved the story and gave it lasting form.

We will therefore examine al-Aʿsha’s poetic version in some detail and compare it with the other historical reports, which greatly increased the confusion surrounding the names and places involved.

What is especially striking in al-Aʿsha’s version is that he attributes al-Samawʾal’s palace or fortress of al-Ablaq to Solomon son of David, as in the famous line:

Solomon son of David built it in an age long past, with a high vaulted roof and strongly bonded masonry.

If this poem really belongs to al-Aʿsha, like the rest of the poetry attributed to him, and if later transmitters did not alter the original text or fabricate these lines—much of the original poem may in fact have been lost—then the word “age” (huqba) at the end of the first half-line is exceptionally important.

It clearly presents the fortress as a monument of remote antiquity, an ancient relic of the Arabs.

If so, al-Samawʾal can hardly have had any direct connection with its construction. Solomon is traditionally dated to around 922 BCE, whereas al-Samawʾal supposedly lived around 550 CE—a gap of almost 1,450 years. It is hardly reasonable to imagine the fortress remaining an ordinary inhabited residence across such a span.

The line should therefore be understood as a conventional way of expressing extreme antiquity by linking the building to Solomon. I discussed this phenomenon and comparable cases in Iram of the Pillars (Riad El-Rayyes Books, 2000). It belongs to a pattern found across ancient cultures, where great cities and monumental buildings are attributed to gods or legendary rulers.

Yet al-Aʿsha’s decision to push the existence of al-Ablaq back to the age of Solomon also undermines the very foundation of his own story about al-Samawʾal’s loyalty. How plausible is it that al-Samawʾal lived in a monument of such fantastic antiquity and used it to hide the weapons of Imruʾ al-Qays?

But who, in the first place, was demanding those weapons?

The sources disagree sharply over the identity of the prince who ordered al-Samawʾal to return them.

In some versions it is al-Harith ibn Abi Shamir al-Ghassani, one of the Ghassanid princes of Syria.

In others it is al-Harith ibn Zalim, sent by al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ, king of al-Hira, on a military campaign to subdue tribes around Tayma. In this version, al-Harith kills one of al-Samawʾal’s sons.

We should also remember that the Ghassanid al-Harith killed al-Mundhir, king of al-Hira, at the famous battle of ʿAyn Abagh in 554 CE, on one of the celebrated battle-days of the Arabs known as the Day of Halima.

Muslim chroniclers concluded that the killer of al-Samawʾal’s son was al-Harith ibn Zalim, commander of al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ’s army, partly on the basis of a line in which Jarir satirized al-Farazdaq:

With Abu Raghwan’s sword, the sword of Mujashiʿ, you struck; you did not strike with the sword of Ibn Zalim.

At this point, a proposal made by Theodor Nöldeke may help us understand the tradition more clearly. In his Princes of Ghassan, Nöldeke suggests that, if the killing of al-Samawʾal’s son really happened, it would have taken place around 550 CE, only about four years before the death of al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ, king of al-Hira.

The difficulty with this hypothesis is that it turns a mythical event into a historical one.

To examine Nöldeke’s proposal more carefully, we need to consider an important account found throughout the early sources and also recorded by Ibn al-Athir in al-Kamil fi al-Tarikh.

Ibn al-Athir writes that the Persian king Khosrow became angry with al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir (585–613 CE) after one of al-Nuʿman’s enemies turned him against him. Khosrow summoned al-Nuʿman to his court.

Al-Nuʿman immediately sought protection among the tribes of the desert, but the advice he received destroyed his remaining hopes: he should go and surrender himself to the Persian king.

Ibn al-Athir writes:

When the letter—the letter of Khosrow—arrived, al-Nuʿman took his weapons and went to the tribes, especially Tayy, asking them to protect him and come to his aid. They refused, fearing Khosrow. None of the Arabs would receive him until he secretly reached Dhu Qar among Banu Shayban. There he met Haniʾ ibn Masʿud ibn ʿAmr al-Shaybani, a powerful tribal chief. Al-Nuʿman knew that Haniʾ would protect his family and his wealth. Among his possessions were four hundred coats of armour, though some say eight hundred.

Al-Nuʿman then went to Khosrow, who put him in chains and sent him to Khanaqin (...) although people believed that he died at Sabat. Al-Aʿsha spent the night saying:

That was the man whom his Lord saved from death at Sabat, until at last he died, bound fast.24
