Khosrow then sent Iyas ibn Qabisa al-Taʾi—whom he would later appoint king of al-Hira as a reward for the position taken by his tribe, which had refused to aid al-Nuʿman—to Dhu Qar.

Iyas was to meet Haniʾ ibn Masʿud and demand that he hand over al-Nuʿman’s weapons.

Haniʾ refused to surrender what had been entrusted to him.

Ibn al-Athir continues:

When Haniʾ refused, Khosrow became angry (...) and sent Iyas ibn Qabisa al-Taʾi as commander of the army, accompanied by the Persian marzbans. The Prophet, peace be upon him, had already been sent by then. Haniʾ distributed al-Nuʿman’s coats of armour and weapons among the tribes.

In this way war broke out between the Persians and the Arabs. It ended at the famous Battle of Dhu Qar, where the Arabs won a brilliant victory over the Persians on one of the most celebrated battle-days in Arab memory.

But did this war really break out because of al-Nuʿman’s armour?

Ibn al-Athir’s account, repeated in most Arabic and Islamic sources, clearly preserves a historical event involving the weapons of a dead hero. He had left those weapons in the care of a tribal chief, and a Persian king later demanded their return.

That event is remarkably close to the other, legendary story in which the armour of Imruʾ al-Qays is demanded after his death.

If so, the historical event occurred in 613 CE, while the corresponding legendary event is placed in 554 CE. The two are separated by roughly fifty-nine years.

This suggests that, during the great age of written compilation, Muslim transmitters confused two different stories. One concerned a campaign by a king of al-Hira against tribes at Tayma. The other concerned another king of al-Hira and a conflict with Banu Shayban at Dhu Qar.

The Arab chroniclers may therefore have merged names, places, and images in stages. The legendary Jewish poet al-Samawʾal took the place of the historical Haniʾ ibn Masʿud al-Shaybani, while Imruʾ al-Qays took the place of al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir.

This can be set out more clearly.

1.

The story of the killing of al-Samawʾal’s son and his famous act of loyalty revolves around al-Harith ibn Abi Shamir al-Ghassani demanding the weapons of Imruʾ al-Qays. This is the version found in al-Isfahani, Ibn Qutayba, al-Yaʿqubi, al-Tabari, Ibn al-Athir, and others.

Yet there is no obvious connection between this Ghassanid prince and Imruʾ al-Qays.

If the Imruʾ al-Qays in question is the poet and son of the murdered king Hujr, then he belonged to the Kindite kingdom in Najd, or to the political world of al-Hira, in the age of al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ. There is therefore no convincing reason to imagine a Ghassanid prince in Syria demanding the weapons of a Kindite prince being pursued by the king of al-Hira in Iraq.

The insertion of al-Harith ibn Abi Shamir into this story makes very little sense.

Al-Harith himself was a historical figure and one of the greatest rulers of Ghassan. His true name was al-Harith ibn Jabala. “Ibn Abi Shamir” came from a familiar epithet attached to his father, as Nöldeke perceptively showed.

It therefore seems likely that his name entered the story through confusion, and that the person originally intended was al-Harith ibn Zalim.

Once that correction is made, the account becomes far more plausible. Al-Harith ibn Zalim was one of the most distinguished military commanders serving al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ in al-Hira, and he led al-Mundhir’s campaigns in the region of Tayma.

Arab chroniclers appear to have confused al-Harith the Ghassanid with al-Harith ibn Zalim, leaving the resulting story difficult to believe.

2.

Al-Harith ibn Zalim was also a historical figure. He became proverbial for his fame, severity, and decisiveness.

That reputation may itself explain why his name was inserted into another story, where he replaced yet another man: Iyas ibn Qabisa al-Taʾi, the commander who led the campaign against Banu Shayban in 613 CE, supposedly to recover the armour of al-Nuʿman.

When people retell stories and forget the original names, they often replace them with famous ones. A familiar name gives the storyteller greater authority and gives the listener greater pleasure in believing the tale.

3.

The presence of al-Samawʾal ibn ʿAdiya at the fortress of al-Ablaq in Tayma must be treated separately from the story of Imruʾ al-Qays travelling to Constantinople to seek the emperor’s help.

According to that story, Imruʾ al-Qays deposited his weapons with the Jewish poet before continuing his journey.

The better historical setting is quite different.

We should instead look to the campaign of al-Harith ibn Zalim, the distinguished commander of al-Hira, against Tayma. Its purpose was to subdue and punish certain tribes as part of the ambitious policy of al-Hira under its powerful king al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ, who was attempting to extend his authority across the whole region.

Such a campaign had nothing to do with recovering one hundred coats of armour—or even four hundred, as Ibn al-Athir would have it.

The Arab chroniclers misunderstood the meaning of al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ’s campaigns against the tribes of Arabia and the Euphrates, which continued throughout the half-century of his rule, from 505 to 554 CE, when he was killed by his rival al-Harith ibn Jabala al-Ghassani.

Evidence for al-Mundhir’s campaigns and for his growing political and military influence in western Arabia from around 516 CE comes from a famous inscription discovered by archaeologists at Wadi Maʾsal al-Jumh, on the road between Mecca and Riyadh. It is commonly associated with the archaeologists Ryckmans and Cassel and refers to the “raids of al-Mundhir.”

All of this suggests that the campaign against Tayma led by al-Harith ibn Zalim—during which a son of al-Samawʾal was supposedly killed as part of a sordid attempt to recover the armour of Imruʾ al-Qays—was effectively pushed aside in Arabic-Islamic historical writing and replaced by a moralizing legend centred on loyalty.

Yet this mythic element is an organic part of the tradition, and it offers a particularly clear example of the way storytellers merge different images, materials, and narrative elements.

4.

As we have already seen, the genealogy of al-Samawʾal is hopelessly confused. It is difficult on this basis to accept him confidently as a historical person, and there is no firm evidence for his existence in this period.

Suppose al-Aʿsha did happen to meet someone who claimed descent from al-Samawʾal and took this as proof that al-Samawʾal had really existed. Or suppose he genuinely met one of his direct descendants and praised him after being released from captivity among Kalb.

Even then, the genealogy recorded for this supposed grandson in the sources does not establish that al-Samawʾal was his immediate grandfather. At most, it places him as a remote ancestor in a long genealogical chain.

He may therefore have been a legendary founding ancestor.

Margoliouth questioned both the historical reality of al-Samawʾal and the poetry attributed to him, regarding the poems as wholly fabricated and falsely ascribed.

Nöldeke, for his part, connected al-Samawʾal with the biblical Samuel and suggested that the supposed al-Samawʾal ibn ʿAdiya, presented as one of the leading men of Tayma, may preserve an echo of traditions about the Jewish prophet Samuel.

There were, however, tribes or groups at Tayma known by a name resembling Samuel. Some scholars proposed that this name might correspond to Ishmael. Van der Werden, in his History of Thamud, mentioned the possibility but, rightly in my view, declined to identify Samuel with Ishmael.

Extreme caution is required here if we are to avoid arbitrary identifications.

The transmitter of the legend of al-Samawʾal’s faithfulness, Darim ibn ʿAqal, is himself said in some remarkably suggestive reports to have been descended from al-Samawʾal:

“He was of the offspring of al-Samawʾal.”

If taken seriously, this would mean that the storyteller was narrating a legend about one of his own ancestors.

That is, of course, precisely the implication behind this sudden genealogical connection: the hero of the myth and the man telling it are turned into members of the same family.

Yet al-Isfahani, in al-Aghani, attacks Darim and questions his literary honesty in a context that matters greatly here because it concerns Imruʾ al-Qays—the man Darim made into a tragic hero in his story about al-Samawʾal.

Al-Isfahani discusses a poem attributed to Imruʾ al-Qays beginning:

Hind came to you after a long estrangement, late at night, though she had never come before.

He declares the poem spurious and suggests that Darim himself may have composed it.

Such an accusation, coming from one of the great historians of early Arabic poetry, inevitably damages the credibility of the story Darim transmitted. That account spread through Arabic and Islamic literature alongside the heroic poetry attributed to al-Aʿsha.

It also adds another layer of doubt to the already confused traditions surrounding Imruʾ al-Qays.

Darim ibn ʿAqal was himself of Jewish origin before becoming Muslim. His telling of the legend openly reflects that background. It is therefore especially interesting that some of its central elements—above all the idea of preserving a sacred trust—resemble the idealized image of the biblical prophet Samuel.

This resemblance encouraged Hugo Winckler, following Nöldeke and Margoliouth, to repeat the claim that al-Samawʾal of Tayma was essentially a reflection of the biblical Samuel.

Tempting though this identification is, and despite its considerable literary appeal, I do not believe there is enough evidence to treat the hurried and largely superficial comparison proposed by Nöldeke, Margoliouth, and Winckler as a magical key to the mystery of al-Samawʾal.

At the same time, one supposed descendant of this legendary Jewish poet has been openly accused of literary dishonesty and of forging poetry attributed to Imruʾ al-Qays—the very figure who stands at the centre of the story about the deposited weapons.

This makes it necessary to examine the collection attributed to al-Samawʾal, usually known as the Diwan of al-Samawʾal, as part of our analysis of the whole tradition.

Arabic and Islamic literary sources describe this collection as having been transmitted by Niftawayh, who died in 323 AH.

Yet Ibn al-Nadim, in al-Fihrist, never mentions such a Diwan in his list of Niftawayh’s works. A quick examination of al-Aghani also shows, surprisingly, that al-Isfahani pays no attention whatsoever to Niftawayh’s supposed transmission of al-Samawʾal’s poetry.

That silence is difficult to explain if such a transmission really existed.

It raises the possibility that there never was such a recension in the first place and that it was another invention associated with Darim ibn ʿAqal.

What do these facts suggest?

The failure of both Ibn al-Nadim and al-Isfahani even to mention a Diwan of al-Samawʾal transmitted by Niftawayh brings us back to the doubts surrounding Darim ibn ʿAqal’s literary honesty.

From here, several elements of the tradition need to be separated completely.

1.

The story in which a hero deposits his weapons with a lord, ruler, king, tribal chief, prince, or protector before setting out for a war in which death awaits him has deep roots.

In one form it is an old Arab story told by tribes of south-western Arabia about Luqman. A chief of Banu Karkar asks Luqman to keep their weapons inside the cave where he worships. Versions of this appear in Wahb ibn Munabbih’s al-Tijan and in al-Andalusi’s Nashwat al-Tarab.

But the motif also preserves traces of a Greek legend from the Trojan cycle. It may have entered and merged with Arab tribal traditions during the age of the Greco-Persian wars, a heroic age that fired the Arab imagination for generations.

In the legend of Odysseus, a bitter conflict breaks out between Odysseus and Ajax over the weapons of the dead hero Achilles.

Achilles had gone unwillingly to war after first hiding from it at the court of King Lycomedes on Skyros, in disguise. After Achilles was killed, a fierce dispute erupted between Odysseus and Ajax over which of them had the right to inherit the fallen hero’s arms.

2.

We should notice how the Arabic accounts portray al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir, in Ibn al-Athir’s version for example, as secretly going to Banu Shayban after receiving Khosrow’s summons.

The same narrative image is used for Imruʾ al-Qays, who supposedly moved from tribe to tribe asking them to hide him from al-Harith ibn Zalim, or from al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ.

Al-Nuʿman is therefore pictured as taking refuge from Khosrow’s threat in the protection of the chief of Shayban, just as Imruʾ al-Qays is pictured hiding inside the fortress of al-Ablaq before leaving for Constantinople.

The tribal encampment has effectively taken the place of the court of King Lycomedes.

This parallel strongly suggests a Greek mythic element within the story of the deposited weapons.

3.

In the Odysseus legend, war follows the death of Achilles.

The struggle centres on the inheritance of the dead hero—his weapons.

The same pattern appears in the Arab historical traditions, where chroniclers imagined the chief cause of war to have been a dispute over armour even when the real conflict arose from much deeper political causes.

Turning a war into a struggle over the inheritance of a dead hero belongs to an old Arab storytelling tradition. Ibn al-Athir, al-Isfahani, al-Yaʿqubi, and Ibn Qutayba transmitted that tradition faithfully, but in doing so they confused the armour of al-Nuʿman with the armour of Imruʾ al-Qays.

4.

Once we remove this mythical layer from the historical tradition and set it aside for separate treatment, two distinct historical conflicts remain.

The first was a campaign led by al-Harith ibn Zalim on behalf of his master, the king of al-Hira, against the tribes of Tayma.

The second was a war led at Dhu Qar by Iyas ibn Qabisa al-Taʾi, the military commander installed as king by the Persians, acting on their behalf against Banu Shayban.

These should be understood as two separate historical episodes.

The extraordinary merging carried out by later chroniclers obscured that distinction.

The first report concerns one of the military campaigns of the kings of al-Hira and, more specifically, a typical campaign of al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ against rebellious Arab tribes at Tayma, tribes which may have been seeking some measure of independence from outside authority.

The consistently harsh behaviour of al-Hira towards such tribes, as reflected in the old reports, appears to have been part of a wider strategy for imposing political and military control.

In my view, this also explains both the Persian interest in al-Mundhir and their eventual hostility towards him. Like the tribes he had fought so fiercely, al-Mundhir eventually pursued a policy with an independent character.

These wars must have taken place after 516 CE at the latest, when al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ recovered the throne he had previously lost through the intrigues of the Persian king Kavad.

From that point, for thirty years or more, with only brief pauses, al-Mundhir continued to pursue tribes that had either refused to support him or shown even the slightest sympathy for his Yemeni rival, the Kindite king al-Harith ibn ʿAmr Akil al-Murar.

That rival had taken the throne of al-Hira from him with the support of Yemeni tribes and, according to the tradition, with the open encouragement of Mazdakite Persia. Persia was said to have engineered al-Mundhir’s removal because he refused to embrace Mazdakism, the official religion favoured in the reign of Kavad.

In these circumstances, it is entirely plausible that the commander of al-Mundhir’s campaigns around Tayma was al-Harith ibn Zalim.

The chroniclers themselves describe him as a determined and ambitious man. His later death, apparently on the orders of one of the princes of al-Hira, supports that picture (Nöldeke, Princes of Ghassan, p. 48).

At some point before the year given in these traditions, al-Harith ibn Zalim clearly came into conflict with a powerful tribal chief at Tayma.

We may never know that man’s real name. The traditions preserve him only through the confused figure of:

al-Samawʾal ibn ʿAdiya.

References in pre-Islamic poetry suggest that he was a Jewish religious leader, one of the rabbis or learned men of the Jewish community.

If he was also one of the leading men of Tayma, there would be nothing strange about his composing poetry. In an old Bedouin religious setting, priest, king, and poet were not sharply separated roles. A king could also be a priest or a poet, and the reverse was equally possible.

We can therefore accept the possibility that a leading man among the Arab tribes of Tayma came into conflict with al-Harith ibn Zalim and that this man was called al-Samawʾal.

Alternatively, he may have belonged to a tribe or clan known as al-Samawʾal, whose collective name eventually displaced his personal one.

As part of the defence of Tayma—a prosperous oasis famous for its ancient religious character, where Nabonidus, the last king of Babylon, had lived for roughly ten years and made it a temporary capital before Babylon fell to the Persians in 539 BCE—this al-Samawʾal may at some point before 554 CE have fortified himself behind the walls of al-Ablaq, or close to them.

The battle may then have become known by the name of the fortress, following the old Arab habit of naming wars as “days” after places or events: the Day of al-Hibar, the Day of Halima, and so on.

A second war took place elsewhere, at another time, though for broadly similar political reasons.

In 613 CE, after the Persians imprisoned and then killed al-Nuʿman ibn al-Mundhir because of his increasingly independent policy, they placed Iyas ibn Qabisa al-Taʾi, a chief from Tayy loyal to Persia, on the throne of al-Hira.

The new ruler then launched a war of submission, desired by the Persians, against Banu Shayban, the tribe that had sympathized with al-Nuʿman, supported his attempt to break away from Persian control, and protected him.

There were, in fact, several marriages connecting al-Nuʿman’s family with Banu Shayban. They were his relatives by marriage.

These two historical wars therefore took place, as we can see, in different places and at different times.

It makes little sense to suppose that both were caused by a demand for the return of one hundred suits of armour—or even four hundred, as Ibn al-Athir reports.

The image of a hero leaving behind his weapons before going to his death, followed by an enemy who appears afterwards and claims the right to inherit them, is a repeatable mythic pattern. A storyteller can use it for whatever purpose his larger narrative requires.

This explains why the armour appears again and again: in one version with Imruʾ al-Qays, and in another with al-Nuʿman.

5.

The old Arab proverb “More faithful than al-Samawʾal” has no necessary connection with the legend about the quarrel over the dead hero’s weapons.

Nor does it necessarily have anything to do with the war launched by al-Hira to subdue Tayma.

Like many old Arabic proverbs, it may instead preserve an echo of a much older Arab culture of sayings and wisdom that spread across the ancient world with the great migrations into Syria, Babylon, Egypt, Crete, and along the Mediterranean coast.

The Bible may provide useful evidence here.

An entire biblical book, Proverbs, was written or compiled in the Babylonian cultural world. It contains a large body of sayings, comparisons, playful turns of phrase, fragments of sermons, and religious maxims—in short, material that can be gathered under the broad heading of wisdom.

At the heart of this culture lies a very old human instinct, one that remains clearly visible in our own time: the desire to build an effective and memorable story around a proverb so that other people will understand exactly what we mean.

The story also helps lodge the symbolic message of the proverb in the listener’s memory.

A proverb’s message is often difficult to convey fully without the help of other narrative devices: the story, the tale, the folktale, or the myth.

Even today, the proverbs we use in everyday speech are usually embedded in stories. Their moral force depends on a simple principle: the symbolic message becomes more powerful when the proverb is carried by a story or myth.

Seen in this light, there is nothing unusual about inserting the names of poets, tribal chiefs, kings, or priests into legendary narratives and turning them into heroes.

The purpose is to communicate the proverb’s message as effectively and persuasively as possible.

This, I believe, is what happened with the supposed Tayman notable al-Samawʾal ibn ʿAdiya.

His name was woven into a vivid and entertaining story so that the ancient Arab proverb “More faithful than al-Samawʾal” would become inseparable from him.

Indeed, the proverb is barely intelligible to someone who hears it without also knowing the legend of the loyalty and the armour.

The legend carries the burden of explaining the saying. It sends the proverb out again in a new form, through the medium of story.

What survives in our collective memory is therefore the symbolic message of trust and faithfulness, together with the strict moral idea that stands behind it.

We must refuse, under any circumstances—even if we are threatened with harm—to do anything that would betray the hero or betray the trust he left in our hands before going to meet his fate and his tragic death.

We are required not to hand over his weapon to the enemy who struck him down. We must not surrender the one inheritance he has left us: his memory, through which his tragedy is told again. Every possible breach of that duty must be resisted.

When we, as modern recipients of the story, refuse to betray the dead hero and preserve his weapon, as the hero of Fort al-Ablaq did—and when we remain ready, in our own way, to renew that fierce struggle with the enemy over possession of the slain hero’s weapon, his living memory—then myth and proverb have achieved their purpose with extraordinary precision.

Previous6. Did Arab Tribes Find Yemeni Inscriptions Beneath the Foundations of the Kaaba?Next8. The Legend of Abu Righal and the Year of the Elephant Campaign against Mecca

8. The Legend of Abu Righal and the Year of the Elephant Campaign against Mecca

Did the Arabs really know, in their distant pre-Islamic past, a traitor named Abu Righal?

In the Prophetic Biography (1:42), Ibn Hisham relates a story taken from Ibn Ishaq, the original author of the biography—a man whom Imam Malik famously described as “one of the impostors.” The story concerns Abu Righal’s supposed betrayal during the campaign of the Ethiopian ruler Abraha against Mecca around 570 CE.

According to the biography, Abraha fought a brief war against two Arab tribes, Shahran and Nahis, defeated them, and took a prisoner with him to guide his army toward Mecca. He eventually reached Taif, where Masʿud ibn Muʿattib, the chief of Thaqif, came out with a group of his tribesmen and addressed Abraha:

“O king, we are your servants. We hear and obey you, and we have no quarrel with you. What you seek is not here among us.”

By this they meant al-Lat, the ancient Arab sanctuary that rivalled the Kaaba.

They continued:

“What you seek is the sanctuary in Mecca. We will send someone with you to show you the way.”

Abraha therefore spared them, and they sent Abu Righal with him as a guide to Mecca. Ibn Hisham continues:

“Abraha set out with Abu Righal until he brought him to al-Mughammas. When he reached that place, Abu Righal died there. The Arabs then stoned his grave, and it is the grave that people still stone at al-Mughammas.”

Neither Ibn Hisham nor, by extension, Ibn Ishaq gives a convincing explanation for Abu Righal’s sudden death. Nor do they satisfactorily explain why his grave was stoned.

If the stoning really arose from Abu Righal’s alleged treachery, then the conduct of the tribes themselves seems far more serious. Ibn Hisham’s own account tells us that the chief of Thaqif described himself as one of Abraha’s servants. The tribes surrendered to the invading army, welcomed it, and offered their help. Abu Righal, by contrast, was simply a man ordered by his tribe to accompany the campaign.

Why, then, would the Arabs stone the grave of a man who was acting under orders, while tribal leaders themselves came out to welcome the invaders and offered to help them destroy the Kaaba?

Ibn Hisham’s account leaves another mystery unexplained. Thaqif was a powerful tribe. Why did it send only one man to assist a vast military expedition crossing dangerous desert routes?

And why did Abu Righal die suddenly in this particular place, al-Mughammas—a location associated with an older Arab legend about a man who was also called Abu Righal and who served the prophet Salih of Thamud?

Were there originally two different men with exactly the same name, linked to exactly the same place but living in different ages?

I want to take Ibn Hisham’s report apart and examine the legend that developed around Abu Righal. As we can see, he became an extraordinarily powerful figure in Arab and Islamic memory. To this day, Arabs may call a traitor an “Abu Righal.”

In Greek and Roman sources, Abraha the Ethiopian appears under the name Abraham. This is the form found in Procopius. Arab chroniclers, however, recorded his name as Abraha.

Jawad Ali noted in al-Mufassal (3:175) that the traditions Ibn Hisham took from Ibn Ishaq are closely connected with the accounts of Wahb ibn Munabbih and Muhammad ibn Kaʿb al-Qurazi, who died sometime between 118 and 120 AH. Both men were of Jewish origin and were storytellers who recited narratives in the mosque.

Some of the reports transmitted by Ibn Ishaq and al-Qurazi also appear to have found their way into al-Tabari’s History of Kings and Messengers, principally through Ibn Ishaq’s biography as preserved by Ibn Hisham. This helps explain how the story of Abu Righal’s betrayal passed from one generation of Arab and Muslim writers to the next.

Greek historians recorded a great deal about Yemen in the age of Abraha, since the campaign against the Kaaba formed part of the wider struggle to spread Christianity in Arabia.

In Greek sources, Abraham—or Ebrahem in some versions—was said to have been a slave owned by a Greek merchant. The Arabs came to know him as Abraha the Ethiopian, the man with the elephant who marched against Mecca to destroy the Kaaba.

The year of this campaign became known as the Year of the Elephant, traditionally also the year in which the Prophet Muhammad was born. The Quran refers to the expedition in the Surah of the Elephant:

Have you not seen how your Lord dealt with the people of the elephant? Did He not bring their scheme to ruin? He sent against them birds in flocks, striking them with stones of baked clay, and left them like eaten straw.

The campaign soon failed. The invading army collapsed near Mecca, and Abraha himself is said to have died of fever.

This broad background, intended mainly for non-specialist readers, shows how historical reports and mythology can become fused.

We must now proceed carefully in trying to uncover the nature of the imaginary figure whom Ibn Hisham created and who, largely because of him, entered both old and modern historical literature as the guide who led Abraha’s Ethiopian army toward Mecca and whose grave the tribes supposedly stoned at al-Mughammas.

Al-Masʿudi, in The Meadows of Gold (2:201), gives a very different account.

According to him, Abu Righal worked as an assistant to the prophet Salih, collecting alms and levies. In modern terms, he was a collector of religious taxes. He apparently performed this duty with great efficiency until the powerful tribe of Thaqif accused him of violating the rules governing collection. They rose against him and killed him.

A verse attributed to Umayya ibn Abi al-Salt—perhaps a later fabrication, though probably resting on some older foundation—suggests that this Abu Righal really was regarded as one of Salih’s assistants, a forceful and unusually capable tax collector who was eventually killed after a bitter struggle:

They drove all Adnan from their land, and had subdued the tribes. They killed the noble Abu Righal in Mecca, while the packs were being loaded.

In this version, Ibn Hisham’s Abu Righal, supposedly living during the Ethiopian campaign against Mecca, disappears entirely as a historical person and becomes an invention.

Umayya ibn Abi al-Salt lived around the time of Abraha’s expedition, yet the poem places Abu Righal in the legendary age of Thamud and says that he was killed in Mecca while collecting taxes.

Ibn al-Athir, following other traditions, likewise places Abu Righal in the age of Thamud and the prophet Salih, not in the Year of the Elephant.

Ibn al-Jawzi does the same in al-Muntazam (1:256), where he tells what appears to be the original legend of Abu Righal.

He describes the punishment of Thamud after the slaughter of Salih’s sacred she-camel. Once they had killed and eaten the animal, divine punishment came upon them and continued for three days. On the fourth morning:

“A cry came to them from heaven containing the sound of every thunderbolt. Their hearts were torn apart inside their chests and they died. One man among them was inside the Sacred Precinct. His name was Abu Righal, and the sanctuary protected him from the punishment.”

Another verse, attributed to the pre-Islamic poet Amr ibn Darrak al-Abdi, suggests that the charge brought against Abu Righal by Thaqif concerned his handling of religious taxes. According to this tradition, the tribe attacked and killed him after an incident in which he displayed unprecedented harshness and injustice.

Al-Abdi says:

If I severed my ties with Qays and allied al-Muzun against Tamim, I would be a greater sinner than Abu Righal and more unjust in judgment than Sodom.

These traditions are enough, in my view, to undermine the fabricated historical report circulated by Ibn Hisham in the Sira: the story that a traitor named Abu Righal guided Abraha’s Ethiopian army toward Mecca around 570 CE.

They also strip this legendary figure of the tragic role later assigned to him throughout ancient Arab history and even into the present day. The echo of this invented betrayal can still be heard in modern political language, where “Abu Righal” is used as a label for traitors and political enemies.

Yet the circumstances of his death deserve closer attention because they preserve the outline of a symbolic act. In the older tradition, Abu Righal is a punished mythical figure whose story seems to concern the betrayal of the land itself—its barrenness, its cruelty, and its injustice toward human beings. He is killed after an incident connected with the forcible collection of religious taxes.

Before dismantling Ibn Hisham’s version more fully, and before presenting the evidence that his Abu Righal is a narrative invention developed by later chroniclers, we should pause over the repeated transformations and contradictions in the Arab portrayals of this figure.

Why, in particular, did the idea of betraying the Arab tribes during Abraha’s campaign become attached to him?

Abu Righal became a figure that the pastoral imagination could repeatedly revive because he had been placed in a legendary age.

This reflects a culture inclined to trace present tensions, passing events, visions, and beliefs back to a distant period whose actual historical existence cannot be verified. Within such a culture, signs and symbols constantly generate vivid and persuasive images of events and beliefs.

Arab tradition repeatedly returned to an almost fixed image of Abu Righal: a figure who stirred fear and revulsion because he embodied mercilessness, indifference, and the reckless destruction of other people’s lives.

Yet this villain, this defiled figure, had once been sacred.

In Ibn al-Jawzi’s words, he had originally been “a righteous man whom the sanctuary protected from punishment.” He was then transformed into the complete opposite: a contemptible and evil man to whom almost any atrocity or shameful act could easily be attributed.

The contradictions became even greater when Ibn Hisham inserted Abu Righal into the story of the Year of the Elephant, the event by which the Arabs dated the Ethiopian attack on Mecca after the occupation of Yemen.

These endlessly conflicting portrayals suggest that a deeper idea lies hidden in the darkest and most obscure layers of the legend.

Abu Righal seems to preserve something of the memory of Iblis, the heavenly Satan: a being who begins as righteous—almost angelic—and ends as evil.

That would explain why the later borrowings and variations built around the character are just as contradictory as the character’s own origin.

A careful study of the custom of stoning Abu Righal’s grave, and of its religious and ritual roots among the earliest Arab tribes before Yemen fell to the Ethiopians around 524 CE, may therefore reveal the true meaning of the practice. The custom survived for centuries, eventually reaching Islam and becoming part of its ritual world.

It may also reveal Abu Righal’s connection with an age of famine in Arabia.

We shall see that the stoning of this grave had nothing to do with an actual Arab traitor named Abu Righal, despite Ibn Hisham’s claim.

The place known to Arab geographers as “the grave of Abu Righal” does not point to a real grave at all. It refers to an imagined grave at al-Mughammas.

Just as the pastoral imagination created this figure and told his legend, it also created a believable history for him. This is one of mythology’s characteristic narrative devices. Myth does more than present its stories as though they were established facts. It also supplies the details that make them believable.

Here is the grave of a hero who changed from angel to devil. Here too is the site at which his ritual stoning takes place.

What can be regarded as historically established in the Arab accounts of the Ethiopian campaign is that al-Mughammas was the place where the army halted and received assistance from Arab tribes that were ready to cooperate.

Their bitter rivalries, along with religious and tribal tensions with other groups dominant in the Hijaz, made some of them willing to offer their services to the Yemenis and their Ethiopian masters. They could join, however loosely, in a military alliance whose immediate aim was to take religious authority away from the northern Arabs.

The words attributed to Thaqif when speaking to Abraha are especially revealing:

“We have no quarrel with you, and what you seek is not here among us.”

They wanted Abraha to spare their own sanctuary, al-Lat, which rivalled the Kaaba.

Indeed, they actively encouraged him:

“What you seek is the sanctuary in Mecca. We will send someone with you to show you the way.”

This is Ibn Hisham’s own account.

In that sense, Abraha received an unexpected degree of encouragement from southern, Qahtani tribes—perhaps more than he had hoped for. Thaqif offered him ideal assistance in exchange for the preservation of its own sanctuary, al-Lat.

A close reading of the historical reports, legends, stories, and anecdotes surrounding both the campaign and Abu Righal suggests that oral transmission eventually confused and merged two geographical locations.

One was associated with a wild plant called raghl, which tribes ate during periods of famine. The other was al-Mughammas.

Until comparatively recent times, tribes in southern Iraq—especially the poor—still ate raghl as food.

This confusion eventually produced the idea of a single place associated with “Abu Raghl,” named after the plant that grew there in abundance.

Over time, the place itself was personified. A geographical name became the name of a hero.

When Ibn Hisham came to write the prophetic biography, he found in it a suitable name for a legendary character who could be branded a traitor and made to carry the blame that properly belonged to tribes that had surrendered and cooperated with the Ethiopian occupiers.

The wider body of traditions about this campaign also suggests that the earliest Arabs once preserved the memory of a particular place in Arabia as a symbol of a great famine that struck the whole region from Babylon to Egypt.

Abu Righal, in this earlier form, seems originally to have been a place-name associated with the appearance of raghl.

Arabic itself preserves the expression arghalat al-ard, “the land became covered in raghl,” used when this plant appears during periods of failed rain.

The place-name may therefore have existed before any character called Abu Righal was imagined, including the later story that he had served the prophet Salih in Thamud as a collector of alms.

Whenever danger threatened, the tribes repeatedly recalled the ancestral memory of themselves as starving communities.

The danger might be a devastating war or a natural disaster: the raging wind of Thamud, the heavenly cry at Mecca, or recurring drought and famine.

In one such act of remembrance, Ibn Hisham appears to have equated the danger of foreign occupation with the danger of ancient famine.

Both contained the idea of betrayal.

In the first case, the land betrayed the tribes: it dried up, became barren, and filled with death.

In the second, some of the tribes themselves committed the betrayal and placed the sacred sanctuary of the Arabs in danger.

The custom of stoning the supposed traitor’s grave can therefore be understood as a symbolic stoning of drought, a practice inherited from the ancient Arabs.

It appears that when Abraha reached the territory of Thaqif, the Arabs were still performing this stoning ritual at the very place where the invading army later halted to receive help.

Yaqut al-Hamawi, in Muʿjam al-Buldan (3:61), connects the legend of Abu Righal with the prophet Salih. He writes that Abu Righal:

“was a king in Taif who oppressed his subjects. He passed a woman nursing an orphaned child with the milk of a she-goat she owned. He seized the goat, leaving the child without milk. It was a year of severe drought, so God struck him with a calamity that destroyed him. The Arabs then stoned his grave, which lies between Mecca and Taif.”

Yaqut’s version is one of a long and continuous series of accounts supporting this interpretation.

Taken together, they strengthen the view that Abu Righal has an ancient mythical origin unrelated to the historical invasion of Mecca.

Several features of these texts are especially important.

1) Ibn Hisham’s account in the Prophetic Biography is inaccurate. Abu Righal, as he describes him, did not exist. The figure belongs more naturally to the legendary age of Thamud than to the Ethiopian campaign.

2) These traditions connect Abu Righal—a symbolic representation of the great famine experienced by the tribes—with memories of Ethiopian occupation.

Yaqut’s account describes a hungry child feeding from a she-goat. Why is the child being nursed by a goat? The arrival of Abu Righal, the tax collector, also occurs during an exceptionally harsh year in which the land has become barren. And immediately after his death, the Arabs stone his grave.

These elements form the raw material of another story, fragments of which eventually reached Ibn Hisham. He merged two ages into one and two places into one.

The result was a false idea that became deeply rooted in ancient Arab history: that an individual traitor named Abu Righal helped the Ethiopians.

The historical accounts themselves tell us something different. Major Arab tribes submitted to Abraha and openly offered him their help.

3) According to these traditions, the stoning of the grave belonged to a specific ritual practice whose roots reached back to the age of the great famine. It had nothing to do with the alleged betrayal during the Ethiopian occupation.

The myths express this symbolically through the loss of the tribes’ source of survival: the single she-goat whose milk sustains the child.

Famine came to strip the tribes of everything they possessed.

That famine was then personified as an evil man who had once served the prophet Salih but eventually betrayed the trust placed in him.

4) The political and social weight of Thaqif and the Qahtani tribes within the emerging Islamic community appears to have helped redirect the historical memory of what had happened.

Thaqif’s submission and the cooperation of Qahtani tribes had been driven in part by religious rivalry between two ancient sacred centres: the Kaaba and al-Lat, the northern Kaaba and the southern sanctuary of al-Lat.

The blame was therefore transferred to an imaginary figure who had never existed.

Yet Abu Righal did not merely become part of history. He came to possess it. The history assigned to him was the history of the Ethiopian campaign against the Kaaba.

Previous7. The Armour of al-Nuʿman or the Armour of Imruʾ al-Qays?Next9. Arab Pharaohs in Egypt and “Foreign Gods” in Crete

9. Arab Pharaohs in Egypt and “Foreign Gods” in Crete

The works of the old Arab and Muslim chroniclers repeatedly make a striking claim: Arab pharaohs once ruled Egypt.

Al-Tabari, al-Masʿudi, al-Fakihi, Ibn al-Athir, and others do not hesitate to give the names of some of these supposed Arab pharaohs.

Al-Tabari, followed by Ibn al-Athir, gives the name al-Rayyan ibn al-Walid ibn Musʿab. Other writers identify him as al-Walid ibn Saʿb.

There are also countless traditional claims that Arab dynasties ruled Egypt during the age of the Hyksos.

Is there anything that supports these claims?

To answer that question, we must begin from a point somewhat removed from the claims themselves.

Before the rise of their great civilization, the ancient Greeks wondered where their gods had originally come from.

There seems to have been something close to a consensus that the Greek gods had come from abroad.

The Egyptians held a similar belief. They regarded the Hyksos age, around 1720 BCE, as the period in which foreign gods entered Egypt.

We shall later examine the significance of this striking agreement between Greek and Egyptian traditions.

Historically, this belief was certainly widespread by the time of Herodotus in the mid-fifth century BCE.

In a long passage quoted by Martin Bernal in Black Athena, Herodotus says:

Almost all the names of the Greek gods came to Greece from Egypt. From my own investigations, I know that they came from abroad, and it seems most likely that they came from Egypt, since the names of all the gods were known there from ancient times. The Greeks also took from Egypt the rituals and other practices that I shall discuss later. In ancient times, according to what I was told at Dodona, the Pelasgians offered every kind of sacrifice to the gods without distinguishing between them by name, because they had not yet heard of such distinctions. They called the Greek gods Theoi, meaning those who hold the final decision.

This passage, I would argue, points to several conclusions.

1) The meaning attributed here to the Greek word Theoi is remarkably close to the Arabic word dhu.

Both can be understood, in this interpretation, as referring to one who possesses authority, decision, or ownership.

If, according to Herodotus, the Greeks called every deity Theoi, the Arabic use of dhu in divine names is also well known: Dhu Bakka, Dhu al-Khalasa, Dhu al-Shara, Dhu Ghaba, Dhu Rajl, Dhu Shaʿr, Dhu Tin, Dhu al-Kaffayn, Dhu Samaʾi, and so on.

This suggests that at some point a borrowing occurred in which a deity was referred to simply as dhi or dhu, “the possessor” or “lord,” and that this became the Greek equivalent Theoi.

The meaning, in this reading, is essentially the same: the one who possesses authority, the master of the matter, as in the Quranic expression “Master of the Day of Judgment.”

Within this framework, the Greeks imagined their chief god as Zeus, a name which, I believe, contains the same phonetic elements as Theoi or Arabic dhu.

2) If Herodotus is correct that most Greek gods were of Egyptian origin, then the supposed entry of foreign gods into Egypt must have occurred during a period of profound upheaval, when the inhabitants found themselves worshipping in temples dedicated to unfamiliar deities.

In my view, that period can only be the age of the Hyksos.

Before going further, however, we have a long list of Arab gods said to have entered the Greek world through Egypt and, more specifically, through another intermediary region: the Egyptian settlement of Thebes in Crete.

Historical reports and Greek mythology tell us that this settlement was built by a Phoenician people from the Arabian Gulf, the people of Cadmus.

Among the gods who supposedly passed by this route was the ancient Arab deity Dhu al-Shara, who became Dusares among the Greeks.

There was also Manat, one of the most famous and important goddesses of the early pre-Islamic Arabs. The Greeks, according to this argument, adopted her as Moneta.

Ali Fahmi Khashim, in The Arab Gods of Egypt (1:2), drew attention to a possible connection between Moneta and the English word money.

In this interpretation, Manat was originally a goddess of reckoning or accounting.

This Manat is distinct from the other Manat mentioned in the Quran—“and Manat, the third, the other” (53:20)—who was associated with fate.

The first Manat, which became Moneta with a change in the opening vowel, was the goddess of reckoning.

The Akkadians used the weight unit mina or man, which I believe was derived from the same name. This unit is still used in rural Iraq today.

The other Manat, by contrast, was a goddess of destiny or fate, reflected in expressions connected with the Arabic word maniyya, meaning one’s appointed and inevitable death.

It appears that the two were once worshipped separately according to their distinct functions but were later merged into a single figure.

Because the Greeks supposedly adopted the first Manat as Moneta, goddess of reckoning, the Romans later minted coins in her name. From there, the word entered Latin and eventually gave rise to money.

Another example is the ancient Arab god Hubal, the most famous of the pre-Islamic Arab idols.

I believe Hubal entered the Greek world through Egypt during the Hyksos period and became Apollo.

The biblical and Arab story of the struggle between Abel and Cain also seems closely connected, in my view, with Hubal, who was a god of hunting, abundance, and food—particularly meat.

The old form of the name itself appears to contain this meaning.

A saying attributed to Aisha refers to the plumpness of young women:

“The women in those days had not yet been made fleshy by meat.”

The Arabic root connected with hubal, with different vowelings, can carry the sense of abundance of flesh and fat.

The Greeks, I suggest, took this image and transformed it into Apollo as a god associated with hunting.

A more radical reading of the Abel and Cain story would allow us to equate the killing of Abel—Hubal—by his brother Cain with the death of the god of hunting, abundance, and food.

Abel is portrayed as a shepherd who offers the finest of his flock as a sacrifice.

In this way, the image of Apollo can be seen as containing all the elements that formed the image of Hubal.

Hubal was one of the idols of Kinana and, according to Ibn al-Kalbi in The Book of Idols, their greatest object of worship:

“Hubal was made of red carnelian in the form of a man whose right hand was broken. Quraysh found him in that condition and gave him a hand of gold.”

At a later period, according to the same line of argument, the Greeks adopted the ancient Arab god Qays, whose name survives as the ancestor of one of the oldest and largest Arab tribal confederations, Qays Aylan.

He entered the Greek pantheon as Cassius.

Since the Arab Qays was a fierce mountain god, he supposedly passed through Egypt into the Greek world with these same attributes and became Cassius, god of the mountain.

3) Herodotus’ claim that the Greek gods came from abroad can, in my view, be fully understood only if it is connected specifically with the Hyksos period.

This was the human movement that brought the earliest Arabs into the eastern Nile Delta around 1720 BCE, where they established what Arab chroniclers later remembered as the age of the “Arab pharaohs in Egypt.”

The Egyptian historian Manetho, writing around 300 BCE, described this movement by saying:

“They were Arabs.”

The description that eventually became dominant, however, was the Greek one: Hyksos, which corresponded to what Egyptians understood as Bedouin kings or shepherd kings.

The movement of Arab gods into the Greek world through Egypt must therefore, in this interpretation, be placed within the same period in which Arab dynasties supposedly entered Egypt.

Later we shall examine what historical meaning may lie behind the Arab chroniclers’ claims that such dynasties existed.

4) This migration replaced Egypt’s national gods with the gods of the Hyksos, or desert peoples.

This, I believe, lies behind Egyptian complaints that foreign gods had arrived and displaced their own.

Some of the names of Hyksos kings bear a puzzling resemblance to the names of ancient Arab tribes.

One example is Thamoudi, recorded in the Egyptian document known as the Turin Papyrus. The name appears to refer both to a king and to a particular human group of the period.

Louis Awad, in An Introduction to Arabic Philology (Cairo, 1980), suspected that the Hyksos royal name Asis resembled the Quranic title al-Aziz, used for the ruler of Egypt.

I would go further.

In its original form, as found in ancient Yemeni inscriptions, the name appears to belong to a southern Arabian deity worshipped as Azizu or Aziz.

The Arabic form Aziz appears in Yemeni inscriptions in combinations such as Antar Azizu, where Antar itself seems to be a transformed form of the Babylonian Ishtar.

Aziz was originally, in this interpretation, a Yemeni god. Some Hyksos kings may have adopted his name as a form of blessing or devotion.

This suggests a possible relationship between the royal designation—not necessarily a personal name—and the worship of the god Aziz.

The name survives to this day as one of the divine names in Islam: al-Aziz, “the Mighty.”

We also find it at al-Suwayda in Syria in the form Azizu/Kubaru.

An inscription from Bayt Dabaʿan, discovered by Mutahhar Ali al-Iryani, contains the phrase:

“And their own god, Antar Azizu of Jaʾub, lord of the temple.”

5) We should also note one of the most famous gods of Egyptian mythology: Seth.

He appears as an evil desert deity, almost demonic, who kills his brother Osiris, god of fertility.

The name Seth, I suggest, is another form of the Arabic Shith, as in Shith son of Adam.

These, in very brief form, are the elements needed to establish a broad historical framework for examining the Arab chroniclers’ claims that Arab pharaohs once ruled Egypt.

The Arab accounts connect the arrival of the Amalekites in Egypt with a great famine.

This famine may have been one of the main forces driving migration—or expansion—toward eastern Egypt, whose fertile land would have made it an obvious target.

Al-Tabari, Abu al-Fida, Abu al-Faraj al-Isfahani, and others all make the same basic claim: some of the pharaohs were of Arab origin, specifically Amalekite.

The Amalekites were remembered as an extinct Arab people.

According to the Egyptian historian Manetho, the Hyksos established a capital in the eastern Delta called Avaris.

Their first six rulers formed the first Hyksos dynasty, and the most famous was the fourth king, Apophis.

The list preserved by the Egyptian priest and historian Manetho around 300 BCE differs, at least on the surface, from the Arab lists given by al-Tabari, al-Masʿudi, al-Hamdani, and Ibn al-Athir.

Nevertheless, it gives a coherent picture of roughly 153 years—the entire period during which Hyksos power was confined to one of Egypt’s two regions.

The real cause of this mass tribal movement toward Egypt seems to have been a great famine that struck Arabia and devastated the conditions necessary for life, destroying traditional food networks.

There are many references to such a famine.

The Bible describes it as the famine in the land of Canaan. Old Arab sources describe it as the famine of Mecca.

The Quran also refers explicitly to Mecca as lying in “a valley without cultivation” (14:37), while the Quranic story of Joseph speaks of years of drought symbolized by “seven lean cows” (12:43), a famine that struck Egypt as well.

According to Manetho’s list, the Arab pharaohs of Egypt were:

  1. Salitis
  2. Beon
  3. Aba-Sinan
  4. Yanas
  5. Abu-Fis
  6. Amsis

The Arab chroniclers, by contrast, give the following names:

  1. Qabus
  2. al-Walid ibn Musʿab
  3. al-Rayyan ibn al-Walid
  4. Sinan ibn Alwan
  5. Nas

A report from al-Hamdani in al-Iklil (1:72) supports the possibility that there was a sixth pharaoh.

He writes:

“The pharaohs of Egypt are commonly said to have been Amalekites. Among them was al-Rayyan. It is also said that al-Walid ibn al-Rayyan was the king in the time of Joseph, while al-Walid ibn Musʿab was the ruler in the time of Moses.”

Why is this qualification important?

It allows us to consider the possibility that al-Walid or al-Rayyan was not simply a personal name but a designation for the pharaoh of Joseph—or simply “the king.”

We have already seen the suggested connection between Asis and al-Aziz, both of which, in this reading, point toward an Egyptian ruler.

The Quran says:

“The wife of al-Aziz said...” (12:51)

In this interpretation, al-Aziz means her king or pharaoh.

Ibn al-Athir, drawing on al-Tabari, gives another striking version, also repeated by al-Masʿudi.

In al-Kamil (1:73), Ibn al-Athir says that the first Arab pharaoh of Egypt was called Sinan ibn Alwan.

Al-Masʿudi says essentially the same thing, though he drops the name Alwan and simply calls the ruler Sinan. He then adds another pharaoh called Nas.

To assess these claims, we should establish several points.

1) Even today, scholars have not reached a single agreed definition of the word Hyksos. Competing interpretations remain contradictory and difficult to reconcile.

2) Despite disagreement over the meaning of the term, there is broad agreement that Hyksos rule lasted no more than 153 years, beginning around 1720 BCE and ending with their departure in 1567 BCE.

3) Historians and interpreters of ancient texts have never seriously considered another possibility: that the evidence points to a broad tribal alliance involving the Amalekites, Thamud, Daws, and other tribes, which together succeeded in taking control of the Delta.

The importance of this hypothesis is that it would place the term Hyksos within a different and more precise framework.

The expressions “pastoral tribes” and “shepherd kings,” in this reading, are no more than descriptions that conceal the true origin of these groups: the earliest Arabs.

4) Ancient Egyptian inscriptions never use the name Hyksos.

The Turin Papyrus instead speaks of six foreign rulers.

The ambiguous word Hyksos entered later through Greek writings about the period.

There can be little doubt, within this interpretation, that these foreign rulers imposed the worship of foreign gods on the Egyptian population in place of the worship of Ra.

These “foreign rulers” were:

1. Apachnan, in Manetho.

This, I suggest, is the Sinan of al-Masʿudi, Ibn al-Athir, and other Arab chroniclers.

In Ibn al-Athir’s al-Kamil, the name appears as Sinan ibn Alwan the Amalekite. Al-Masʿudi drops Alwan and retains only Sinan.

The name Alwan also appears in biblical lists of clans and tribes during the “numbering of the people,” and one of the large Amalekite branches carried the same name.

There is probably a connection between this form and Aylan, as in one of the largest ancient Arab tribal confederations, Qays Aylan.

In my view, the form ultimately derives from the ancient Arab divine name Aliwan, meaning “the Most High God.”

2. Innas, in Manetho.

This is Nas in al-Masʿudi.

The important point is that the name appears to designate a tribe rather than an individual, much like Apachnan ibn Alwan.

Ibn Nas also appears in Arab genealogical lists as a direct son of Ishmael.

3. Asis, in Manetho.

The Arab chroniclers omit this name and replace it with expressions such as “the pharaoh of Joseph” or “the king in the time of Joseph.”

The Quranic form is clearer: Aziz of Egypt.

As for some of the remaining names, two are particularly easy, in my view, to connect with Arabic forms: Abu-Fis, which may be compared with Apachnan, and Salitis, which can readily be read as al-Salt.

What does this reading suggest?

Modern Arab intellectual habits have encouraged a tradition of dismissing or belittling the “claims” of the old Arab chroniclers.

We often ignore the value of their statements because they are expressed in mythical language.

And we frequently fail to consider another possibility: that these writers were not simply inventing falsehoods.

The sweeping accusation that they did so does not stand up to scrutiny.

What we need today is a more careful and conservative reading of the old texts, free from contempt and from the deliberate dismissal of clues preserved in an ancient language whose alphabet the Arabs themselves have forgotten—the language of historical tradition in all its mythical shades.

Previous8. The Legend of Abu Righal and the Year of the Elephant Campaign against MeccaNext10. The Fabrication of Imruʾ al-Qays

10. The Fabrication of Imruʾ al-Qays

The Poisoned Gifts in Pre-Islamic Poetry

Who was Imruʾ al-Qays?

Was he the sick poet covered in sores, or the prince fighting to recover his throne?

Was he the Wandering King, or the captive prince—the man therefore called the Lesser King?

Was he the young man who appealed to the tribes of northern Arabia for support, only to be abandoned by them and left unable to avenge his murdered father? Or was he the warrior who attacked those same tribes, killing and slaughtering them?

Was he the outlaw poet and wanderer who led a band of drifters and outcasts? Or was he an ascetic and contemplative man, a poet of lightning and rain?

Was he from Kinda or Himyar? A Ghassanid, or a resident of al-Hira in Iraq?

Was his name Junduh, Awf, Malik, or Adi?

Across these scattered and conflicting portraits, the poet Imruʾ al-Qays appears as a king and an outlaw, a prince and a prisoner, a sick man and a lover of women, an ascetic worshipper and a friend of the Byzantine emperor Justinian (527–565 CE). It is ultimately impossible to accept all these portraits as descriptions of a single historical person: Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hujr, the supposed author of the famous Muʿallaqa beginning “Stop, let us weep,” and of the collection of poetry published under his name.

To reconstruct the historical account of this poet, we must therefore take apart the narratives written by the chroniclers who recorded his life. We must do the same with the collection attributed to him. Biography and Diwan alike need to be reread as material that is not purely historical, but has been subject to combination, confusion, and overlap. Much of it appears to preserve the images and stories of other figures, some contemporary with Imruʾ al-Qays and others considerably older.

In fact, more than twenty poets in ancient Arab history were known by the name Imruʾ al-Qays. Some lived very close to the rise of Islam, and at least one was counted among the Prophet’s Companions. Al-Amidi, in al-Muʾtalif wa-l-Mukhtalif (p. 9), listed around ten pre-Islamic poets known by this name. The Orientalist Ahlwardt added another six whom he believed had genuinely borne the name and were poets.

During my own wider investigation into the material surrounding the name Qays, however, I found still more. The total rises to more than twenty-five poets whose genealogies and poems are known and who were called Imruʾ al-Qays.

The importance of these lists, in my view, is that they allow us to reconstruct the historical account of pre-Islamic poetry and of its greatest legendary poet, Imruʾ al-Qays. From there, we may arrive at a more accurate picture of this essential part of ancient Arab history.

The Diwan of Imruʾ al-Qays has been printed many times. The copies I relied on for this study are held at Leiden University in the Netherlands. The first printed edition appeared in Paris in 1837, edited by de Slane under the title A Delight for the Discerning and a Gift to Men of Letters: The Poems of Imruʾ al-Qays, based on the recension of al-Shantamari.

Ahlwardt attacked de Slane’s edition and refused to accept it. Using the recension of al-Sukkari instead, he republished the Diwan and added several poems and shorter fragments. In my view, these additions only deepened the unreasonable confusion between the different portraits of the poet and the poems supposedly belonging to him.

Around 1958, another edition appeared in Egypt. It relied entirely on the reports of al-Asmaʿi and contained twenty-eight poems. Al-Tusi believed these poems had been fabricated and attributed to the poet by later transmitters.

Taken as a whole, the chronicles, legends, and the Diwan itself give us the following portraits of Imruʾ al-Qays:

  1. Imruʾ al-Qays seeking vengeance for his father Hujr, who was killed by Banu Asad and their leader Ilbaʾ ibn Qays in northern Arabia.

  2. The prince pursued by al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ, king of al-Hira (516–556 CE), who sought refuge among the tribes of northern Arabia after his entire family had been killed and stripped of its throne.

  3. The formidable warrior who defeated tribes and mistakenly inflicted killing and slaughter on some of them.

  4. The Wandering King who lost his throne and travelled to Byzantium to meet Emperor Justinian and ask for military help in recovering it.

  5. The sick poet known as Dhu al-Quruh—“the Man of Sores.”

  6. The poet who lived in al-Hira in Iraq.

  7. The Ghassanid poet who sang of places in Syria and lived there.

  8. The Himyarite poet who sought the emperor’s help in attacking Aden.

  9. The Najdi poet who sang of places in Najd and described northern Arabia with a precision suggesting deep familiarity with the region.

  10. The poet who wept over deserted campsites and was known as Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Khidham, or Khudam.

  11. The debauched outlaw poet who immersed himself in wine and women and led a band of wandering outcasts.

  12. The poet who died in Ankara.

  13. Imruʾ al-Qays the Yemeni, poet of Dilmun.

  14. Imruʾ al-Qays known as al-Tammah.

  15. Imruʾ al-Qays al-Kalbi.

  16. The captive prince who fell into Ghassanid hands and was later freed.

  17. The Imruʾ al-Qays captured by the Yemeni king Ilsharah Yahdib in a battle near Aden.

  18. Imruʾ al-Qays, poet of lightning and rain.

Which of these men, then, wrote the Muʿallaqa and the Diwan?

Al-Amidi, writing in al-Muʾtalif wa-l-Mukhtalif around 370 AH, lists several poets whom the Arabs knew by the name Imruʾ al-Qays:

1. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hujr ibn al-Harith ibn Amr ibn Hujr Akil al-Murar

He was a pre-Islamic poet of Kinda whose poetry disappeared and was lost.

His grandfather al-Harith is claimed to have been the same al-Harith al-Kindi who ruled al-Hira after the Persians installed him there, having expelled its king al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ following the accession of Qubadh ibn Firuz (488–531 CE) in Persia.

2. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Abis ibn al-Mundhir al-Simt

He is the author of the lines:

Stand by the dwellings, as one held back, and take your time—you have not lost hope.

What harm is there in stopping at ruins whose traces have faded?

3. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Bakr ibn Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Harith al-Kindi

This was another man altogether, unrelated to the family of al-Harith al-Kindi mentioned above, the ruler of al-Hira who was killed by al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ after al-Mundhir recovered his throne with the help of the Iraqi tribes, following the death of Qubadh and the accession of Anushirvan in Persia.

4. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Amr ibn al-Harith ibn Muawiya al-Akbar ibn Thawr ibn Murattiʿ al-Kindi

Another pre-Islamic poet, unrelated to the two men with similar names mentioned above.

Ibn al-Athir also records him in Usd al-Ghaba (1:155), in addition to al-Amidi, under the name Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Bakr ibn al-Harith.

5. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hammam ibn Malik

The chroniclers called this man Adl al-Usra. He was said to have fled from battle during a raid launched by Zuhayr ibn Janab al-Kalbi. Ubayd ibn al-Abras mocked him for this in a famous poem, as did al-Muhalhil, who himself also bore the title Imruʾ al-Qays.

Yet this same Ibn Hammam appears in the poetry of Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hujr—the first poet on this list—as the earliest pre-Islamic poet to weep over deserted dwellings. Ibn Hujr says:

Turn aside with me to the weathered campsite; perhaps we may weep for the dwellings as Ibn Hammam wept.

Neither al-Amidi, nor Ahlwardt, nor al-Baghdadi, author of Khizanat al-Adab, noticed the significance of the merging of Ibn Khidham with Ibn Hammam. Nor did any of them give serious attention to what it meant for the poet of deserted ruins, Ibn Hammam, to become confused with Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hujr, who would then arbitrarily be credited with the poetic tradition of stopping and weeping at abandoned campsites.

6. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Bahr al-Zuhayri al-Kalbi

A poet of the Kalb tribe, who says:

At dawn in al-Qaʿ I struck Shamla with a thrust that left Abu Aws fallen, stretched upon the ground.

I dragged my spear through him and left him there, while the beasts of al-Qaʿ descended upon his body.

The martial poetry of this Imruʾ al-Qays clearly does not fit the poetry of the sick Imruʾ al-Qays, the Man of Sores. Nor does it fit the poetry attributed to the captive prince, the outlaw, or the poet who fled from a raid.

Yet the image of Imruʾ al-Qays al-Kalbi, the warrior, became mixed with the images of the prince, the prisoner, and the sick man. In the accounts of the Arab chroniclers, the result was the familiar image of a warrior-poet who defeats entire tribes.

7. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Harith ibn Zuhayr ibn Jusham

His real name was Adi. He is the famous poet known as al-Muhalhil.

8. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Adi al-Kalbi

Al-Amidi did not know this poet’s genealogy, even though he had consulted the Kalb tribe’s own collection of poetry.

9. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Kilab ibn Rizam al-Uqayli, later al-Huwaylidi

10. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Malik al-Himyari

He is the author of a poem that appears in the Diwan of Imruʾ al-Qays:

O Hind, do not marry a weakling who still seems to wear the locks of his infancy.

This, then, is the list of poets known by the name Imruʾ al-Qays as recorded by the careful critic al-Amidi.

Al-Baghdadi adds two more in Khizanat al-Adab (p. 335), both from the early Islamic period:

a) Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Asbagh al-Kalbi.

b) Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Fakhir ibn al-Tammah.

But the lists of al-Amidi and al-Baghdadi, together with Ahlwardt’s additions, can themselves be expanded.

Drawing on the chronicles, legends, and the Diwan, I can add a number of poets known as Imruʾ al-Qays who do not appear in the lists of al-Amidi, al-Baghdadi, or Ahlwardt.

1. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Zayd Manat ibn Tamim ibn Murr ibn Udad

See Ibn Sallam, Tabaqat, 2:555, and al-Yaqubi, History, 1.

2. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Awf

According to the Orientalist Albert Jamme, on the basis of inscriptions he examined, this man ruled a small state in the land of Adum called Khasmasatan (al-Mufassal, 3:316).

In my view, this was the prince who appealed to the Romans for help in extending his power over Saba and Raydan. His attempt failed when he was captured in battle by the forces of the Yemeni king Ilsharah Yahdib, around 65–50 BCE.

At this time, as Jamme observed, Kinda was a small independent kingdom. One of its kings, a man called Malik, joined Imruʾ al-Qays, ruler of Khasmasatan, in an organized military attack on the forces of Ilsharah Yahdib and his brother Yaʾzil Bayyin.

The attack ended in humiliating defeat. Both men were captured and held as hostages by the Yemeni king.

The battle took place near Marib, perhaps near Shiʿb Marib, as Jawad Ali suggested, since this place is indeed close to Aden.

Eventually they were released on condition that they declare loyalty to Yemen and refrain from attacking the forces of the king of Saba and Dhu Raydan, Ilsharah Yahdib.

This captive prince is certainly not the man referred to in the poems of Ubayd ibn al-Abras.

I believe instead that he is the author of the poems preserved in the Diwan which describe a journey to Rome to meet the emperor and ask for his help. These poems became confused with the work of another Imruʾ al-Qays, a man whom the Romans offered command of a mercenary army for an invasion of Yemen, but who refused the offer. See the Diwan, 33 and 95:

Had he wished, the campaign could have begun in Himyar, but instead he deliberately set out for the Romans.

The captive prince mentioned in the Muʿallaqa of al-Harith ibn Hilliza, however, is someone else:

And we broke the fetters from Imruʾ al-Qays after his imprisonment and suffering had dragged on.

This man was Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ, king of al-Hira. Through his father, he was the brother of Amr ibn Hind.

The Ghassanids captured him when al-Mundhir was killed at the famous Battle of Ayn Abagh around 554 CE.

To make the identity of this prince completely clear: this Imruʾ al-Qays was the son of the king of al-Hira and the brother of the crown prince Amr ibn Hind. He fell into Ghassanid hands in Syria after a battle in which his father, King al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ, was killed and the young prince taken prisoner.

Later, however, Amr ibn Hind launched a swift and limited campaign and succeeded in freeing him. Al-Harith ibn Hilliza praises this event in his Muʿallaqa and mentions the prince’s release.

The chroniclers appear to have confused names and periods on an extraordinary scale. Around six hundred years separate the first captive, in Aden, from the second captive, in Syria.

3. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Tamlik

See the Diwan and al-Anbari, p. 459.

4. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Khidham, or Qutam

See the Diwan, p. 162.

5. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hammana ibn Waʾil

See Ibn al-Kalbi; Ibn Asakir, 8:380; Usd al-Ghaba, 2:237.

6. Dhu al-Quruh, “the Man of Sores”

This title belonged to two poets. One was Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hujr; the other was Kaʿb ibn Khafaja, whose poetry survives in Ibn Aqil’s book, as al-Amidi noted.

7. Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Hammam ibn Malik ibn Hubal ibn Kinana ibn Bakr ibn Awf, of Banu Udhra in Syria

See Ibn Qutayba, Poetry and Poets, p. 92.

In the Diwan bearing the name of Imruʾ al-Qays ibn Hujr, the poet appears as Dhu al-Quruh, the Man of Sores, and several poems attributed to him describe a long-standing illness. See the Diwan, p. 118:

If only the soul could die all at once; instead it sheds one soul after another.

After health, it gave forth a bleeding sore— what blessing could turn so bitterly to misery!

Al-Tammah has reached out from his distant land to clothe me in the sickness that clothed him.

The poem clearly suggests that its author was a man suffering from an old illness.

The same is true of the poem beginning “Whose deserted campsite is this?” (Diwan, p. 121):

Whose deserted campsite is this, its signs worn away through ages long gone and silent?

If you see this blemish upon me, I look like a man crippled by gout.

The sores have put me inside a robe you would think I wore, though I never put it on.

You can see the marks of the sores upon his skin, like the impressions of signet rings.

Or in the poem beginning “Stop at the ancient campsite” (Diwan, p. 117):

If you see me unable to close my eyes for even an hour of the night, unless I bend over and drift into sleep,

my old illness has returned to me before dawn; I fear my sickness will come back and lay me low.

Taking apart the Diwan that bears the name Imruʾ al-Qays, along with the Muʿallaqa itself, allows us to redraw the poet’s image.

That image should not be treated as belonging to one particular poet, or as the exclusive biography of a single man. It belongs instead to a group of poets who, within an old Arab cultural tradition, successively carried the same name or title: Imruʾ al-Qays.

This leads us toward a better understanding of what is usually called fabrication or false attribution in pre-Islamic poetry.

In other words, this method can sharpen our understanding of the problem of poems being forged and placed in the mouths of other poets. It is an old phenomenon, and one that has played a destructive role in shaping our picture of Arab history before Islam.

One of the most important images that needs to be disentangled is that of the poet who supposedly travelled to meet Emperor Justinian II and ask Byzantium for military and political support in recovering his stolen throne.

According to the story, while at the imperial court he fell in love with the emperor’s daughter. Arab chroniclers then tell us that this led to a plot against his life. The emperor gave him a poisoned robe, which he put on during his journey home. His skin began to peel away and decay, he broke out in fatal sores, and he died.

Umar Farrukh goes even further than the old chroniclers in his History of Arabic Literature, vol. 1. He claims to have found an old manuscript stating that when the ruler of Constantinople learned of Imruʾ al-Qays’s death, he ordered a statue to be made in his likeness. According to this account, the statue was still standing in the days of al-Maʾmun, and the Abbasid caliph saw it while travelling through Byzantine lands.

This story reveals the tragic extent to which images and narratives became merged and confused among modern scholars, just as they had among the ancients.

Modern researchers repeated the legend of the poisoned clothes almost without criticism or review, effectively surrendering themselves to the accounts of the Arab chroniclers despite the glaring historical contradictions within the story.

Even al-Isfahani, while recounting the poet’s life in al-Aghani (9:90), found himself drawn in by the legend. He claims that Imruʾ al-Qays went to meet Emperor Justinian and received such a warm welcome that he was even allowed to bathe with him.

Even if we accepted this story purely for the sake of historical and literary argument, it would still clash with the other portraits created by the transmitters.

Imruʾ al-Qays supposedly tells us elsewhere that he suffered from a long-standing illness—gout—and lived as a sick man. This is difficult to reconcile with the image of a man enjoying intimate access to the emperor’s court.

It also conflicts with the portrait of the hunted prince searching for shelter among the northern tribes, and with that of the wandering outlaw poet.

Al-Isfahani’s version in al-Aghani (9:90) says that the emperor treated Imruʾ al-Qays with great honour and even bathed with him. The poet then fell in love with the emperor’s daughter, and the two began secretly visiting one another.

When Banu Asad learned of this, they sent one of their men, named al-Tammah, to Byzantium. He informed the emperor of the affair. Enraged, the emperor sent the poet poisoned clothing, and Imruʾ al-Qays died on his journey home near Ankara.

Reconstructing the historical account of Imruʾ al-Qays requires us to separate the legendary from the historical in every report and every poem attributed to him and later incorporated into his Diwan.

Several points need to be made.

1. The Byzantine Imruʾ al-Qays

Byzantine historians say almost nothing explicit about a visit by the poet or about his requesting military assistance to recover his throne.

The Byzantine historian Procopius does, however, mention in a historical account a man called Imruʾ al-Qays who was somehow connected with a military expedition Byzantium prepared against Yemen through its local Abyssinian ally in 524–525 CE.

This suggests that the Himyarite prince who contacted the Romans and sought their help in extending his influence over Yemen was following a pattern similar to that of another Imruʾ al-Qays some six centuries earlier, around 65–50 BCE. Both presented themselves to Rome as potential allies against Persian influence in Yemen.

It appears that the Romans asked this Himyarite prince to lead a mercenary army against the Persians himself.

That is exactly what the poem wrongly placed by Orientalists in the Diwan of Imruʾ al-Qays appears to say:

Had he wished, the campaign could have begun in Himyar, but instead he deliberately set out for the Romans.

According to Jawad Ali in al-Mufassal (3:265), drawing on Byzantine sources consulted by writers such as Nonnosus, the emperor responded to the prince’s refusal by appointing this Imruʾ al-Qays as his ambassador, effectively making him a representative to the southern Arab tribes.

Caussin de Perceval mistakenly assumed that the Imruʾ al-Qays mentioned by Byzantine historians was the famous Kindi poet himself.

The error made by de Perceval, which later spread among modern Arab writers, was probably the culmination of earlier mistakes made by Nonnosus himself. He failed to distinguish between Kinda the tribe and Kinda the kingdom, and claimed both that this prince was called Kaisos and that he was king of Kinda, while also identifying the name with that of the famous pre-Islamic poet.

2. The visit of Qabus

This journey was then confused with another genuine historical event that took place around thirty years after the Abyssinian invasion of Yemen.

A Himyarite prince of al-Hira named Qabus, one of the sons of Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Mundhir ibn Maʾ al-Samaʾ, visited Byzantium.

Qabus was greatly respected and feared by the people of al-Hira. He inherited the throne after the death of his brother Amr ibn Hind, known as Muḍarriṭ al-Hijara, and became famous for successful campaigns against the Romans in 556–557 CE.

These local wars broke out after the Romans refused to pay the financial support owed to the kings of al-Hira under the treaty of 556.

At this point, Amr ibn Hind decided to send his brother to meet Emperor Justinian II and discuss the disputed payments.

The envoy of the Himyarite king was poorly received. Negotiations collapsed, and fighting followed.

This event appears to have left its mark on the narratives of the Arab chroniclers. They may have transformed the failure of the negotiations into the image of a murderous imperial rage expressed through a poisoned suit of clothing.

One version of the story therefore imagines an inexplicable fury directed against the emperor’s guest, ending in his death, even though other versions describe the same guest as having been warmly received at the imperial court.

This anger is the historical material from which the chroniclers wove the legend of the poisoned clothes. We will see this more clearly when we examine the myth itself.

3. Another Imruʾ al-Qays and another Roman connection

These two journeys were then merged with yet another historical episode.

Greek sources mention an Imruʾ al-Qays as one of the Arabs subject to the Persian kings. They portray him as a ruler from the Lakhmid dynasty in northern Hijaz who raided Arab tribes and extended his authority over them.
