He even succeeded in seizing a Greek island called Lotaba—Tiran, at the entrance to the Gulf of Aqaba—after expelling the Roman tax collectors.

At the beginning of 473 CE, however, this Lakhmid “king” began seeking friendship with the Romans and trying to secure their recognition.

At some point that year, he sent the bishop of the Arabs to negotiate with the emperor over the possibility of appointing Imruʾ al-Qays as governor of southern Jordan and the coast of the Gulf of Aqaba, with the title of phylarch.

The bishop appears to have succeeded completely. This King Imruʾ al-Qays was invited to Byzantium, where the emperor treated him with extraordinary honour.

Ibn Habib remarks in al-Muhabbar that Firuz, father of Qubadh, was the man who appointed Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Mundhir al-Lakhmi as king between 457 and 483 CE.

Shawqi Dayf, in Pre-Islamic Literature (p. 242), doubted that any such king appeared in the records of al-Hira at this time—and in that he was correct.

But Dayf himself then recognized that this Imruʾ al-Qays was based in northern Arabia rather than al-Hira. He had originally been loyal to Persia before turning against the Persians in favour of Rome.

This, I believe, is the king who was said to have bathed with the emperor, an image intended to express the extraordinary honour shown to him.

The chroniclers transformed him into a poet. They attached to him the story of the visit to Byzantium, the love affair with the emperor’s daughter, and the death at Ankara.

The legend of the poisoned clothes belongs to a whole family of Greek myths in which heroes receive poisoned gifts and die from them.

One of these myths concerns Heracles.

The story runs as follows:

After Heracles had completed the twelve deeds required of him, he went to Thebes and entered an archery contest with King Orthios, who had promised to give him his daughter in marriage.27 Heracles won, but the king refused to keep his promise.

After a series of adventures in Sparta, Heracles finally fought the satyr Sinos, a mythical monster who had tried to abduct his wife.28

As the creature lay dying, however, it gave Heracles’s wife a poisonous liquid and told her that if she poured it onto Heracles’s clothes, his love for her would last forever.

Believing that this would prevent him from falling in love with the king’s daughter or marrying her, she did as she was told.

When Heracles put on the poisoned garment, his body began to decay. He suffered terrible pain and died.

This myth found its way into historical accounts of Imruʾ al-Qays: the sick poet who lived in al-Hira, the Man of Sores.

His image was then merged with that of the Imruʾ al-Qays who sought revenge against Banu Asad for killing his father Hujr, leader of Kinda.

At this point, a man from Banu Asad named al-Tammah was inserted into the story and turned into an informer who slipped into the Roman Empire to reveal the secret love affair between the emperor’s daughter and the poet.

What is striking is that the name al-Tammah was itself associated with another Imruʾ al-Qays: Imruʾ al-Qays ibn al-Fakhir ibn al-Tammah, an Islamic-era poet. See al-Baghdadi, Khizanat al-Adab, p. 335.

One poem says:

Al-Tammah has reached out from his distant land to clothe me in the sickness that clothed him.29

In my view, this poem belongs to that Islamic-era poet, who bore the title Imruʾ al-Qays just as earlier poets had, within the same cultural and poetic tradition.

Both in the structure of the poem and in its poetic values, sickness and the torment of sores belong to the idea of the suffering holy man.

There is no reason to assume that the poet called the Man of Sores was literally sick.

On the contrary, we have enough evidence to suggest that presenting oneself as sick, wounded, and tormented became a poetic convention which poets inherited and treated as literary material.

The poem itself allows us to see this more clearly.

The distant al-Tammah is not, as the old transmitters and critics understood him, a corrupt informer. He is the sick man himself, whose suffering left a painful trace in the poetry of others through the inherited poetic tradition.

The poem therefore does not belong to Ibn Hujr. It belongs to the Man of Sores, the poet of al-Hira, whose poems were lost and were later copied, imitated, or attributed to others.

Because the later transmitters did not respect the historical sequence of people and events, they inserted the name al-Tammah twice.

In one version, he became a man of Banu Asad who informed against Imruʾ al-Qays at the emperor’s court.

In another, he became the sick man—the Man of Sores—whose disease was somehow “put onto” the poet.

The two cannot logically be the same figure appearing in two contradictory forms at once.

This poem, then, is the work of later transmitters who constructed it from remnants of lost and forgotten poetry belonging to the poet of al-Hira: Imruʾ al-Qays, the Man of Sores.

The title itself, in my view, echoes the old religious image of the wretched and tormented man, or the righteous sufferer. The same image appears in the ancient Arab poetic epic of the prophet Job, which later found its way into the Torah, and it appears in Babylonian and Sumerian poetry as well.

The Greek myth of the poisoned garment gave the transmitters an ideal framework within which these different images could be fused.

But where, in fact, was Ankara?

The transmitters relied without justification on a line of poetry attributed to this Imruʾ al-Qays and concluded that he died at Ankara after returning from his meeting with the emperor:

Many a daunting affair, many a fierce thrust,

and many a brimming platter came to rest in the land of Anqara.

The Anqara mentioned here was not, in fact, in the land of the Romans, as the Arab chroniclers imagined and as some modern writers later assumed.

It lay within the kingdom of al-Hira itself, the homeland of the sick poet known as the Man of Sores.

Anqara was a place outside Kufa, in the area of al-Hira. Yaqut al-Hamawi records it in Muʿjam al-Buldan (1:323), and it also appears on old maps of the city.

The geography can be supported by an extremely important report transmitted by al-Asmaʿi.

He says:

A man from Banu Darim came before the judge Sawwar ibn Abd Allah to give testimony. He found the judge reciting verses by al-Aswad ibn Yaʿfur:

What can I hope for after the house of Muharriq left its dwellings—and after Iyad?

The people of al-Khawarnaq, al-Sadir, and Bariq, and the turreted palace of Sindad.

They settled at Anqara, where over them flows the water of the Euphrates, descending from the highlands.

The judge then turned to the man from Darim and asked, “Do you know this poem?”

He said, “No.”

“Do you know who composed it?”

“No.”

The judge replied, “A man from your own people, a man of such distinction, says wisdom like this, and yet you neither know his poetry nor even know who wrote it? Muzahim! Record his testimony with you. As for me, I will withhold judgment until I ask about this man, for I suspect he may be unreliable!”

Al-Asmaʿi’s account makes it clear that the judge was reciting poetry describing places in al-Hira, the city where he himself lived.

The witness knew nothing about the poem even though he belonged to the same tribe as its poet and came from the same city.

In Muʿjam ma Istaʿjam (p. 201), Anqara is likewise listed as one of the places of al-Hira—the very region where the Himyarite poet Imruʾ al-Qays, the Man of Sores, lived, died, and was buried.

This is how the images of many poets who bore the name Imruʾ al-Qays were gradually fused into a single figure.

The result was the invention of a poet who never existed in that form, to whom a Diwan was then attributed.

In the end, that Diwan is the collected poetry of more than twenty-two different poets.

Previous9. Arab Pharaohs in Egypt and “Foreign Gods” in CreteNext11. Who Was the First al-Aʿsha, and Who Was the Seventeenth?

11. Who Was the First al-Aʿsha, and Who Was the Seventeenth?

From Homer of Troy, the Blind Poet, to al-Aʿsha of Tarud

Who was the poet known in ancient, pre-Islamic Arabic poetry as Sannajat al-Arab, “the Singer of the Arabs”?

Was he the same man as al-A‘sha of Qays?

Or was he al-A‘sha of Bakr?

Was he al-A‘sha of Bahila?

And was this last poet the same man as al-A‘sha of Tarud?

Or was the pre-Islamic al-A‘sha actually al-A‘sha of Tamim—that is, al-A‘sha ibn Zurara ibn al-Nabbash al-Tamimi? (Ibn Hisham, Sira 3:91.)

If so, then who was the poet known as al-A‘sha of Maymun?

All these different forms in which al-A‘sha’s name and ancestry appear point, in one way or another, to the poet whom modern readers, like the ancients, know as the Singer of the Arabs—in other words, the Arabs’ great singing poet. They all occur in the old Arabic and Islamic sources, and together they raise an unexpected question.

Was there, at some distant point in Arab poetic and social history, a real poet who sang of tribal heroism, epic deeds, and legends, and who became known as al-A‘sha because he genuinely suffered from night blindness? Did the Arabs eventually lose track of his real name and tribal ancestry, leaving later writers to record him under contradictory and conflicting identities?

Put another way: did the ancient Arabs once know a blind—or partly blind—poet who celebrated tribal heroes and achievements in lyrical verse filled with maxims, wisdom, proverbs, and myth? Did he leave such a powerful mark on early Arabic poetry that poets from many different tribes later adopted the same title?

Or did this poet never exist at all?

Perhaps the Arabs never had an actual blind poet who wandered among them singing heroic songs. Perhaps they created his image in imitation of a legend they already knew: the story of a blind poet walking among fallen warriors and singing of their mythical deeds on the battlefield.

Could the tribes have encountered the legend of the blind poet in merchants’ gatherings and public assemblies—for example, in the famous council of Quraysh during the time of Qusayy, the Prophet’s ancestor, around AD 500? Or had they known such stories much earlier, through their contact with other peoples in trade and regional warfare, including the Greeks and Persians?

If so, the tribal imagination may have fashioned an Arab poet who never really existed, modelled on an image that was widespread across the cultures of the ancient world.

These questions are crucial if we want to recover the historical truth that has been lost or confused in the case of al-A‘sha, the Arabs’ celebrated poet and singer. Yet, unfortunately, neither ancient nor modern historians have given the problem anything like the attention it deserves.

The sharp and discerning critic Abu al-Qasim al-Hasan al-Amidi, who died in AH 370 and is well known among scholars in the West—from Leiden to London—though relatively unfamiliar to many contemporary Arab intellectuals, lists around seventeen pre-Islamic and Islamic poets who were known by the name al-A‘sha (al-Mu’talif wa-l-Mukhtalif, p. 13).

Ibn Qutayba sometimes copies al-Amidi almost word for word (Poetry and the Poets, p. 212), as does al-Baghdadi, author of Khizanat al-Adab (1:342). Both rely on his account of the poets called al-A‘sha. Yet neither is prompted to ask the obvious question.

That is genuinely striking. Both men recorded and criticised the history of early Arabic poetry, yet neither approached this particular problem with any real critical suspicion.

Al-Amidi based his list of seventeen poets called al-A‘sha on two sources.

The first was a list compiled by his teacher Niftawayh, containing eight early poets. The second was al-Amidi’s own research in collections of tribal poetry belonging to tribes such as Kalb, Tamim, Hamdan, and Uqayl. From these tribal records he identified another nine poets whose poems and genealogies had been preserved by the tribes that claimed them as their own.

Some had clear tribal lineages. Others, as we shall see, remained of uncertain ancestry.

Here is Niftawayh’s list.

1. Al-Aʿsha ibn Qays ibn Thaʿlaba

His name was Maymun ibn Qays ibn Jandal ibn Sharahil ibn Awf ibn Sa‘d ibn Dubay‘a ibn Qays ibn Tha‘laba ibn Ukaba ibn Sa‘b ibn Ali ibn Bakr ibn Wa’il.

This is the al-A‘sha most famous among the Arabs.

2. Al-Aʿsha of Banu Rabiʿa ibn Dhuhl ibn Shayban

His name was Abd Allah ibn Kharija ibn Amr ibn Ya‘sub ibn Qays ibn Amr ibn Abi Rabi‘a.

Al-Amidi found another form of this poet’s name in the genealogies of Banu Shayban, where he appears as Habib ibn Amr al-Muzdalif. But in the collection of the poetry of Banu Rabi‘a ibn Dhuhl, where the tribe had preserved fragments of his work, the genealogy indicates that he was actually a grandson of this Amr al-Muzdalif rather than his direct son.

3. Al-Aʿsha of Banu Awf ibn Hammam ibn Murra ibn Dhuhl ibn Shayban

His real name was said to be Dabi’, though another genealogy gives it as Yazid.

This was the al-A‘sha whose verses Abd al-Malik ibn Marwan was said to quote:

If you would preserve knowledge, or those who bear it, or keep a witness who can speak of the absent, learn the land by its names, and judge a companion by his companion.

Al-Tayalisi, however, attributes another poem to this al-A‘sha, including the line:

By your father’s life, what befell Dabi’ was bitter indeed, and al-A‘sha would never lack the support of Utayb.

From this line Niftawayh concluded that the poet’s name was Dabi’. Al-Tayalisi and al-Amidi reached the same conclusion.

4. Al-Aʿsha of Bahila

He was known by the kunya Abu Qahfan and was an early pre-Islamic poet. His name was said to be Amir ibn al-Harith.

The Banu Bahila, incidentally, traced their tribal name through their mother, Bahila.

This al-A‘sha was a famous poet, remembered especially for his magnificent elegy for his maternal brother al-Muntashir, which begins:

News has come to me that brings me no joy, from on high—nothing wondrous in it, and no sorcery.

5. Al-Aʿsha of Hamdan

His name was Abd al-Rahman ibn Abd Allah ibn al-Harith ibn Nizam ibn Jusham ibn Amr ibn Malik.

In al-Amidi’s judgement, he was a gifted and distinguished poet. He lived during the time of al-Hajjaj ibn Yusuf al-Thaqafi and was famous for a poem attacking the tribe of Thaqif:

Thaqif has produced two liars: its liar of old and another after him. We rose against the godless tempter when he turned to unbelief after faith, under the noble lord Abd al-Rahman. Lord, give Hamdan power over Thaqif.

Historical reports say that al-Hajjaj eventually captured him and said:

“God has now given Thaqif power over you, you sinner.”

He then ordered his execution.

6. Al-Aʿsha of Banu Sawra

7. Al-Aʿsha of Banu Jilan

His name was Salama ibn al-Harith.

8. Al-Aʿsha of Banu Mazin ibn Amr ibn Tamim

This is the poet who was said to have come before the Prophet and recited his famous poem:

You left me in the middle of a tangled thicket, wooden spikes wearing at my feet; blind from birth, unable to see the knot of a strap, unable to see a companion unless he comes close.

He is also sometimes identified as al-A‘sha of Banu Harmaz.

Al-Amidi, however, believed that Banu Mazin had no al-A‘sha of their own. He also understood the second line of the poem as referring to defective night vision. We shall return to this poem in detail later.

Al-Amidi then supplemented Niftawayh’s list with another nine poets. Some are particularly puzzling because their ancestry is far from clear.

9. Al-Aʿsha of Tarud

Tarud belonged to Fahm ibn Amr ibn Qays Aylan.

Al-Tayalisi says that his name was Iyas ibn Amir ibn Sulaym ibn Amir al-Tarudi and that his kunya was Abu Khattab.

According to al-Tayalisi, Tarud was a branch of Fahm and belonged to one of the clans of Banu Khafaf or Banu Haritha. He was said to have been a devout ascetic who eventually went blind.

Al-Jahiz attributes the following lines to him:

My life be your ransom, O visitor, when the houses put on their coat of ice. You fulfilled the hope for which you came, and became to me both father and child.

Al-Amidi strongly rejected al-Jahiz’s attribution. He said that the poem had wrongly been assigned to this al-A‘sha and that he had found poems genuinely belonging to him in the collection of Banu Sulaym. Among them was the famous early Arabic poem beginning:

O dwelling of Asma, between al-Safh and al-Rahb, the passing ages have emptied you and worn you away.

10. Al-Aʿsha of Nahshal

He was the well-known poet al-Aswad ibn Ya‘fur.

11. Al-Aʿsha of Banu Asad

He was said to be al-A‘sha ibn Bajra—or Bahra—ibn Munqidh al-Asadi.

12. The al-Aʿsha called Talha

He was the brother of al-Kumayt al-Akbar ibn Ma‘ruf.

13. Al-Aʿsha of Ukl

His name was Kuhmus ibn Qa‘nab ibn Wa‘la ibn Atiyya.

He wrote:

Youth has left me now, and my failing sight troubles me; companions too may scatter and part. Youth once wrapped me in its handsome cloak and gave me strength among my peers.

He was a contemporary of Jarir and attacked him viciously in another poem:

I asked the people who among mankind was worst, foulest when things were stripped to their essence, basest in speech and meanest in deed. They said: the family from which Jarir comes.

14. Al-Aʿsha of Banu Uqayl

He was Mu‘adh ibn Kulayb ibn Hazn ibn Mu‘awiya ibn Khafaja.

15. Al-Aʿsha of Banu Malik

A well-known poet of rajaz verse.

16. Al-Aʿsha al-Thaʿlabi

His name was Nu‘man ibn Najwan. He wrote:

I have grown old and dim of sight; the turns of time have betrayed me, as they often did before. Good sense returned to my heart after the folly of youth, though ignorance was my companion for many years.

17. Al-Aʿsha ibn al-Nabbash ibn Zurara al-Tamimi

I found in Ibn Hisham’s Sira that this was the poet who composed an elegy for those killed at Uhud, although al-Amidi does not mention this.

Analysing these lists—where the title al-A‘sha appears among poets from tribe after tribe—and then taking apart the collected poetry attributed to al-A‘sha may allow us to reconstruct the historical tradition surrounding the poet who so captivated the Arabs with his heroic songs that other poets were named after him, or adopted the same title under the force of his influence.

Al-Amidi himself admits that the real names, genealogies, and tribal origins of some of these poets were unknown. Arab genealogists could not reliably place them within any lineage. Nor did anyone know with certainty when some of them had lived or died.

This severely limits our chances of giving definitive answers to the questions before us. However much effort the researcher makes, the surviving historical material available for analysis, comparison, and criticism is painfully thin.

Yet the very existence of so many poets called al-A‘sha—many of them famous within their own tribal traditions—may actually help us solve the puzzle of why no fewer than seventeen al-A‘shas appear in the history of Arabic poetry.

Reconstructing the history of al-A‘sha, and correcting the historical and literary mistakes made first by Orientalists and then repeated by Arab historians and critics of early poetry, requires us to separate the mythical elements from the historical ones.

Doing so may give us a clearer and more satisfying view of the neglected side of this history: the part that has hardly been thought about at all.

What we need is an account that comes closer to historical truth—or, if that truth is no longer fully recoverable, one that at least challenges the prevailing story through the force of evidence and analysis.

If one myth can be a polished variation on an older myth, historical narrative can also grow through the shifting and merging of very different stories and images, though often on a much deeper and more radical scale.

History has an almost unlimited capacity to absorb scattered and highly diverse material and weave it into a durable narrative.

We can see this in the familiar picture presented in books of literature and history of an ancient poet called al-A‘sha—a poet who appears in forms that are extremely difficult to reconcile with one another.

Was the pre-Islamic Singer of the Arabs the same al-A‘sha who lived into the Islamic period?

Or was there a later Muslim poet who adopted the title al-A‘sha, after which older poems were attributed to him?

And was the earliest al-A‘sha truly a historical person at all, or a mythical one?

The large number of poets listed by Niftawayh, al-Amidi, and al-Baghdadi—and by the further list we shall add to theirs—points, in my view, to one important conclusion.

There were in fact two fundamental figures called al-A‘sha.

One was a historical pre-Islamic poet who lived close to the rise of Islam. As we shall show, he never met the Prophet and never praised him in the famous poem later attributed to him. He died a pagan.

The other was a far more ancient, mythical poet: a creation of the tribal imagination, shaped by an image of the blind poet already familiar from the cultures of the ancient world.

Between these two figures—the historical and the mythical al-A‘sha—lies an enormous poetic space. It was filled by poets from many different tribes who appeared before and after Islam, perhaps one after another and perhaps sometimes at the same time, within an old poetic tradition that encouraged each community to possess its own blind poet.

Making this distinction is essential if we are to reconstruct the historical account.

What happened in the old Arabic reports can be described as follows.

The tribal transmitters who collected poetry into books belonging to individual tribes—the very collections that al-Amidi himself consulted around AH 250 while composing al-Mu’talif wa-l-Mukhtalif—confused two different figures: the historical al-A‘sha and the mythical one.

The same kind of confusion, as we have already seen, affected the stories of kings, princes, and other poets.

Poems belonging to different people were combined almost at random, so long as they seemed to fit the stereotype that had taken root in the pastoral imagination: the blind wandering poet who travelled from place to place singing of tragic heroism, mourning disaster, and turning the myths of the Arabs into long popular poems.

Some of the fabrication and false attribution of poetry associated with the age of Hammad al-Rawiya, Khalaf al-Ahmar, and Ibn Sallam—roughly AH 150–222—also seems to have entered the body of poetry attributed to the historical al-A‘sha.

It then became part of his accepted corpus, even though some of it was either deliberately forged or composed by other, unknown poets.

We know at least something reliable about the historical al-A‘shas—that is, about roughly sixteen poets who bore the title.

But we know almost nothing about the first, mythical al-A‘sha, whose title those sixteen later poets inherited.

This is the figure I want to clarify.

First

At some point shortly before Islam, and continuing into the early Islamic period, several attempts were made to identify the mythical al-A‘sha—the ancient blind singer known as the Singer of the Arabs—to establish his ancestry, collect his poetry, and place him within a particular historical period and a clear genealogy.

The result was chaos.

Every transmitter, storyteller, or unreliable genealogist produced a different version, assigning the poet to one tribe or another.

Later transmitters probably played a direct part in worsening this confusion when they wrote down genealogies and collected poems. They did not seem to notice what happened when different images and different bodies of material were merged.

The same poetic images began to appear again and again under supposedly different identities, without proper criticism or separation.

A powerful but overly permissive imagination helped many transmitters create bodies of poetry that matched the image of the poet as singer.

Yet once this poetic fabric is carefully dissected, we find something very different.

The heroic images have drifted far from their original centre. They have become mixed with licentious poetic language and with social and moral values alien to the tribal environment in which this heroic poetry was supposedly created.

Some poems are so openly lewd that they tear apart the image of the corpus as a coherent whole.

As we shall see when we analyse the Diwan, it is difficult to imagine openly libertine poetry and heroic poetry belonging naturally to a single poetic personality. It is equally difficult to identify a poet who appears in one place as an ascetic or blind wanderer with another whose work expresses an entirely different, shameless sensibility.

Second

The most difficult problem surrounding al-A‘sha lies in the poetry attributed to him.

At different times, epic-style poems were composed—or, more precisely, fabricated—and then presented as the work of al-A‘sha.

Yet much of this material is written in weak, narrative verse, poor in imagery and closer in language to Abbasid poetry than to the heroic and chivalric style associated with pre-Islamic verse.

Without serious criticism or reconsideration, Arab transmitters continued to pass these poems down from generation to generation as the work of the historical al-A‘sha.

One example is the poetic account of part of the legendary epic concerning the wars of Hassan ibn Malik Karib, one of the princes of Najran, against what was said to be the tribe of Tasm.

See al-Tabari’s account of the legend of Zarqa al-Yamama (History 1:451), al-Mas‘udi (Meadows of Gold 2:264), and Yaqut al-Hamawi (Mu‘jam al-Udaba’ 5:506).

In these verses we can see, in my view, a stylised imitation of the Trojan epic and the poetry of Homer:

When she looked, with no shame in her gaze, and the shimmering distance raised what seemed the head of a dog, she said: “I see a man, something in his hand, or perhaps he is mending a sandal—what strange work is this?”

They called her a liar for what she said, and at dawn Hassan’s forces came upon them, driving spears and weapons forward. They brought the people of Jaw down from their dwellings and demolished the standing buildings until they lay low.

These poems, with their romantic narrative structure and thin, impoverished poetic language, plainly do not belong to the poetic texture otherwise attributed to al-A‘sha by the transmitters.

Taha Husayn, in On Pre-Islamic Poetry, avoided the difficult problem of al-A‘sha by simply accepting that he had been a historical poet. Shawqi Dayf did the same in History of Arabic Literature: The Pre-Islamic Age, p. 333.

Neither supplied sufficient, still less decisive, evidence.

The real problem, in my view, is not simply that there was more than one al-A‘sha, nor merely that some poetry was fabricated or altered. The Arabs themselves were fully aware of such literary phenomena, and early critics discussed them at length once the problem of Hammad al-Rawiya and Khalaf al-Ahmar had been raised.

Both men partly admitted responsibility for false attribution.

The more interesting question is what this practice originally meant.

Was fabrication at first a literary method used to complete poetic works whose missing portions had been lost?

Was it originally a cultural convention by which fragments surviving in collective memory were repaired, expanded, and rebuilt—before that convention eventually turned into a professional craft of forgery?

Third

We shall begin from the following hypothesis.

The first, mythical al-A‘sha was a purely literary image created in imitation of the tradition surrounding Homer, the legendary blind poet, and of the Greek epic tradition represented by the Iliad and, by extension, the Trojan Wars.

These stories, I believe, entered deeply into the consciousness of ancient Arabs during the heroic period of the Greek-Persian wars in the third century BC.

At that time, some southern Arabian tribes fought in organised units beside the Persians against the Greeks along the Red Sea coast, on the Yemeni island of Socotra, and in central Arabia.

Over time, Greek settler communities in some of these regions were absorbed and Arabised.

Other channels also helped Greek culture spread among the Arabs, including maritime trade and political relations with Rome.

The Arab tribes, I suggest, imagined for themselves a blind poet much as the Greeks had done around 850 BC: a man leaning on his staff, witnessing bloody scenes of heroism, describing terrible tragedies, wandering among the dead and singing his songs, praising fallen heroes brought down by betrayal or ill fortune.

Together with the stories of Alexander of Macedon—Dhu al-Qarnayn—this image reached Arab ears during the age of the Greek-Persian wars.

Alexander himself was eventually transformed into a Yemeni king in Yemeni genealogies and historical reports.

The same happened to major figures from his army. A commander such as Ifriqis, for example, was imagined by Wahb ibn Munabbih in The Crowns of the Kings of Himyar as a king of Yemen.

In the same way, the Greek image of Homer, the blind singing poet, may have settled into Arab storytelling as the image of an Arab al-A‘sha, a sannaja: a wandering singer.

It is striking that the traditional accounts describe al-A‘sha precisely as a man constantly on the road, travelling from Yemen to Najd, then to al-Hira, Persia, Oman, Syria, Homs, and Jerusalem. He is even said to have crossed the sea to visit the Negus of Abyssinia.

See the Diwan, p. 63; al-Aghani 9:115; and Ibn Qutayba, Poetry and the Poets 1:214, where Ibn Qutayba states categorically that al-A‘sha was blind.

Inspired by this compelling heroic image, a succession of poets may have adopted the same title and even claimed blindness.

Eventually we reach the last al-A‘sha, whom the Arabs, remarkably enough, gave the kunya Abu Basir—a name associated with blindness.

This historical al-A‘sha also repeated in his poetry, as did other poets who bore the title, the claim that he was not merely night-blind but fully blind.

What, then, does this recurring claim of blindness actually mean?

If we take it literally, are we really expected to believe that blindness happened to accompany nearly every poet who called himself al-A‘sha?

Why did blindness become associated with these poets in particular, but not with other pre-Islamic poets?

Why Did al-Aʿsha Claim to Be Blind?

At least four of the seventeen poets listed by Niftawayh and al-Amidi left behind striking verses in which they claimed to be blind.

Some even lamented this symbolic blindness as though it were a physical fact.

Should we therefore understand these claims as remnants of an old poetic tradition—one now lost and forgotten—in which the title itself implied blindness?

Such a tradition may have roots deep in the ancient cultures of the East, stretching from the poets of Sumer and Akkad to the Greeks, where the legend of Homer, the blind poet, eventually emerged.

Take al-A‘sha of Banu Harmaz.

He explicitly presents himself as blind in a famous poem preserved in tribal memory and recorded among the verses attributed to him:

You left me in the middle of a tangled thicket, wooden spikes wearing at my feet; blind from birth, unable to see the knot of a strap, unable to see a companion unless he comes close.

Banu Harmaz did not reject this claim on behalf of their poet.

The same claim appears in the poetry preserved by Banu Qays for their own al-A‘sha, in a famous poem whose authenticity was not doubted:

When she saw me being led, she spoke at what she saw; she saw a man changed in form, dim-sighted and blind.

The transmitters repeatedly cited this as evidence that the historical al-A‘sha had genuinely been blind.

Yet another poet called al-A‘sha—al-A‘sha of Ukl ibn Qa‘nab—made essentially the same claim:

Youth has left me now, and my sight troubles me; companions too may scatter and part.

The transmitters understood this line to mean that he had gone blind late in life.

Then another poet, again called al-A‘sha, repeats the same idea. Numerous sources preserve the words of al-A‘sha of Banu Taghlib:

I have grown old and dim of sight; the turns of time have betrayed me, as they did in earlier days.

Here too the poem is taken to mean that the poet became blind in old age.

Even the historical al-A‘sha whom transmitters claimed had visited the Prophet and praised him in a famous poem—al-A‘sha of Banu Maymun—uses the same imagery:

Did your eyes not fail to close through a night of soreness, while the torment of the sleepless, snake-bitten man returned to you?

And elsewhere:

If you ask about me—many a questioner has eagerly asked after al-A‘sha wherever he travelled.

The latter line, without much ambiguity, contains the image of the blind poet being led by others.

So who was the historical blind al-A‘sha who claimed blindness?

Was he from Banu Harmaz?

From Banu Taghlib?

From Banu Qays?

From Banu Maymun?

Or was he Ukl ibn Qa‘nab?

And, in this context, what does the repeated claim of blindness actually signify?

For the moment, let us set aside the historical al-A‘sha—a title borne by seventeen poets—and examine the title itself as the title originally given to a mythical poet, the figure whom transmitters and storytellers called the Singer of the Arabs.

If we want to compare al-A‘sha, the “blind” poet of the Arab tribes, with Homer, the legendary blind Greek poet, we first need to separate what belongs to myth from what belongs to history.

1. Both Homer and al-Aʿsha were blind poets

The legendary Greek poet Homer, conventionally placed around 850 BC, was blind.

The Arabs’ Singer of the Arabs, al-A‘sha, is likewise represented as blind in both reports and poetry.

When we later examine the word a‘sha itself, we shall see that it means a person who cannot see properly at night.

That meaning intensifies the idea of depriving the human being of sight—the sense most closely connected with the poet’s task of observing and describing events.

It is especially important to connect the title al-A‘sha, the visually impaired poet, with the kunya the Arabs imagined for him: Abu Basir.

The two together create, almost theatrically, an ideal state of detachment.

By symbolically losing ordinary sight, the poet gains an extraordinary ability to perceive the tragic course of events and penetrate beneath their surface.

For this reason, I find it impossible to believe that the kunya Abu Basir was simply attached to the poet after he happened to lose his sight late in life.

The striking connection between title and kunya suggests instead that the kunya accompanied him from the moment the title itself was adopted.

If we place this within the continuing poetic traditions of tribal society—the tradition of a blind poet who wandered everywhere, describing heroism and tragedy—then it becomes likely that the Arabs preserved a mythical image of an unknown blind poet.

That image was repeatedly absorbed and recreated within an unbroken poetic tradition in which the poet assumed the role of the blind singer.

2. Both were singing poets

Homer was the singing poet of the Greeks.

Al-A‘sha was the Arabs’ sannaja: literally a performer associated with the sanj, a musical instrument.

This reinforces an ancient conception of the poet’s profession.

The poet was originally a wandering singer.

The Arabs developed rich traditions of lyrical poetry—praise, satire, love poetry, and elegy—which may have shaped pre-Islamic poetry as a whole.

Another legendary poet, al-Muhalhil, who also called himself Imru al-Qays, strengthens this interpretation, because his title likewise points toward a tradition of singing.

The old and modern critics of pre-Islamic poetry are therefore mistaken when they say that al-A‘sha was called Sannajat al-Arab simply because he played the sanj, and that his title came from the instrument itself.

The more convincing explanation is that the title belongs to an older poetic tradition in which the poet understood himself as the singer of the tribe.

The verse that ancient and modern critics cite as the supposed reason for calling al-A‘sha the Singer of the Arabs reads:

And one who answers, as though hearing the sound of the sanj, whenever its refrain returns from time to time.

But this verse does not in fact explain the title.

It merely describes a poet listening to music.

If anything, it can be understood as expressing the natural harmony between the poet’s awareness of music and his own role as a singing poet.

The word sanj, borrowed from Persian sanj, refers to a musical instrument used in keeping rhythm, and it can also refer to the person who plays it—that is, the singer or performer.

The Arabs were said to have used this instrument to regulate the metres of their old poetry after borrowing it from Persian civilisation.

Poetic metre, of course, lies at the heart of musical rhythm, just as rhythm lies at the heart of song, the ancient poet’s profession.

Ibn Manzur, under the entry sanj in Lisan al-Arab, gives a fabricated example attributed to an unknown poet. It is nevertheless valuable because it confirms this meaning:

Tell Sawwar, when you come to him, and Ibn Allana: Ubayd Allah has added three strings to the sanj.

Across all these uses, the evidence points toward al-A‘sha having received the title Singer of the Arabs within this older poetic tradition.

Al-Isfahani also reports in al-Aghani 9:115 that the Persian king Khosrow listened to al-A‘sha sing.

That detail again shows that the Arabs conceived of the poet as a singer.

3. Both poets recorded epics, heroic deeds, and tragedies in verse

Just as Homer’s Iliad reveals the heroic character of his poetry, Arab epics, battles, myths, and heroic legends were likewise attributed to al-A‘sha.

Among them are his supposed poetic accounts of the wars of Jadhima and al-Zabba’, as transmitted by al-Tabari; the epic of Zarqa al-Yamama and Hassan’s wars in Arabia; the elegy for the lost kingdom of al-Hadr; and the destruction of the Marib Dam.

One of these poems includes the lines:

In that there is a lesson for the bereaved, and Marib too was destroyed by the flood.

Al-Marzubani rejected this poem in al-Muwashshah, p. 49, because it strings together a whole series of interwoven legends.

4. Both were wandering poets

Homer was imagined wandering the earth, describing wars and tragedies.

Arab transmitters likewise turned al-A‘sha into a traveller who ranged across the world: from Khosrow, who listened to his singing, to the Negus in Abyssinia, passing by the kings of al-Hira and Syria, the princes of central Arabia, Homs, Oman, Jerusalem, Ukaz, and Najran.

The poetry attributed to him says:

I have travelled the horizons in search of wealth: Oman, then Homs, then Jerusalem. I came to the Negus in his own land, to the land of the Nabataeans and the land of the Persians.

Using the method of separating different layers of poetic and historical material, we shall now try to isolate the historical side of al-A‘sha from the mythical one.

The aim is to show that, at some point under the influence of the heroic age of the Greek-Persian wars beginning around 300 BC, the Arabs created their own first al-A‘sha in imitation of what the Greeks had done before them.

They fashioned the literary image of a blind singing poet who wandered from place to place recording epics and tragedies.

This image seems to have settled deeply into the traditions of early poetry and to have been repeatedly revived in later periods.

Eventually almost every tribe came to possess its own blind poet, each inheriting the symbolic blindness and the defining features of the mythical al-A‘sha: singer, traveller, blind man, recorder of epics, myths, and heroic deeds.

There can be little doubt that the tradition continued into the Islamic period.

Tamim, for example, produced its own al-A‘sha, who mourned those killed at Uhud (Sira 3:91) and lamented the men of Banu Abd al-Dar who had fallen in battle.

The tradition continued even into the Umayyad period, when the last al-A‘sha appeared and satirised Jarir.

In this way, almost every Arab tribe came to possess its own al-A‘sha: its singer, mourning on the battlefield over fallen heroes.

But if the Arabs never knew a poetic tradition of this kind, how else are we to explain the appearance of an al-A‘sha in so many tribes?

The distant memory of the Greek-Persian wars alone may seem too weak to have survived with such force for so long.

Did the tribes perhaps recover and recreate that memory?

And what else are we to make of the presence of an al-A‘sha of Bakr, an al-A‘sha of Hudhayl, and still others among Banu Harmaz, Taghlib, Asad, Mazin, Tamim, and Hamdan?

Finally, did this long succession of poets really exist?

Or were some of them creations of the transmitters?

If the image of the mythical al-A‘sha corresponds closely to that of the mythical Homer, then the historical al-A‘sha can also be examined from a parallel angle: as a figure partly inspired by, or in some sense “Arabised” from, the Greek model.

To do this, we need to dismantle the dominant image of the historical al-A‘sha and then rebuild the historical account more carefully.

A few further observations may help.

1. Who was the historical al-Aʿsha who actually lived into the Islamic period and praised the Prophet in a famous poem?

Was he al-A‘sha of Qays?

Al-A‘sha of Maymun?

Al-A‘sha of Bahila?

Or someone else?

At this point the thin partitions separating the various historical images of al-A‘sha begin to collapse.

Genealogies and poems overlap so extensively that identifying the pre-Islamic poet, his lifetime, and his poetry becomes extremely difficult.

The old Arab transmitters did not preserve a single clear and coherent picture of the poet who, according to tradition, became so fascinated by Islam that he travelled in the hope of meeting the Prophet.

Instead we find conflicting stories, each attributing the famous poem to a different al-A‘sha.

More striking still, the transmitters reused the old description of the mythical al-A‘sha—Singer of the Arabs—when telling the story of the supposed poem of praise and the supposed meeting with the Prophet.

Historically, however, this is impossible if the Singer of the Arabs was another man who lived long before Islam.

If that poet never met the Prophet and never praised him, then the contradictions must come from confusion among the transmitters.

There were two pre-Islamic poets called al-A‘sha who appear to have lived at roughly the same time.

One was known as al-A‘sha of Qays, whose grave was said by some transmitters to be in al-Yamama.

His genealogy is given as Maymun ibn Qays ibn Sharahil ibn Awf … ibn Bakr.

His father was known as Qatil al-Ju‘, “the one killed by hunger.”

But Arab tribal genealogies tell us that Qatil al-Ju‘ was not originally the name of an individual at all. It was a title associated with Rabi‘a, from which two major branches, Bakr and Wa’il, descended.

The other poet is called al-A‘sha of Bahila.

His genealogy is given as Amir ibn al-Harith ibn Rabah, and his kunya was Abu Qahfan.

But if al-A‘sha of Banu Qays was named Maymun, as his genealogy explicitly states, why did transmitters and genealogists imagine another al-A‘sha whom they called al-A‘sha of Maymun?

Is al-A‘sha of Maymun therefore the same man as al-A‘sha of Qays?

A terrible confusion arose among genealogists and transmitters. They imagined that al-A‘sha of Qays and al-A‘sha of Maymun were two different people, whereas their own genealogical evidence suggests that they were one and the same.

The most difficult aspect of the historical al-A‘sha lies in these genealogies, which have been merged, overlapped, and distorted to such an extent that they are genuinely difficult to untangle.

Ibn Hisham, for example, gives the genealogy in Sira 3:91 as:

al-A‘sha of Qays ibn Tha‘laba ibn Ukaba ibn Sa‘b ibn Ali ibn Bakr ibn Wa’il.

If this is correct, then the supposed al-A‘sha ibn Ukaba—or Ukaba ibn Qa‘nab in Niftawayh and al-Amidi—does not exist as a separate poet, because he carries the same genealogy as al-A‘sha of Qays.

And if we accept Ibn Hisham’s identification of the poet as a descendant of Bakr ibn Wa’il, then we must also remove the supposedly separate al-A‘sha of Bakr from the lists, because Ibn al-Athir (al-Kamil 1:290) and the author of al-Iqd al-Farid (6:111) give him the same ancestry.

The contradiction can therefore be summarised quite simply.

We appear to have three poets carrying the same title, al-A‘sha, and belonging to the same genealogical line.

He is simultaneously:

  • al-A‘sha of Maymun,
  • al-A‘sha of Qays,
  • and al-A‘sha of Bakr,

and perhaps, as we shall see, also al-A‘sha of Ukaba and al-A‘sha of Tarud.

So where is the historical truth?

2. What about the other poet called al-Aʿsha of Bahila?

Can we establish that he really existed?

Can we verify his genealogy and poetry?

Or was he another creation of the transmitters?

If he was historical, did he live at the same time as al-A‘sha of Qays?

Let us look more closely.

The Arabic and Islamic literary and historical sources generally present the al-A‘sha whose poetry has reached us—the poet whose biography we have accepted without proper examination—as Maymun ibn Qays ibn Jandal.

The same sources also place him near the time of the legendary poet al-Samaw’al, claiming, for example, that he composed an epic about the killing of al-Samaw’al’s son after al-Harith ibn Ma’ al-Sama’ besieged him around AD 516.

But that creates an obvious contradiction with the separate claim that he lived into Islam and tried to meet the Prophet around AD 610.

There is almost a century between the two.

This poet is directly associated with the name Qays.

Yet among the ancient Arabs, individuals were often identified through the names of tribes rather than simply through their immediate fathers. This reflected older cultural traditions in which the tribe occupied the symbolic position of the father.

Some forms of marriage themselves preserve traces of this pattern.

A poet’s genealogy, like other old genealogies, may therefore indicate the tribe and its branches rather than a simple sequence of biological fathers.

When someone is called “son of Qays” or “one of Banu Qays,” the Qays in question may be the tribe, just as someone might be called a son of Mudar, Khuzaima, or Kinana.

If so, the genealogy of al-A‘sha may preserve the name of his tribe, Qays, rather than the name of his immediate father.

This is supported by the fact that the ancients called him al-A‘sha of Banu Qays.

That raises another question.

Which tribe called Qays was this?

In fact, the Arabs knew no major tribe simply called Qays except the great tribal grouping Qays Aylan.

If we accept that identification—reluctantly, but with some care—then the claimed descent from Bakr and Wa’il becomes impossible.

Qays Aylan was a major branch of Mudar, while Bakr and Wa’il belonged elsewhere in the genealogical structure.

If, on the other hand, we assume that this Qays really was the poet’s biological father rather than his tribe, then the genealogy would extend through Qays to Bakr and Ukaba.

We would once again end up with three supposedly different poets called al-A‘sha sharing the same lineage:

al-A‘sha of Qays, al-A‘sha of Bakr, and al-A‘sha of Ukaba.

Our hypothesis raises another interesting problem.

Arab transmitters and early poetry critics claimed that Arabic poetic history also contained a poet called al-A‘sha of Tarud.

Al-Amidi included him in his list, as we saw earlier.

Yet none of the genealogists could establish his ancestry with confidence.

Al-Amidi writes in al-Mu’talif wa-l-Mukhtalif, p. 13:

“Al-A‘sha of Tarud; Banu Tarud belong to Fahm ibn Amr ibn Qays Aylan.”

This is striking, because if so, Qays Aylan would have produced yet another poet called al-A‘sha.

Al-Amidi then adds that neither the man’s genealogy nor even his real name was known.

Famous poems were nevertheless attributed to him, including:

O dwelling of Asma, between al-Safh and al-Rahb, the passing ages have emptied you and worn you away.

Al-Baghdadi, however, claims in Khizanat al-Adab 1:343 that al-A‘sha of Tarud was the author of another poem:

Leave the ways of people who possess no virtue. You have attained honour; take care not to stain it.

Al-Baghdadi was clearly uncomfortable with the uncertainty surrounding this al-A‘sha, particularly because he was relying on al-Amidi for much of his information.

He therefore seems to have seized on any evidence that might establish the poet as a real historical person.

He records the following report:

Al-Lakhmi related from Abd al-Malik ibn Siraj that al-A‘sha of Tarud was Iyas ibn Musa.

Nothing more was added.

But how did al-Lakhmi, Abd al-Malik, and then al-Baghdadi conclude that al-A‘sha of Tarud was named Iyas ibn Musa?

And who was this Iyas?

Al-Baghdadi returns once again to al-Marzubani—probably to al-Muwashshah, though he does not name the work—and reports the following story.

During the caliphate of Umar ibn al-Khattab, Hawdha ibn al-Harith, known as Ibn Hamla, came to receive his stipend.

Umar called Iyas ibn Musa before him.

This angered Hawdha, who mockingly recited:

This affair has passed into hands that are not its own; so look, Commander of the Faithful, at how we are pushed aside. Are Juthaym and al-Suwayd to be called before us, and Iyas before us—and Tarud as well?

These two lines provide no basis at all for deriving the name of al-A‘sha of Tarud from Iyas, as al-Baghdadi and al-Marzubani seem to have done, or as al-Lakhmi and Abd al-Malik supposedly claimed.

The misunderstanding comes from reading the final line as though Iyas and Tarud were the same person.

But the verse plainly names two people:

“Iyas before us—and Tarud.”

The confusion created by this reading led to the fabrication of a name for al-A‘sha and perhaps to the fabrication of the entire story, including its placement in the age of Umar ibn al-Khattab.

The methods used by the transmitters—and perhaps by early poetry critics as well—often seem almost impossible to restrain.

Their accounts have an extraordinary ability to suggest that some precious historical fact lies hidden inside them.

At the same time, however, they can contain a kind of playful manipulation of wording and structure.

Their apparent solidity comes partly from their cleverness: they attach themselves to chains of transmission involving people whose reports are difficult to challenge.

Even so, the name Tarud remains interesting.

At the very least it confirms that the word was known in the Arab vocabulary by the age of Umar ibn al-Khattab.

It had evidently been used for some time and had a meaning the Arabs understood, since a poet connected with Qays Aylan came to be called:

al-A‘sha of Tarud.

Was Tarud, then, an Arab tribe?

Al-Amidi says that he does not know the ancestry of Aʿsha of Tarud or the tribe to which he belonged. Al-Baghdadi, meanwhile, claims that his name was Iyas ibn Musa, a man who was either a Companion of the Prophet or a member of the generation that followed them. Are these two different men, as the verse of poetry seems to suggest, or one and the same person?

What seems certain is that the word Tarud, which the early genealogists and linguists failed either to explain or to place securely within the vocabulary of Arab tribal names, is closely connected with the Greek name Troy (Troada). The Arabs never knew a tribe, branch, sub-branch, clan, or lineage group called Tarud. In Umar Kahhala’s Dictionary of the Arab Tribes, we found only a small tribal branch called al-Tarad, descended from al-Arif of al-Samada and belonging to a minor clan known as al-Dhafir, which moved through the al-Dibdiba and al-Hijra regions of the Arabian desert.

Ibn Duraid, however, in al-Ishtiqaq (p. 318), traces Tarud to Jurm ibn Zayyan and claims that it belonged to the Qahtani Arabs—that is, to the ancient “pure Arabs” who disappeared, died out, and left the historical stage before Islam. If we accept Ibn Duraid’s account, then al-Amidi’s claim that Tarud descended from Fahm ibn Amr ibn Qays Aylan becomes difficult to accept. Ibn Duraid makes Tarud a Qahtani branch, while al-Amidi makes it Adnani.

This contradiction—between a southern pastoral branch and a settled northern one—is extremely important, provided we use it with care.

According to Ibn Khaldun (2:305; 6:82), al-Qalqashandi (p. 318), and al-Zabidi in Taj al-Arus (1:244), Tarud was a branch of Qays Aylan and therefore belonged to the northern Adnani Arabs. They are said to have wandered through Najd before eventually moving west toward the Maghreb. Al-Dhafir, however, the clan to which Tarud is supposedly connected, is described in the Dictionary of the Arab Tribes (2:696) as a clan that cannot be traced back to any tribe at all.

This leaves us with two possibilities.

Either Tarud belonged to the ancient Arabs who had already disappeared from history, or it belonged to a clan with no known tribal ancestry.

In either case, the poet’s title, Aʿsha of Tarud, should be distinguished from the name of this obscure group. The title by which the group was known—Tarud—may itself preserve some lingering memory of an earlier event or place, just as the poet’s title may do. Tribal names, after all, were often drawn from events, locations, and remembered experiences. Quraysh is one example: its name has been explained as meaning a whale or fish, or alternatively as a gathering together, in memory of the tribe’s regrouping in Mecca during the great famine in the age of Qusayy.

In this respect, Ibn Khaldun was mistaken, as were al-Qalqashandi and al-Zabidi, author of Taj al-Arus, when they assumed that Tarud was a branch of Qays Aylan. Nothing in the northern or southern Arab genealogies confirms such a lineage. Perhaps they confused it with Matrud, a branch of Sulaym and Hawazin belonging to Qays ibn Aylan. The difference between the two names is clear.

We do not know how this error arose. A chance identification based on the similarity of the names remains entirely possible, especially when genealogists were faced with the puzzle of a tribal branch whose identity remained obscure even into the age of Umar ibn al-Khattab.

And now another question arises:

Did a pre-Islamic or early Islamic poet called Aʿsha of Tarud really exist?

What lies behind this name?

When did the poet appear?

During the age of Umar ibn al-Khattab?

If so, where is his poetry?

What was his lineage?

What was his actual name?

What was his father’s name?

How could the Muslims have preserved nothing about him, and remained uncertain even about the meaning of his title, at a time when the age of Umar was undergoing an extensive process of recording in which even the smallest details of Arab and early Islamic history were being written down?

Our hypothesis is that there is a structural resemblance between two images: the Greek Homer and al-Aʿsha, the great singer of the Arabs. Both are presented as blind poets who preserved heroic deeds, epics, and myths in long songs.

Seen in that light, the otherwise puzzling word Tarud falls within a remarkably suggestive field of resemblance: the Trojan Wars.

There is no reason to rule out the possibility that Arab tribes knew stories about Troy, or that those stories, with all their appeal and fascination, found their way into societies that were themselves deeply heroic in outlook. Tribal societies continually drew inspiration from stories of courage and chivalry, whether these came from neighbouring peoples or from nations with whom they had direct contact.

Tarud, therefore, which became attached specifically to the name of the blind Arab poet—or the poet who claimed blindness—and to no other Arab poet, may preserve a residual memory of another blind poet who sang of the heroic wars of Troy while travelling from tribe to tribe.

To test this hypothesis, we can make several observations.

1.

Strictly speaking, the Arabic word aʿsha means someone who cannot see at night but can see during the day.

Ibn Manzur, however, in Lisan al-Arab under the root ʿasha, does not accept this definition. In his view, al-aʿsha is a person who has lost his sight, while al-ʿasha means defective vision both by night and by day.

Quoting Ibn al-Arabi, he tells us that seven poets bore the title al-Aʿsha. One of them was Aʿsha of Tarud, who belonged to Banu Sulaym.

If we combine the linguistic meaning of the name with the fact that five of these poets appear in their poetry to claim that they were blind, blindness begins to look like a poetic device in its own right. It became a pose through which the poet could give his imagery a wider and more universal reach, following a poetic convention already familiar from ancient Greek poetry through the Iliad.

From the perspective of a poet who claims blindness, the whole world comes within his imaginative grasp. That is the source of the broad, universal quality of classical poetic vision. Symbolically blindfolded, the poet reconstructs the world and its events while competing with a sighted world around him.

The innocence suggested by those covered eyes—an innocence difficult to distrust, along with the images supposedly produced under its influence—becomes the poet’s real advantage in this contest. The sighted world may possess the faculty of vision, but the poet’s advantage, which allows him to soar above that world in a state of exhilaration, lies precisely in blindness and in his inability to see other people.

By claiming blindness, the poet frees himself from the familiar world available to ordinary sight. He enters into a new encounter with reality and rediscovers it not with his eyes alone, but with all his senses.

He feels it with his fingers.

He smells it.

He tastes it.

He listens to its seismic echoes.

The self-confident sighted observer, proud of seeing reality through a single sense, cannot do the same.

In this sense, the ancient Sumerian poet, the Greek poet, and later the Arab poet of the pre-Islamic age all followed a poetic tradition in which the poet wore the mask of the blind man.

2.

The title sannaja, which was applied to al-Aʿsha, is closely connected with the Arabic word sanj, itself an Arabized form of the Persian sanj.

According to Ibn Manzur, under the entry sanj in Lisan al-Arab, the word refers to a musical instrument. Al-Jawhari believed that the Arabs knew a kind of sanj made of yellow metal or copper. A person who played this instrument was called a sannaj—in other words, a singer or musician.

Arab chroniclers claim that the Persian king Khosrow listened to al-Aʿsha sing. If we reluctantly accept that report, then the poet was functioning as a singer. This was also the ancient role associated with the Greek Homer.

The poems attributed to the legendary Homer form a series of songs extending across a relatively long period—about three centuries, from the twelfth to the ninth century BC. They were assembled with a considerable degree of arbitrariness because there are no historical sources by which their authenticity can be tested.

There has always been a possibility that the Iliad and the Odyssey were not composed by a single poet at all, but by two different poets, even though Greek literary tradition came to attribute both works to one man who immortalized the Trojan Wars.

Within this context, Ibn Qutayba and al-Baghdadi both report that the historical al-Aʿsha died in al-Yamama and was buried in the courtyard of his house. This was Aʿsha of Qays.

Al-Baghdadi cites Yunus the grammarian, a highly trusted scholar, who was once asked:

“Who are the noblest of men?”

He replied:

I do not point to any one man in particular. I say: Imru al-Qays when he rides, al-Nabigha when he gives, Zuhayr when he desires, and al-Aʿsha when he is carried away by song.

What matters here is the firm association between the title al-Aʿsha and tarab—the exhilaration of music and song.

Of all the images the Arabs preserved of him, it was above all the image of the singer that distinguished al-Aʿsha and remained lodged in their memory.

3.

The Arabic and Islamic narrative sources agree that the historical al-Aʿsha died in al-Yamama and was buried in the courtyard of his home.

In this connection, al-Baghdadi relates the following story in Khizanat al-Adab:

Abu Sulayman al-Nawfali said:

I came to al-Yamama as governor and passed through Manfuha, the home of al-Aʿsha, of which he says:

“By the bank of Manfuha, then al-Hajir.”

I asked, “Is this al-Aʿsha’s village?”

They said, “Yes.”

I asked, “Where is his house?”

They said, “There,” and pointed it out.

I asked, “And where is his grave?”

They said, “In the courtyard of his house.”

So I turned the army toward it and came to his grave. I saw that it was wet.

I asked, “Why do I see it wet?”

They replied, “The young men drink with him. They count his grave as the place of one member of their drinking circle, and when the cup reaches him, they pour it over the grave, because of his saying:

‘I shall return to al-Yamama and take my fill of the two delights: gambling and wine.’”

This is the poet of the famous ode beginning:

Bid Hurayra farewell, for the caravan is departing. Can you bear a farewell, man?

The difficulty with this story is as follows.

a.

If we accept that the historical al-Aʿsha was buried in al-Yamama, and that his grave remained wet until the Muslim governor arrived because young Muslim men in al-Yamama were drinking wine and pouring some of it over the grave, then we must reject the reports claiming that he met the Prophet and praised him in the famous poem beginning:

Did your eyes not close through a night of sleepless pain, visited by the torment that keeps the snake-bitten man awake?

This was not from love of women; rather, before sleep you remembered a beloved who lived under threat.

O you who ask me where I have set my course, my destination lies among the people of Yathrib.

And if you ask after me, many a questioner has lost the trail of al-Aʿsha on the road he took.

It is difficult to imagine any convincing way to reconcile the story of his meeting with the Prophet and praising him—a story full of charm, attraction, and emotional force—with the claim that the poet continued drinking wine and eventually returned to al-Yamama so that he could indulge in his “two delights,” gambling and wine.

We must therefore separate the pre-Islamic al-Aʿsha who died in al-Yamama, drank wine, and gambled from another al-Aʿsha who travelled to Yathrib to meet the Prophet.

That second poet was most probably al-Aʿsha ibn al-Nabbash, of the family of Zurara, one of the leading families of Mecca, a Muslim from Tamim. Ibn Hisham attributes to him an elegy for those killed at Uhud.

In our view, he is the only one among all the poets who carried the title al-Aʿsha whose name can reasonably be attached to the poem quoted above—a poem that Orientalists and early critics mistakenly included in the collected poetry of Aʿsha of Qays.

b.

Once we distinguish between Aʿsha of al-Yamama and Aʿsha ibn al-Nabbash, we must make another distinction: between the owner of the grave and the story that wine was poured over it. As we shall see, these also belong to two different figures.

First, we should remember that the lists of al-Amidi and Niftawayh mention an ascetic Aʿsha, a devout worshipper known for the chastity of his speech. According to these accounts, this was Aʿsha of Tarud.

There can be little doubt, on this reading, that the grave of this ascetic had become a shrine in al-Yamama by the time the Muslim governor Abu Sulayman al-Nawfali arrived there. The presence of young men at the grave points in this direction, as does the governor’s own visit.

The practice of pouring wine over the grave should instead be placed within the context of an older ritual dating from before Islam and perhaps surviving into the Islamic period. Its central act was the pouring of wine or water in order to seek blessing from the damp earth, as a symbolic expression of continuing life and immortality.

It appears that the image of another al-Aʿsha—the wine drinker—became confused with that of the ascetic al-Aʿsha, whose shrine was associated with a ritual pouring of wine. As time passed, the two images merged, and the ascetic who had died in al-Yamama became identified with the drinking poet.

c.

We should therefore ask why the Arabs preserved memories and stories about the grave of the al-Aʿsha who died in al-Yamama, when no comparable accounts survive about the graves of other poets whose burial places and histories are unknown.

Why, if this al-Aʿsha was a drinker, rejected Islam, and lived a life of indulgence, did first the Arabs and then the Muslims take such care over his grave?

Why do we not know the graves of Imru al-Qays, Ubayd ibn al-Abras, Antara ibn Shaddad, or Tarafa ibn al-Abd?

This suggests that the man buried in al-Yamama was in fact a blind and devout holy man whose grave became a shrine, much as shrines to holy men still exist in Arab societies today. He had no connection with the famous poet of al-Yamama.

Even now there are shrines dedicated to pious men and worshippers about whom the general public knows nothing historically. Yet people seek blessing at their graves and give them names and titles.

If we accept al-Amidi’s reference to an ascetic al-Aʿsha known to the Arabs as Aʿsha of Tarud, then this shrine may have been the shrine of the legendary blind poet who fired the imagination of Arab tribes with his heroic songs.

Over time, the force of cultural memory may have transformed him into an imagined historical person with a real grave, when in fact the grave was symbolic: the memorial of a singing poet who had once enchanted the tribes with his songs.

d.

According to Ibn Qutayba in Poetry and the Poets, the historical al-Aʿsha set out to meet the Prophet when the signs of Islam began to appear.

Ibn Qutayba quotes Hisham ibn al-Qasim, described in some Arabic and Islamic historical sources as an expert on this particular al-Aʿsha, concerning the poet’s attempted visit to the Prophet around the time of the Treaty of Hudaybiyya.

The meeting never took place.

The leaders of Quraysh intercepted him on the road and filled his heart with fear of Islam. Even so, he is said to have circulated among the tribes a poem praising the Prophet.

Ibn Qutayba writes:

Al-Aʿsha was an old poet of the pre-Islamic age. He lived into the final years of his life after the coming of Islam and set out for the Prophet at the time of the Treaty of Hudaybiyya.

Abu Sufyan ibn Harb asked him where he was going.

He replied, “I am going to Muhammad.”

Abu Sufyan said, “He forbids wine, fornication, and gambling.”

Al-Aʿsha replied, “As for fornication, it has left me even though I have not left it. As for wine, I have already had my fill of it. And as for gambling, perhaps I may receive something from him in compensation for giving it up.”

This weak report presents al-Aʿsha—the wandering singer and supposed poet of heroic verse—as a man immersed in a dissolute way of life, pursuing values that stand in sharp tension with those of chivalry and heroic song. It also places him on the verge of accepting Islam, prevented only by the intervention of Quraysh.

For several reasons that would take us too far from the present discussion, this appears to be a story invented by later Muslim narrators.

The poet intended in the story of joining Islam was more probably Aʿsha of Tamim, who really did stand at the heart of the great battles and composed elegies for fallen heroes.

The other al-Aʿsha—the drinker, womanizer, and gambler—is said to have been Aʿsha of Banu Mazin, a pre-Islamic poet who died before the rise of Islam.

e.

Let us return to al-Yamama.

The tradition that an al-Aʿsha had a grave there concerns Aʿsha of Banu Qays, not any of the other poets bearing that title. He belonged to Bakr ibn Waʾil, one of the great Bedouin tribal confederations whose territory stretched from the Jazira along the Euphrates, in the region of Syria, as far south as al-Yamama.

In the Diwan (p. 34), this al-Aʿsha proudly celebrates his Bedouin way of life:

We are not like Iyad, who made Tikrit its home, waiting for its grain to ripen for the harvest.

God placed our food within our herds, a provision guaranteed to us that will never fail.

They stand like hills, slaughter-stock beneath our swords; when sent to graze, they cannot simply be driven away.

Our cooking pots are assured their hindquarters, and their udders give us pure, unmixed milk.

This poet mocks Iyad for taking up agriculture and boasts instead of his Bedouin life and the abundance of his tribe’s camels.

It is difficult to imagine such a poet as a decadent pleasure-seeker, an ascetic, or a man wandering abroad in search of money at the court of the Negus in Abyssinia or Khosrow in Persia. The other images attached to him are equally difficult to accept.

What we can imagine, however, is that he may have returned to die in the old homeland of his tribe, al-Yamama, moved by the Bedouin’s intense longing for the landscape of childhood.

These, then, are seventeen poets called al-Aʿsha.

Which one of them was the author of the Diwan and the famous Muʿallaqa?

Previous10. The Fabrication of Imruʾ al-QaysNext12. The Fish, the Pen, and the Qurʾanic Nun

12. The Fish, the Pen, and the Qurʾanic Nun

Nun. By the pen and what they write. By the grace of your Lord, you are not mad. And surely you shall have a reward without end. —Surat al-Qalam, 1–3

So be patient with your Lord’s judgement, and do not be like the companion of the fish when he cried out in anguish. Had grace from his Lord not reached him, he would have been cast onto the barren shore in disgrace. But his Lord chose him and made him one of the righteous. —Surat al-Qalam, 48–50

Introduction

At some point in their ancient history, according to Herodotus, the Greeks adopted a belief found among the Phoenicians and also among the Sumerians of Mesopotamia—a belief explained, in fact, in ancient tablets and artistic images.

Its central idea was this:

The first human being was a fish.

A powerful myth of the fish-man, Oannes, therefore appeared in Greek belief. The name also occurs in another form, O-Annes. This name is closely connected, in our view, with the southern Arabian deity Ammianas, or Amm Anas, whom Ibn al-Kalbi included in his list of the idols and deities worshipped by the Arabs.

According to Herodotus, the Greeks believed that this fish-man came from the Arabian Gulf and brought civilization with him onto dry land.

A new reading of the Qur’anic sura and of the verse “Nun, by the pen”, one that places the verse outside the conventional framework of traditional jurisprudential interpretation, becomes possible if we take Arab myths apart and reconnect them with the central ideas of ancient religious belief.

The most important Sumerian ideas about their own origins and ancestry revolved around the same theme. They too believed themselves descended from a fish-man who came from the Gulf into ancient Iraq. But instead of being called O-Annes, he was known as Enki.

The two names share an important feature: the sound represented by the letter n lies at the heart of both.

Enki was the local god of the city of Ur, which lay on the banks of an inlet branching from the Arabian Gulf. Sumerian documents and records tell us that Ur was the first settlement in ancient Iraq inhabited by human beings south of Babylon. In Sumerian belief it was also counted among the first five cities founded after the Flood.

In our view, this points toward a major movement of people who migrated by sea from somewhere else and made their way inland into Mesopotamia.

The Egyptian word hut meant “fish.” Its Arabic equivalent is hut, meaning a fish or whale, and even today people in the Arabian Gulf use hut to mean fish.

From a mythological perspective, the relationship between the Egyptian word hut and the Egyptian god Thoth—Tahut—appears to go well beyond a simple resemblance in sound. We shall return to this when we examine the myth of Thoth the Thrice-Great, whom the Greeks adopted and called Hermes.

Thoth and Hermes alike were gods of wisdom, the former in ancient Egypt and the latter among the Greeks.

The Greeks called the Egyptian Thoth Hermes after the place where his worship flourished in Egypt, on a rocky height they named Hermopolis. We believe that this name derives from the root h-r-m, corresponding in early Arabic to Iram, meaning rocky stones.

According to ancient Egyptian belief, Thoth had originally been a moon god who sprang from the head of the desert god Seth.

Among the ancient Arabs Seth was Shith, a son of Adam. He was a desert deity whom the Hyksos brought into Egypt during the famous invasion associated with the age of the Shepherd Kings, around 1720 BC, and he was a symbol of darkness.

Egyptian mythology says that Thoth emerged from Seth’s head: symbolically, the moon emerging from the head of darkness.

This is a traditional pastoral image belonging to desert peoples.

The Egyptians therefore gave Thoth the title Lord of Time, because through this symbolic rising of the god, human beings learned to calculate time by observing the lunar cycle.

In Egyptian images, Thoth appears as a man with a bird above his head and a pen in his hand. Egyptian documents also call him the Lord of Light.

Before the triumph of Islam in Mecca, and immediately after it, echoes could still be heard of this ancient belief in a primordial god of wisdom who brought civilization through the heart of an immense danger: the crossing of the sea or desert, the passage from the inorganic world into the organic, from darkness into light, and from migration into settled life.

This deep emotional echo appeared with the revelation in Mecca of the verse:

“Nun, by the pen and what they write.”

Muslim jurists became engaged in a vigorous debate over the interpretation of the verse.

When we return to those rich and intelligent discussions, we find that the participants repeatedly brought the entire question back to one central issue:

What does the word Nun mean?

Does it refer to a fish or whale?

From the history of ancient Egyptian religions and beliefs, we know that the god Thoth—whose name and form, in our view, correspond to the Arabic word hut, “fish”—was one of the greatest Egyptian gods and a supreme object of worship.

He was called the Thrice-Great God, because he was the god of wisdom, writing, and astronomy.

We should notice here the remarkably precise correspondence between the image of the Egyptian pyramid, associated with the worship of Thoth, and the description of the god himself as “thrice-great.”

If, as Herodotus tells us, the Greeks borrowed their major gods from Egypt through the Phoenicians, then the Arabs, who in our view had originally brought these gods into Egypt during the Hyksos period, recovered them during the great Abbasid translation movement that brought the treasures of Greek knowledge into Arabic under al-Maʾmun (170–218 AH / 786–833 AD).

In the course of this recovery, however, they transformed Hermes Trismegistus, the Greek Thrice-Great God, into Enoch.

His name then appeared in Arabic historical narratives and Islamic works of jurisprudence as Akhnukh or Khanukh.

At the same time, Muslim writers identified this Enoch with the prophet Idris.

Three images were thus merged into one.

Yet beneath this apparently innocent fusion lies, in fact, an unconscious recovery of the much older image of the god Thoth.

Anyone who reads the historical chronicles and books of Islamic jurisprudence is likely to wonder why Muslim authors identify the prophet Idris in this particular way:

Idris is Enoch, and Enoch is Hermes.

When jurists explain the name Idris, they generally say that he received it because he devoted himself intensely to study—dars—and to the pursuit of knowledge.

Once again, the name becomes associated with the old idea of a god of writing, wisdom, and learning.

These qualities unite all the other forms of the figure:

Enoch, Idris, Hermes, Thoth, Nun, and, as we shall see, Noah, Jonah, and Yunus.

The qualities are distributed across three major conceptions of the god, who therefore appears as a deity of threefold power and greatness.

His greatness lies in knowledge, writing, and wisdom, which together form his distinguishing privilege.

From this same material arose the Christian conception of the divine Trinity. The idea would later enter Islamic belief through Shiʿi doctrine, where the threefold pattern reached one of its clearest popular expressions through the elevation of Imam Ali ibn Abi Talib into an organic part of the doctrine of divine unity.

This, in condensed form, is the wider framework within which we should understand the biblical Enoch—that is, Idris in Islamic jurisprudential tradition, who is also Hermes.

It is also the path along which the figure evolved.

He appears first in Sumer in the mythological form of the water god Enki.

In parallel, Egypt has the figure of Thoth.

The Greeks have Hermes.

The Arabs have Nun.

Finally, Islam presents the figure in the form of a prophet named Idris.

If we concentrate on the relationship between the Sumerian root En and Nun, both of which in our view carry the meaning of fish or whale, we remain very close to the earliest ideas surrounding the ancient god of water as they developed in Sumerian and Egyptian consciousness.

The first part of the Sumerian-Babylonian name En-ki, En, is associated with water. It points toward the development of the root En into the Arabic root N, which the Qur’anic text endowed with awe and sacred significance by linking it directly with learning, writing, and wisdom in the verse:

“Nun, by the pen.”

The original name, Enki, referred to a god regarded by the successive ancient cultures of Mesopotamia as a being of enormous power.

He was the first human being imagined in the shape of a fish—the first fish—who emerged from the water after overcoming its terrors and dangers, carrying with him onto dry land his great gift:

writing and knowledge.

A clay tablet from the dawn of Sumerian history describes a creature that is half fish and half man. He is called O-Annes. He emerges onto dry land after surviving the terrors of the sea, bringing civilization with him.

What is striking about this Sumerian name—which the Greeks also rendered as O-Annes—is the resemblance between a being who is human in the form of a fish and the structure of the Arabic word anas, the root from which insan, “human being,” is formed.

In our view, the name of the prophet Yunus in Arabic religious and historical tradition ultimately derives from this same linguistic material.

From it also comes the biblical name Yunan, or in Hebrew Yonah.

Both emerge miraculously from the water. Both survive danger and cross the sea before finally reaching dry land with a gift: the religious message.

Within this wider framework, it is important to notice that the Thamudites knew and worshipped a god called Nun. They even wrote his name in the same form in which it appears in the Qur’an: Nun.

F. Brandi, in History of Thamud (p. 57), observed that the food of this tribe was connected with the worship of the god Nun.

This, in his view, reveals something about their earlier homeland near the Red Sea coast of Arabia. Rock carvings have been found showing Thamudite boats that tribesmen used to cross the sea for trade. This supports the archaeological conclusion that at one stage the Thamudites lived on resources drawn from the Red Sea and obtained their principal food from it.
